# Ghost invariants

The package map and the load-bearing design invariants: what each package owns, and the rules a change must not break. Moved verbatim from `CLAUDE.md` so that file stays short; the full design narrative is `docs/architecture.md`. The PR reviewer workflow reads this file from the base ref as part of its conventions.

## Stack
- Go 1.26+ CLI application
- SQLite with FTS5 for memory persistence (modernc.org/sqlite — pure Go, no CGO)
- CLI harness callers (claude/opencode/codex/goose subprocesses) — used by reflection, resolve, and supersede. Each harness owns its authentication and billing; Ghost does not add a direct API client. The prompt is written to the child's stdin and no positional message is passed: the kernel caps one argv element at 32 pages (128 KiB on a 4 KiB-page x86, 512 KiB on a 16 KiB-page one) and a reflect prompt reaches megabytes, so an argv prompt failed the spawn with E2BIG instead of answering. `claude -p` and `opencode run` read the message from a pipe when none is given; codex gets `exec -` and goose `run -i -`. `claude --system-prompt` stays an argument — small, fixed, and never confusable with user content. `cli.model_reflect` / `model_resolve` / `model_supersede` pin the opencode harness model per phase (each phase is its own process; ignored by claude/codex/goose which have no model flag). Empty = any inherited `GHOST_OPENCODE_MODEL`, else Ghost's explicit `opencode/big-pickle` default; a configured pin overrides an inherited env pin for that phase.
- MCP server via modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk (stdio transport)

## Package map
- `cmd/ghost/main.go` — CLI entrypoint; subcommands: mcp, hook, reflect, resolve, supersede, prune, lifecycle, project, obsidian, opencode, bench, backup, export, import, history, maintenance, upgrade, context, version, help. `runCLI` answers `-h`/`--help` (`cmd/ghost/help.go`, one usage table) before the dispatch, so a help request has no side effect. A command line the dispatch cannot route — an unknown command, a missing or unknown subcommand, no command at all — is `usageError` on stderr (the diagnostic, then that level's own usage) and exit `2`, the conventional usage-error code, kept distinct from the `1` every command uses for a run that started and failed (#691; bare `ghost mcp` still starts the server, the one group with a default action). Only the groups in `commandGroups` dispatch on a subcommand word: a word after a command that takes an OPERAND (`ghost history <memory-id>`, `ghost reflect <project>`) is data and its parser's business, and an unknown FLAG is that parser's too — exit `1` where it rejects one (`ghost reflect` still ignores it, as it always has). A SCOPE FLAG'S EMPTY VALUE IS A REFUSAL, NEVER ITS DEFAULT, for every parser that takes a project. Every one of those parsers counts OCCURRENCES of a project with a seen bool, asks the duplicate before the empty, and refuses an empty value — but the set of SPELLINGS a given parser accepts differs, and the seen bool covers exactly the spellings that parser takes, no more: `parseResolveArgs`, `parseReflectArgs` and `parseSupersedeArgs` take all three (`--project X`, `--project=X`, and a bare positional), `parseExportArgs`, `parseHistoryCompactArgs` and `parseObsidianFlags` take the two flag spellings and no positional, `parseLifecycleArgs` takes `--project X` and the positional but has no `--project=` form, and `parseContextAuditArgs` (`ghost context --audit`) and `parsePruneArgs` (after #646) take the flag spellings. `runProjectDelete`'s project is positional alone and it counts it the same way, refusing an empty name. In short: an empty value is a refusal in every spelling the parser accepts, and the count is what makes the refusal order-independent (`cmd/ghost/scope_flag_parsers_test.go` asks each of these parsers the questions its own spellings admit). The rule has no exception, and it is one rule for parsers whose empty value answers DIFFERENT things, which is why none of them may take it as its default: for `prune`, `export` and `obsidian` an empty scope is answered with EVERY project in the store, and for `resolve`/`reflect` an empty one decides nothing and prints the usage block instead, so what the refusal stops is not one wrong corpus but a scope the command line never named (`cmd/ghost/obsidian.go`'s --out and --interval stay last-one-wins, because a destination and a cadence are one value each however many times an operator types them). Every stored value a report prints is rendered, not echoed: an id through `assemble.Token`, a name or path through `assemble.Label`, and stored free text inside `«…»` data delimiters, so a newline inside a stored id or memory cannot start a line the reader takes for Ghost's own output — `cmd/ghost/print_sites_forge_test.go` holds the adversarial test for every print site, and `assemble.Data` is the one delimiter implementation the CLI reaches rather than a seventh copy
- `internal/adversarial/` — Test-only support: the shared hostile-input corpus (prompt injection, secret shapes, invisible-character tricks, path shapes) and the shared inert-content invariant the three untrusted parse surfaces assert against (#585). Nothing under `cmd/` may import it; it exists to be called from `_test.go` files. The invariant in one sentence: a value parsed from an untrusted surface is data — stored exactly as parsed, naming at most one path component inside the directory its surface owns, and never deciding the parse. `internal/mcpserver/adversarial_test.go` (#538) proves the same invariant one layer out, over the wire
- `internal/ai/` — CLI-harness providers (`CLIClient`/`OpenCodeClient`/`SourceProvider`) used by reflection + resolve + supersede — no Anthropic HTTP API client. Every child is spawned through the one `harnessCommand` funnel, which applies the `harnessEnv` allowlist and confines the working directory and temp variables to an invocation-owned scratch dir. Confinement is not conditional on that scratch dir: when it cannot be created the funnel confines the child in its own invocation-owned `MkdirTemp` tree instead, under the same rewrite, and when even that is impossible it returns an error and the call fails — no backend can run a child unconfined (#751). The backend is part of the policy, so each harness keeps only its own auth/config root (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `CODEX_HOME`, the safe `GOOSE_*` set, `OPENCODE_API_KEY`). Two harnesses additionally get an isolated config tree because their plugin/MCP discovery would otherwise find Ghost's own registration: opencode gets a fresh home plus an `mcp: {}`/`plugin: []` config, and goose gets an empty invocation-owned `HOME` (its plugin root is home-relative by specification, so `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` alone cannot move it). The goose home carries the config rather than restating where it lives — the inherited `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` when set, otherwise reproducing each home-relative root goose may resolve (`.config/goose`, `Library/Application Support/goose`; Windows uses `%APPDATA%`, which the allowlist already passes through) — so goose's own per-platform resolution is untouched and authentication still works. `GOOSE_PATH_ROOT` is the exception: goose consults it before any home variable (plugins resolve to `$GOOSE_PATH_ROOT/.agents/plugins`), so it is repointed at a root inside the isolated tree with the real `config/` carried into it, and a relative value is left alone because goose ignores one. Where a symlink is refused (Windows without Developer Mode) the files are copied instead. If the home cannot be established, the call fails rather than running with the real home. Codex needs no tree — `--ignore-user-config` already covers it. OpenCode's tree also owns a DATA root (`XDG_DATA_HOME`), which is what keeps a lifecycle run out of the user's session list (#588): `opencode run` has no flag to skip saving a session, so the store's location is the only control, and OpenCode resolves its session database from that variable (verified on v2.0.15 with `opencode debug paths`). V2 needs `--standalone` for this to hold — without it `run` attaches to the user's shared background service, and the server holding the user's data dir is what creates the session. No `session delete` follows a run: the store sits in the invocation-owned directory whose deferred cleanup removes it, so a session cannot outlive its call, and a delete would cost a second OpenCode process per harness call (hundreds per lifecycle) to erase a row from a directory about to be deleted. The pre-isolation backlog is `ghost opencode cleanup-sessions`. A child gets a minimal environment AND NO TOOL SURFACE — see “No harness child gets a tool” below for the per-harness mechanism, the argv goldens, the codex probe, the residuals and the live tests.
- `internal/memory/` — SQLite CRUD, FTS5 search, vector search, time-decay scoring, and the retriever contract behind the assembler (`Store.Candidates`: one read snapshot, widened untrimmed rows with their scoring facts, leg/edge statuses). It is also the seam's second shape: a request with no query and a non-empty `Passive` list is a PASSIVE retrieval, one `[]SlicePolicy` per bucket, one policy per bucket BECAUSE the buckets genuinely disagree (order, over-fetch, two-pass selection, near-duplicate policy: the `_global` policy DROPS its losers, the project policy only reorders and leaves the drop to the cap), where the policy states the ORDER (`decay`, or `pinned_importance_updated` for `_global`), the over-fetch, the two-pass selection, the near-duplicate threshold and whether its losers are dropped — and where `SlicePolicy.Bucket` IS the project predicate, because `passiveFetchSQL` binds it as the `WHERE` clause and does not consult `CandidateRequest.Mode` at all — EXCEPT that `SlicePolicy.IncludeGlobal` widens that one predicate to the union `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'`, which is what a whole-project listing reads and what TWO buckets cannot express (capped at the caller's limit each, they admit twice the rows the caller asked for). The union is **PARENTHESISED** and that is load-bearing rather than decoration: `AND` binds tighter than `OR`, so an unbracketed form reads as `project_id = ? OR (… AND resolved_at IS NULL)` — every row of the requesting project escapes the resolved filter and the scope clause applies to the `_global` half alone (`TestCandidatesPassiveAMixedBucketFiltersResolvedAndScopeForBothProjects`). The flag defaults to FALSE, so the two session-start buckets stay disjoint and its golden does not move. Because a bucket name and a row's own project then stop being the same thing, `Candidate.FetchedBy` reports the policy that ADMITTED each row and the assembler's stage 8 keys its per-slice cap on that — a `_global` row inside a project bucket names no slice by its own project, so a cap keyed on the row leaves it unbounded and "at most N rows" becomes "at most N project rows, plus however many globals ranked nearby". A request that both mixes `_global` into one bucket and fetches it in another is refused at BOTH seams: the bucket names differ, so the repeated-bucket refusal cannot see it, and the rows would be admitted twice under two different caps (`TestCandidatesPassiveRejectsAPolicyThatBothMixesAndFetchesGlobal`, and the assembler's half, which resolves the flag across the WHOLE budget because a check that sets it as it walks refuses `[mixing, _global]` and serves `[_global, mixing]`). A caller that wants both runs two REQUESTS, which is what the project-context surface does. The decay order binds the caller's instant (`decayRankingSQLAt` derives the expression AND its bindings from one count of the constant's `julianday('now')` occurrences, round-tripped in a test) rather than `julianday('now')`, so the window, the Go-side decay score and the row ages the trace reports cannot straddle a second boundary. Passive rows carry the **-1 leg-rank sentinels**, not 0: rank 0 is a real first place, and a passive row reading 0 would clear the keyword floor arm on a rank nobody produced. Every bucket window is bounded, because this path runs at every session start and a passive window is read, ordered and demoted IN FULL — there is no leg to fuse and nothing to discard behind it — so a policy stating no `OverFetch` is refused (its window would be the whole store) and one above `maxPassiveOverFetch` (200) is refused rather than CLAMPED: a caller that states 5000 and is silently given 200 has been answered a different question. A policy naming no bucket, and two policies naming one bucket, are both refused — the first reads as an empty bucket and the second returns every row twice. A passive request cannot also be a HISTORICAL read: `AsOf` and `Passive` are mutually exclusive, checked in `Candidates` before the passive branch because the bench harness and the tests call it without going through the assembler. Routing it to the historical path would silently discard the policies and answer with `Fetch.Limit` while the caller believed its per-bucket over-fetches were in force; the two select over different things (live rows vs recorded versions) and there is no combination of the two here yet. A passive read is the first whole-`Memory` read this package makes through a NON-MIGRATING handle — every store Ghost opens itself migrates on the way in, and the session-start block it serves reads through `OpenReadDB`, which runs no DDL — so the fetch SELECTs `memoryColumnNames` with a column the store may lack replaced by a literal **of the shape `scanMemories` expects**: `retention` becomes `'' AS retention` and NOT NULL, because the scanner binds that one into a plain `string` and resolves `""` to `project` itself ("a row whose tier reads empty is a row a query did not select, not a fourth tier"), so a NULL there is a `Scan` error — "converting NULL to string is unsupported" — rather than a label; `scope` and `expires_at` take NULL precisely because they ARE bound as `sql.NullString` and NULL is the honest value for "no scope stated" and "no expiry claimed". The version is probed through the SNAPSHOT (`s.queryDB()`), never the pool: the read transaction already holds the only connection at `MaxOpenConns(1)`, so a PRAGMA on the pool is a deadlock rather than an error. An UNREADABLE version is treated as the FLOOR, not as "current" — a store whose version cannot be read is read without the columns that might not be there, which loses a label rather than failing the read, where returning an error would turn a transient PRAGMA failure into no session context at all. That leaves the evidence table (`memory_provenance`, v18) with a THREE-WAY decision rather than a boolean (`evidenceReadMode`), because substituting for a column that might exist is the safe direction while SKIPPING a read that might have succeeded is not: **read** (version read and at/above the floor), **report zero** (version read and below it, so the table was never created), and **read, tolerating ONLY a missing table** (version unreadable, so existence is unknown — skipping would report "no recorded evidence" as a fact about support, and a zero on a store that HAS the table would be a false claim that no memory is supported; anything but a missing table stays a real error). An unstated `DemotionThreshold` falls back to the store's configured one rather than to 0.0, which `nearDuplicatePenaltyRows` binds as `l.strength >= ?` — so 0.0 makes EVERY `related` edge a near-duplicate and a `DropDemotedLosers` bucket then DELETES a memory over a 0.1-similarity edge. `DemoteOnlyWhenOverCap` skips the near-duplicate demotion when the selected set fits entirely under the bucket's cap, because a demotion is a REORDER and on such a set it can only shuffle rows the answer shows in full — which is what both shipped readers do: the session block's project bucket states it, and the tool surface has no cap to be over. Near-duplicate protection is `Pinned || RetentionExempt(m)`, the same map every other caller passes: a pin-only map would penalise an unpinned `persistent` row on the losing end of a pair here and spare it everywhere else, and on a bucket that DROPS losers penalised means REMOVED from the block. `Store.CreateWithID` is the only writer that takes a primary key THE CALLER COMPUTED for a row Ghost is creating fresh: Create's write — same transaction, same history and evidence rows, same checks — plus a named key, and an empty one is refused rather than falling back to the column default. The other two writers that write a caller's id preserve an id Ghost already held rather than being handed a new one, and that is the whole difference: `ImportMemory` writes the id an artifact carries (so an unchanged store re-exports byte-identically, and id-only dedup means the destination's own copy is never overwritten), and `RestoreSnapshot` brings back rows under the id they had, so everything still pointing at them resolves again. `CreateWithID` is the inverse — nothing Ghost holds names the row otherwise, which is exactly what a reproducible measurement needs, and the only caller is the benchmark: a store's ids are part of what a measurement of that store means (the ranking breaks tied fused scores by id, and re-sorts the window by a decay factor built from `created_at`), so `internal/bench` seeds a corpus under ids derived from its keys and one `created_at` per pass rather than from `hex(randomblob(16))` and `datetime('now')` (#708). The benchmark calls `CreateWithIDFromCorpus` rather than `CreateWithID`, which is the same write with the record bit OFF, and the distinction is the corpus rule and not tidiness: a dataset's `verified_at` is the DATASET's claim, so the column keeps it verbatim and no `verified` record is appended — a record's stamp is the store's clock by design, so one for a claim the dataset dated to a year earlier would assert a check that happened now. `CreateWithIDFromCorpus` keeps the credential guard, which `CreateFromCorpus` waives for downloaded public eval corpora; the benchmark's own fixtures are committed and a review has read them, so it gets the rule without giving up the guard. Nothing here is on `provider.MemoryStore` or in the MCP surface, and `Create` still mints every id a live save gets — production rows are unaffected. `migrateV20` REFUSES a database that already holds a `retrieval_record` that is not Ghost's — the same treatment `migrateV18` gives the evidence table, and the guard is the half of that step a copied DDL does not bring with it. `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` is a SILENT no-op against a same-named table, so without the refusal the step stamps v20 over a shape nothing here can write and the store opens and then fails every search's record with `no such column: verdicts` — a store that appears to work and is not, reached through a migration that reported success. It recognises the table by an IDENTITY subset (`project_id`, `source`, `verdicts`) rather than by its whole column set, per `evidenceTableIdentity`: the remedy it prints is `DROP TABLE retrieval_record`, so a check that demanded every column would issue a destructive instruction to a store whose table is merely a version ahead, and this one runs on EVERY open rather than only mid-migration. It runs at TWO sites, and the second is the interesting one: `migrateV20` calls it before its own create (the create's index statement is what fails first, with an error naming neither the table nor a remedy), and `OpenDB` calls it BEFORE `initSQL` and therefore before `backupBeforeMigrate`. `retrieval_record` (v20, #646) is one row per RETRIEVAL CALL — the grain is the call and not the (call, memory) pair, because the audit's denominator is calls and a table holding only the calls that admitted something has already dropped the ones worth auditing (the empty call above all). It holds ids, verdicts, a digest and the reason vocabulary, and NOTHING ELSE: there is no query column and no content column, and `RowVerdict` has no field either could be written into. `query_hash` is structural about it — `CHECK (query_hash = '' OR (length = 64 AND NOT GLOB '*[^0-9a-f]*'))`, so the column physically cannot hold a question however a future writer builds its statement. Growth is a `retrievalRecordRowsCap` (5000) window on rowid, oldest first, and the eviction DELETE rides in the SAME transaction as the append off the INSERT's own `RETURNING rowid` (nothing is probed in the common case) so the table cannot exceed its bound even transiently. The write is refused on a store a newer Ghost owns (#746) like every other write here, because a stale server's records are exactly the ones that lie — the store may since have gained a stage or a retention tier that build's verdict vocabulary cannot name. The parsed arm goes through `readableVerdicts`, because TWO DIFFERENT functions raise on a column they cannot read — `json_each` on a document that is not JSON, and the `->>` path accessor on the value `json_each` yields from one that is a JSON OBJECT rather than an array (measured on the SQLite 3.53.4 this build links: `json_each('{"id":"X"}')` walks the object happily; the `->>` on its value is what raises). But a substituted `'[]'` is an EMPTY array, so a row whose verdicts column is malformed matches nothing — and if that row names the memory, a parsed-only purge reports success over a name it left in the store. The second arm is **SCOPED to exactly those unreadable rows** (`COALESCE(verdictsUnreadable(...), 0)`), because an unscoped `OR` would also fuzzy-match every well-formed row: purging the id `A_1` would delete a record whose verdict names `A_1%done`, which the parsed arm — which knows the exact ids — correctly left alone. `verdictsUnreadable` is the single definition the substitution and the scope are both built from, and it is written to be TOTAL rather than to rely on `AND`/`OR` short-circuiting: `json_valid` never raises but `json_type` does, and an expression that read correctly inside a `CASE` raised as soon as it appeared in a `WHERE`, so the inner `json_type` is handed the column only once `json_valid` has accepted it. `verdicts` deliberately carries no `json_valid` CHECK: the readers must tolerate a document they cannot read, and a constraint refusing to store one would make that tolerance unreachable from Ghost's own writers. A reader returns such a row with NO verdicts rather than failing — a reader that errors on one bad row cannot report on the store at all, which is the state an operator is in precisely when they need the report. The `project_id` index is honest about being unread: no build queries it yet (`RetrievalRecords` takes a limit and no project, and the purge matches a memory id), and it is kept for the per-project read #646 asks for — one b-tree insert per search inside a write that already costs an INSERT, measured ~45us — because re-adding an index is itself a migration. `retrieval_record` (v20, #646) holds no query TEXT and no content: `query_hash` is an **HMAC-SHA256 under a per-install key**, because a bare digest of a short question is a fingerprint — the store's own memories are a dictionary of what a user would ask, and a hash confirms a guess as efficiently as it hides the original. The key is a sibling FILE (`retrieval-record.key`, 0600) in the data directory and **NOT in the database**, because `ghost backup` is a `VACUUM INTO` of `ghost.db` and a backup is the most ordinary thing to hand a colleague; `TestTheQueryKeyLivesOutsideTheDatabaseAndIsNotInABackup` asserts the key's bytes are absent from the database file. The key is **published atomically** (staged to a temp file, fsynced, then hard-linked into place, with an `O_EXCL` fallback where a filesystem has no links) so the real path is never a 0-byte file: creating with `O_EXCL` and writing afterwards leaves exactly that window, and a second process reading it would be refused over a file that is about to become valid. A key is resolved **at startup** (`Store.WarmQueryKey`, called from `mcpserver.New`) so the search path never pays a directory resolution, a read and a create — and a **failure is cached with a 30s backoff** rather than not cached at all — neither extreme is right: caching it forever makes one unlucky moment permanent, while never caching it re-runs the whole resolution (directory lookup, read, mkdir, rand, a temp file, an fsync, a link) on EVERY search while holding the cache mutex, for a store that simply cannot keep a key. The backoff makes that per-interval instead of per-search, and `WarmQueryKey` **bypasses** it so startup gets a real attempt rather than an inherited failure. That retry needs TWO distinguishable verdicts, which is why one `classifyQueryKey` reads every key file: a file too SHORT to be a key is what a half-finished write looks like, so it is retried, while a full-length file that is not hex is corruption or a human's edit and is refused on the first attempt. A key that cannot be read yields an EMPTY hash plus a log line and never an unsalted fallback — an empty `query_hash` means either "no query" (a session-start injection) or "no key", and the column never guesses. `RecordRetrieval` is **bounded three times** because it runs on EVERY retrieval path — the search tool and, since #850, the two passive surfaces — and each bound closes a wait the other two cannot. **One, the write itself**: a 250ms sub-deadline in `assemble` — the measured uncontended cost is ~45us and the fleet's contended p99 wait ~179ms, so it sits above what contention actually costs and far below the store's 5s busy_timeout. **Two, the store's own mutex**: `sync.RWMutex` is taken through a ctx-aware `TryLock` poll, because `Lock` ignores a context and would otherwise wait out another tool call's long transaction. **Three, another process's write lock**, which is the one no context can give: that wait is SQLite's busy handler, a sleep loop inside the driver's C call, so a 250ms deadline had no effect on it at all — a `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` spent 5s inside SQLite and, through `beginWrite`'s one bounded retry, **10s**, on a search that had already been answered. So the record write does not use `beginWrite`: `beginScopedWrite` pins one connection and asks it for a `busy_timeout` of 150ms as a **ceiling**, lowered to `remaining − 40ms` when that is positive and under it — the floor is kept when the remaining deadline is at or under the margin, or when there is none, so the timeout is a ceiling this path cannot always be talked below (deliberate: it loses a row rather than a caller's time) — and restores the value it READ before returning. The pin is load-bearing: `busy_timeout` is per-connection, and an unpinned `PRAGMA` could land on a connection the transaction does not use. The store-wide five seconds is untouched for every other write, and this path does not retry, because two attempts at 150ms is past the caller's budget and a record is the one write whose loss is recoverable. The second arm matches the id in the **VALUE position** — `instr(verdicts, '"id":' || json_quote(?))` — and not as a substring of the document, because the document is serialized JSON full of the schema's own words: a bare `instr(verdicts, ?)` against an id like `id`, `stage`, `true` or `validity` matched nearly every row, and `ImportMemory` accepts any non-empty id verbatim, so a short imported id turned `ghost history purge` into a delete of the whole audit trail. Every importer (`ImportProject`, `ImportMemory`, `ImportTask`, `ImportDecision`) takes `s.mu` above the presence check, then runs the id-shape check (`CheckImportedID`, `CheckImportedProjectID`, `CheckImportedProjectText`) BEFORE any field check or error message that names the id, and re-checks presence inside the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction. `s.mu` is a per-Store lock and closes nothing across processes, so the in-transaction re-check is what makes the check-then-write atomic; `projectCollision` takes a `Queryer` and is called twice for the same reason. A record id is REFUSED rather than clamped, because a shortened key names a DIFFERENT ROW; a project id carries no length bound, because a project id is routinely a filesystem path that `ghost export` writes into the artifact. A failed project step cascades — its id is never recorded, so every memory, task and decision naming it is rejected as project-not-found. `TestThePresenceCheckIsUnderTheLockAndBeforeTheShapeCheck` and `TestNoImporterErrorAboveTheShapeCheckNamesTheID` pin the order textually, `TestAnIDAlreadyInTheStoreStaysASkip` pins idempotence across all four record types, and `TestTheShapeCheckStillPrecedesEveryMessageThatNamesTheID` pins the half that keeps a hostile id out of a refusal. `retrieval_audit` (v21, #646 part 2) is one row per (call, KEPT memory) saying what the agent did with what that call admitted, and it exists because a derived verdict has to be PERSISTED: the verdicts are a function of the transcript, and no part of Ghost can read a transcript again after the hook that wrote the sidecar has run (opencode and codex remove theirs on hook close). Nothing is backfilled, for the same reason `retrieval_record` is not, and it is sharper here — a verdict is a judgement ABOUT a transcript, so a synthesised row would report every pre-v21 call as `ignored`, a claim about sessions nobody watched, in the denominator of a report about precision. It holds ids, a fixed outcome vocabulary, a signal word and — since schema v22 — `content_hash`, the 64-character DIGEST of the text the verdict judged and never that text, and NOTHING ELSE: there is no transcript column, no query column and no content column, and the reader re-derives the memory's own tokens from the store rather than being handed them (`TestRetrievalAuditsStoresNoText` rejects the table's own vocabulary in its bytes). Growth is a `retrievalAuditRowsCap` window on rowid, oldest first, and the eviction DELETE rides in the SAME transaction as the append off the INSERT's own `RETURNING rowid`, exactly as the record cap does and for the same reason: `recorded_at` is second-precision, so two rows written in one second have no defined order by it. `record_rowid` is `NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` and ZERO IS ACCEPTED, deliberately: a verdict about a session rather than about one call is a real thing to want to record, so it is filed unattributed rather than dropped. The replacement skips it — `DELETE ... WHERE record_rowid = ?` runs only for a named call, because "replace everything with rowid 0" would delete every unattributed verdict in the table — and so a re-audit of one call is idempotent rather than doubling its denominators. **THERE IS NO FOREIGN KEY, AND THE RULE THAT STANDS IN ITS PLACE**: `project_id` is deliberately not one, so the rule is that every path DELETING `retrieval_record` rows deletes the `retrieval_audit` rows whose `record_rowid` names those rowids, IN THE SAME TRANSACTION, keyed on the CALL that is going away and never on the memory that named it — a purge is asked to remove a NAME, but the row it removes is a call that judged many memories, and a delete by `memory_id` reaches one of that call's verdicts and not the rest. It has to be this exact rule because `retrieval_record` has no AUTOINCREMENT: a freed rowid is REUSED by the next call, so a verdict left behind is not merely dangling but silently re-attributed to a call that never admitted its memory, and `RetrievalAudits` counts it in a report about precision. **THE PAIRING IS TWO-SIDED, AND THE WRITE IS THE HALF THE DELETE CANNOT REACH**: a verdict is filed from a rowid its caller read BEFORE it began judging (`audit.Run` reads the recent calls, judges them, and only then writes), so a purge landing in that window deletes the call row, takes the verdicts filed so far, and frees a rowid the next `RecordRetrieval` takes — the sweep cannot help the write that follows it, because by then the rows it would have swept are the ones being written anew. So `RecordRetrievalAudits` files a verdict only against a call that KEPT its memory, and the guard checks the call's CONTENT rather than the rowid: `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM retrieval_record WHERE rowid = ? AND <kept that memory>)`, because checking only that the rowid still EXISTS is not enough — the window is wide enough for the freed rowid to be RE-LET before the write lands (the purge removes the newest call, the next `RecordRetrieval` takes the table's maximum), and a verdict filed against the successor is the same wrong pair by another route. A verdict whose call did not keep its memory becomes a HOLE: one lost (call, memory) pair, against the alternative of a WRONG pair counted in the denominator and indistinguishable afterwards from a real one. What remains deliberately is a row whose pair is TRUE but whose call INSTANCE may be a successor that admitted the same memory — closing THAT needs `AUTOINCREMENT` on `retrieval_record`, i.e. a table rebuild and a migration, and it is not a claim about a memory a call never admitted. The guard is a `WHERE` on the INSERT rather than a branch above it, so the check runs in the same transaction as the write it guards — AND IT GATES THE REPLACE-DELETE TOO, which is the half that is easy to miss: the batch's `DELETE FROM retrieval_audit WHERE record_rowid = ?` used to run for every rowid up front and unconditionally, so a batch carrying a row the guard refuses still WIPED that rowid's existing verdicts on the way to refusing it. In the re-let window that destroys a legitimate call's evidence — a stale pass deletes the successor's stored verdict and is then refused its own row, so the table loses a pair nothing re-files and the successor's already-printed report claims a figure the table does not hold, which is the wrong number `Summary.Unfiled` exists to prevent reached through the DELETE instead of the INSERT. So a pass may replace a call's verdicts only once the guard has accepted a row for it: the claim is earned, not assumed, and the delete runs immediately after that insert with `AND rowid <> ?` so the row just written does not erase itself (which matters when a batch is PARTLY refused, the one shape where a refusal and an acceptance share a rowid). It cannot be narrowed to `(record_rowid, memory_id)` pairs instead: the call a batch may speak for is the one it read, and the rows already under that rowid may name memories this pass judged differently or never reached, and replacing them is the point. `? <= 0` keeps the accepted unattributed verdict, and `ErrNoRows` from a refused row is a drop rather than a failure — BUT IT IS RETURNED, as the refused ROWS rather than a count, because the caller has already counted every row it handed over into the figures it is about to print, so a refusal it cannot see leaves the report claiming a number the table does not hold and makes a branch that stored nothing indistinguishable from one that stored everything. The rows and not a count because the caller subtracts per source and per outcome as well as from the total: `audit.Run` reconciles them through `dropUnfiled` into `Summary.Unfiled`, takes each one out of `Verdicts`, `VerdictList` and its per-source bucket, and `Summary.String()` prints the loss BESIDE the figures ("N stored verdict(s) and not the M judged") rather than folding it into them — a report whose figures describe the table and state the difference. Absent when nothing was refused, because a line that always prints is a line an operator learns to skip. The predicate is `retrievalRecordKeepingMemory`, which is deliberately NOT the purge's `retrievalRecordNamingMemory`: the purge must MISS nothing and so keeps a fuzzy textual arm for rows whose verdicts JSON cannot be walked, while a report must claim nothing it cannot support, so an unreadable verdicts column reads as a call that kept nothing. `kept` is compared as `= 1` and not `= '1'`/`= 'true'`, because `->>` returns a JSON boolean as the INTEGER 1 (`typeof` 'integer') and the text forms compare an integer against text, match nothing, and silently reduce the guard to "never". What it costs per row is one primary-key seek on `retrieval_record` plus a walk of that ONE row's verdicts array — O(one call), not O(rows stored), so it needs no index on `record_rowid` and `TestRetrievalAuditsCarryOneIndex` is unaffected. `DeleteProject` deletes both tables by `project_id` and was correct from the start; `ghost history purge` collects the doomed rowids with `retrievalRecordNamingMemory()` — ONE function building the TWO statements' predicate, because two copies of that JSON could drift into removing the record rows while keeping their verdicts — and the cap's eviction takes them by the SAME rowid bound (`record_rowid > 0 AND record_rowid <= ?`, where `> 0` is load-bearing: 0 is the accepted "not attributable to a call" value and a bare `<= ?` would read it as inside the range and take every unattributed verdict in the table). The purge's sweep runs BEFORE the record delete because its predicate is a subquery over rows that are about to be deleted and can no longer be seen once they are, and it costs one scan of a table bounded at `retrievalAuditRowsCap` — the same scan `TestRetrievalAuditsCarryOneIndex` says the report's own read already costs, and the reason that test forbids an index on `record_rowid`. No trigger and no schema version: the pairing is a code invariant, and `TestAPurgeDoesNotLeaveAVerdictThatTheNextCallCanInherit`, `TestTheRecordCapTakesTheVerdictsWithIt`, `TestTheRecordCapPairsOnEveryEvictionNotOnlyTheFirst`, `TestEveryWriterThatDeletesRetrievalRecordsTakesTheirVerdicts`, `TestAVerdictForACallThatHasGoneAwayIsDroppedRatherThanInherited`, `TestAVerdictForACallThatHasBeenReplacedIsNotInheritedByItsSuccessor`, `TestAVerdictIsNotFiledAgainstACallThatConsideredItsMemoryAndDroppedIt`, `TestTheAuditCapStillBoundsTheTableWhenAPassDropsARow`, `TestTheAuditWriteReportsTheVerdictsItRefused`, `TestTheSummaryCountsOnlyTheVerdictsThatWereFiled`, `TestTheSummaryKeepsEveryFigureWhenNothingWasRefused`, `TestTheSummaryNamesTheRefusals` and `TestRunReconcilesWhatTheStoreRefused`, `TestALatePassCannotWipeTheSuccessorsVerdicts` and `TestAPartlyRefusedBatchStillKeepsTheRowsItFiled` hold each path and each way the write-side guard can be broken to it (#852). The export path excludes the table exactly as it excludes `retrieval_record`, since both are derived evidence about a machine's own sessions and neither survives an import onto another one. **THE AUDIT'S NEGATIVE VERDICTS REACH resolve AND reflect, AND `used`/`ignored` CANNOT (#648)**: `Store.UsefulnessByMemory(projectID)` returns `{contradicted, superseded_in_session}` counts per memory plus the latest of THOSE two verdicts' session and date, and it is the only evidence resolve and reflect are given. The two positive buckets are filtered in the reader's SQL and not at the call sites, because `used` means a call RETURNED the memory — true of everything in the result set — so in front of a classifier it is the #284 popularity loop: a memory that has merely been shown starts to look reliable because it is often enough in the context, and a maintainer told so keeps it. A reader returning four buckets and trusting two callers not to look at the other two is one careless caller away from a boost, so `TestUsefulnessByMemoryCountsTheNegativeBucketsAndNothingElse`, `TestAUsedOrIgnoredVerdictChangesNothingResolveSendsTheHarness`, `TestAUsedOrIgnoredVerdictChangesNothingThePromptSays` and the two `cmd/ghost` capture tests hold BYTE EQUALITY of what each pass sends, so a leak as a count, a ratio, a mention in the most-recent clause or a trailing byte all fail them. The most recent is therefore the latest NEGATIVE verdict and never the latest row — on any store this is for, the latest row is usually a `used` verdict, and ranking by it would report a popularity signal under an evidence label. The project scope is REQUIRED rather than defaulted, since another project's contradiction is not a claim about this one's corpus. **THE READER IS ALSO NARROWED TWICE MORE, AND BOTH NARROWINGS ERR TOWARD SILENCE**, because the failure this reader must not have is a WRONG annotation and not a missing one. (1) A verdict whose `degraded` column is set came off a PARTIAL transcript read, and the schema says outright that a reader which cannot see that caveat reads the row as a claim about the session — so `degraded <> ''` is filtered in SQL rather than qualified at render time, which keeps the byte-for-byte promise: a memory whose only negative verdicts are degraded renders NOTHING, not a weaker line that is still a line. `internal/audit` prints "N of those verdicts are degraded" for the same reason. (2) Every verdict is kept only while it still DESCRIBES the content the memory holds now, and since schema v22 that comparison is against `retrieval_audit.content_hash` — the hash of the text the auditor judged, stamped by `audit.Run` from `memory.ContentHash`. A verdict is a claim about the CONTENT a call admitted, and Ghost rewrites content IN PLACE under a stable id — `ReplaceNonManual`'s reuse branch and `ghost_memory_update` both UPDATE `content` on the existing row — while nothing deletes the audit rows for a rewritten memory (`retrieval_audit` has no foreign key to `memories`, and the only DELETE by `memory_id` is `PurgeMemoryHistory`'s). Without that rule the next pass annotates a claim written AFTER the contradiction it is quoting. The rule is per VERDICT, not per memory: a memory with one pre-rewrite and one post-rewrite verdict keeps the post-rewrite figure, and one whose text has since changed renders nothing at all — byte-for-byte the input the pass had before the audit existed. A row whose stored stamp is EMPTY — every verdict written before v22, and nothing is backfilled onto one — falls back to the NAMED LEGACY RULE, `recorded_at >= updated_at`, which is the pre-v22 rule verbatim: the old rows' judged text is not recorded anywhere, and a hash computed against TODAY's text would make a stale row look current. The hash arm, the withdrawal arm and the legacy arm are pinned separately by `TestAHashedVerdictSurvivesAMetadataOnlyEdit`, `TestAHashedVerdictIsWithdrawnWhenTheContentChanges` and `TestAVerdictWithNoHashFollowsTheLegacyStampRule`. **WHY THE RULE IS A HASH AND NOT A TIMESTAMP**: `updated_at` is not a content clock. `UpdateMemory` moves it on a METADATA-ONLY edit (store.go:5164, unconditionally, including the tags/importance/category case its own comment names at store.go:5145), and `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch (store.go:5451-5453, the UPDATE at store.go:6160-6172, reached through `ApplyReflection`) moves it after writing an IDENTICAL `content` back — `reusePreservesAge` compares content and category only, so any re-emission matching on those two bumps the stamp whether or not importance, tags or scope changed; only the narrower `reuseChangesNothing` case skips the write (#727). So a retag, a re-weight or a `ghost_memory_update --verified` on a memory the audit contradicted used to WITHHOLD that memory's evidence from every later resolve and reflect pass — silently, with no error, no report line and no log. `resolve_kept_hash` was the near miss and cannot serve (`MarkResolveKept` stamps it only on a resolve KEEP, `SetResolved` and `MarkResolved` clear it, and no content edit writes it), so the stamp lives beside the verdict as its own column. The digest is `sha256(ContentHashVersion || NUL || content)` hex — of the TEXT alone, so a tag, importance, scope or verified edit leaves it standing while a rewrite moves it — and it is ONE implementation: `internal/resolve`'s KEEP-cache key delegates to `memory.ContentHash`, so the two can never drift apart. Bumping `ContentHashVersion` withdraws every stored verdict at once, which is deliberate and survivable for the same reason every other narrowing here is: it errs toward silence and toward re-asking, never toward a wrong annotation. Passive validity (#892): the passive fetch applies the validity window inside `passiveFetchSQL`, BEFORE its `LIMIT`, against the request's `Now` (`validityMatchesSQL`, one predicate shared by the fetch and the exclusion probe; held to `ValidityState` by `TestValiditySQLAgreesWithValidityState`, which includes fractional-second bounds and a fractional Now; the SQL compares at whole-second precision, so it is a SUPERSET of Go's verdict, may keep a row stage 2 then drops, and never drops one Go keeps), so a closed or not-yet-open row never spends the over-fetch. Assemble stage 2 stays the authority and must agree. Rows the predicate removed from an emptied bucket are counted into `CandidateSet.ValidityExcluded`, a lower bound on what stage 2 would withhold, which the assembler reports as `all_invalid` rather than `no_memories`.

**TWO BOUNDED READS PER PASS — ONE SNAPSHOT, TAKEN BY ONE DEFERRED READ TRANSACTION**, read-only, and both consumers FAIL OPEN on an unreadable map (the annotation is an addition to a judgement that works without it; failing the pass would make the audit a dependency of maintenance rather than an input to it). The two reads are the project's negative verdicts and then the content of exactly the memories those verdicts name — **ONE ROW PER MEMORY**, because a single `JOIN`ed statement would project a full copy of `memories.content` (the largest field in the table) once per VERDICT row, where `retrieval_audit` is capped at 50 000 rows and `memories.content` is unbounded — and the transaction is what makes the pair ONE SNAPSHOT, so a verdict another process deleted between the reads is never counted. `UsefulnessEvidence.Line` renders ONE fixed format from counts and a session id, and the id is rendered through **memory.SafeToken — an ALLOWLIST, not a denylist**, which is the same function `assemble.Token` delegates to, so there is one implementation of the rule rather than one per reader. The id is **BOUNDED TO 64 RUNES FIRST and quoted second**, and the order is load-bearing: bounding second would cut an ESCAPE rather than a rune and return a malformed literal. A value is written **bare when every rune is allowlisted** — ASCII letters, digits and the seven separators `._-:/@+`, which is every id Ghost mints (32 hex characters) and every scope name — and otherwise **the WHOLE id becomes one ASCII-quoted Go string**. It is an allowlist because a denylist can only list what somebody thought of: the renderer this replaced was one, and it passed U+2028 and U+0085 (LINE TERMINATORS to JavaScript and to several newline-splitting readers), a bidi override that reverses how the rest of a line displays, and a zero-width space that no reader can see. The set is pinned EXACTLY by `TestTheBareTokenAllowlistIsExactlyTheDocumentedSet`, exhaustively over every code point, because a table of hostile examples passes whenever the set is widened by a character nobody listed — and by `TestTheBareTokenAllowlistHoldsNothingInvisible`, which names the exclusions that are printable and therefore hard to notice by eye. Note the ceiling the rune bound does NOT give: under ASCII-only quoting a BMP rune escapes to six characters and an ASTRAL one to ten, so 64 escaping runes render 642 bytes and the line 703 — measured, against 125 for a bare id, where the renderer it replaced bounded the OUTPUT at about 64. It is still a constant, which is what "without limit" has to mean. The bound exists because reflect lists one memory per line inside «...» and a stored id carrying `\n- id:E1 …` would forge a record the model then emits an operation against. A memory with NO audit rows renders NOTHING, not an empty bracket: `TestAMemoryWithNoAuditRowsProducesThePromptItProducedBefore` and its resolve twin compare the whole prompt byte for byte, so an unaudited project is unaffected by this and cannot be blamed for a verdict that moved. resolve puts the line INSIDE the note's own content, so it lands in the «...» the classifier's `quoteData` renders and is read under the rubric's own data rule; and `reassess` deliberately reads none (its evidence points toward staying resolved, the opposite of a repair). **THE EVIDENCE IS READ ABOVE THE CACHE GATE, AND THE STAMP COVERS IT** (#880): `resolve.Run` reads the map ONCE per pass whenever any candidate reaches the cache gate, and the gate matches `keptHashes[m.ID] == KeepStamp(m.Content, evidence[m.ID])` — `memory.ContentHash(content)` BYTE FOR BYTE when the audit has no negative verdict about that content, and the content hash combined with the two negative counts plus the latest negative verdict's stamp when it does. So a pre-#880 hash stored for un-doubted content is still a cache hit and no `keepCacheHashVersion` bump was needed, while a contradiction recorded AFTER the KEEP does not match: the memory goes back in the pending set, is re-asked ONCE with the line attached, and the KEEP that comes back is stamped with that same evidence, so the next pass matches and skips until the audit records another verdict, which moves the stamp once more. **A negative verdict RE-ASKS; it never RESCUES.** It reaches the classifier as data and the classifier decides, exactly as it decides everything else — never `resolve` on its own, and never a demotion. The bound is what makes the cost arguable: a pass with nothing at the gate (nothing prefiltered, or nothing the veto did not settle) pays no read at all, and the only NEW payer is the converged pass, which pays ONE bounded read and still zero classifier calls (measured by `TestMeasuredCostOfTheConvergedPassEvidenceRead`). Positive verdicts cannot move a stamp — `used` and `ignored` are filtered in SQL — so a memory that is merely retrieved often can never re-ask itself, which is the #284 loop closed at the reader rather than at this comparison. The content key itself is unchanged: a retag or a re-weight still re-asks nothing, because neither is evidence about the content — an audit verdict arrives independently of the content, which is why it rides in the stamp instead of replacing the hash. The repair pass compares the BARE content hash and reads no evidence (#880 re-examined that exemption and kept it): it cannot construct a stamp covering evidence it never read, so a stamp carrying evidence costs one re-ask per repair run over that row, erring toward asking; the ordinary pass then re-stamps it and both are quiet in between. The cache gate in `internal/resolve/resolve.go` says the same where a reader of the gate will meet it. `InputSignature` is deliberately unchanged, so `--skip-unchanged` still skips a corpus that has merely been audited — mirroring the evidence would stop the gate skipping on any store that records verdicts, and the audit grows on most sessions.
- `internal/assemble/` — context assembly: `assemble.Run` is the one entry point (one `[]stage` in `pipeline.go`), owning selection, validity, category/scope predicates before window closure, provenance (pinned at 1.0), budget closure, the outcome, the `response_fit` post-pass, the always-recorded trace and the shared `Item.Line()` renderer. Depends on one method, `Candidates`; `internal/memory` never imports it. `ScopeLabel` is the scope label `Item.Line()` embeds and the session-start block prints, so no surface re-derives it; `ScopeContradicts` is the named negation of `memory.ScopeMatches` rather than a second copy of the rule. `Result.Leaks()` and `Trace.TrimmedByBudget()` are the ONLY way a consumer learns which rows it should not have carried and what the budget cut, and both READ the verdicts the stages recorded (`Trace.Signals[id]`: `ValidityState`, `ScopeMatched`, `ProjectMatch`, plus the row's `resolved_at`) rather than re-deciding them — a second classifier anywhere, and the benchmark is the surface that wants one, is a second implementation of the same rules free to drift until a leak is reported as clean by the only component whose job is to say otherwise. `LeakArms` is the fixed report order with zeros included, and a consumer meeting an arm it has no row for refuses rather than counting that row clean. The session-start block is a caller of `Run` (`loadSessionPassive`, `internal/mcpinit/session_passive.go`), and it is the reason the golden block is byte-identical for every branch its fixture exercises: the block's rows are chosen by ONE `assemble.Run` with two bucket policies, where before they were chosen by two private queries with two private over-fetches, two private two-pass selections and two private near-duplicate passes, so the assembler's stages ran over rows a second implementation of the same rules had already picked. A `SourceSessionStart` with an empty `Query` is a PASSIVE request, and `Store.Candidates` serves it from `CandidateRequest.Passive`; a source alone is not what makes it passive, which is why the empty `Query` is load-bearing rather than decorative. The golden's byte-identity is scoped rather than absolute, and the exception is the `_global` bucket's SUPERSEDE demotion: `loadGlobalMemories` ran only the near-duplicate lookup over global rows, while the assembler's passive demotion runs `SupersedePenalties` for every bucket with no field to decline it, so a `supersedes` edge between two `_global` rows now pushes the replaced row out of a bucket capped at 8 — a membership change, not a reorder. It is kept, because that bucket already DROPS its near-duplicate losers on the stated ground that a superseded preference is not worth one of eight cross-project slots, and an explicit edge is a stronger signal than a cosine neighbour. `TestTheGlobalBucketNowDemotesSupersededRows` states it, and the golden cannot: its one `supersedes` edge joins two project rows. PASSIVE is keyed on the ABSENCE OF A QUERY (`assemble.Passive`), not on `Source`, because the consequences follow from the absence: no leg ran, so no FTS rank and no cosine exist to compare, a passive block is NEVER `weak` (it is `answerable`/`not_applicable` — `weak` would be a relevance verdict about a question the surface was never asked), and an empty passive window is `no_memories` rather than `no_candidates` because the passive path never counts the corpus. Both wordings are refusals in the other direction and each is a different lie: `no matching memories found` claims a census that was never taken, and `no sufficiently trustworthy memory found` claims rows were found and rejected when the window came back empty. A window that came back NON-empty and was then emptied by a stage IS an exclusion and says so in the shared wording. TWO passive requests are REFUSED rather than served, both because the seam cannot honour what they ask and would answer a confident false negative instead. One: a slice's bucket must be the requested project or `_global` (`validatePassiveBudget`). The bucket IS the project predicate — the store binds `SlicePolicy.Bucket` as the `WHERE` clause and never consults `Mode` — so a mismatched bucket would READ AND INJECT a project the request never named, and stage 3 only RECORDS that as `Signals[id].ProjectMatch=false`, which nothing refuses; the same decision `sliceBuckets` makes for a repeated bucket, one named bucket per slice, and it has to be a bucket this request is about. Two: a passive request cannot carry a `Category` or `Retention` filter. `passiveFetchSQL` binds no category and no tier in its SQL at all, while the query path WIDENS the window when either is set (`predicateFetchWiden`, what closed #573) precisely so a matching row below the cut stays reachable — and the passive branch of `retrievalWindow` returns before that widening, because the window there is the policies' own over-fetches. So the filter never applied at all: stage 3 drops every non-matching row out of a window nothing widened, and the caller gets `all_out_of_category` while the store holds the rows it asked for just below the cut. That is the damage, and it is why this is a refusal and not a widened fetch: a window is a bound, and an over-reported one costs a field's honesty, but a filter decides MEMBERSHIP, and a caller told its category is absent from the project will not widen anything. `abstain_cosine` therefore reports `not_applied` for a passive block rather than `off`: `off` is a configuration a reader can change, and the session-start surface has no vector arm to configure, whatever `context.abstain_cosine` is set to. The budget is what carries the bucket policies — `Slice` gains `OverFetch`/`Order`/`TwoPass`/`BehaviorFloor`/`BehaviorCategories`/`CategoryWeights`/`CategoryCaps`/`DemotionThreshold`/`DemoteOnlyWhenOverCap` — because a passive retrieval has no query to shape it, and a passive slice stating neither an over-fetch nor a cap is REFUSED at both seams: passive runs at every session start, and an unbounded fetch is a store scan. `Slice.MaxItems`/`MaxBytes` still bound membership, and `Budget.MaxItems`/`MaxBytes` stay 0 for both passive surfaces because `Budget.MaxBytes` bounds the complete rendered RESPONSE, which is the search framing a differently-framed surface must not spend (see `Result`). A `SourceProjectCtx` with an empty `Query` is the same PASSIVE shape, and it backs FOUR agent-facing surfaces: `ghost_project_context`, the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt share ONE budget statement (`projectContextBudget`), so the tool's caller-supplied `limit` and the resource's two fixed read caps — a 20-row project window, and a 15-row `_global` second read the resource's section adds to that window's own `_global` half — cannot drift apart, and the tool's block is byte-identical through the move — the fixture seeds no validity column, so the one behaviour the move changes (a closed or not-yet-opened window is DROPPED rather than rendered with an `expired` marker, because stage 2 runs unconditionally) is pinned by `TestTheProjectContextDropsRowsWhoseWindowHasClosed` and NOT by the goldens, which would have to be re-recorded to hold it and a re-recorded baseline is a diff record rather than a proof. Its bucket is the one that sets `IncludeGlobal`, so the assembler's per-slice cap is keyed on `Candidate.FetchedBy` — the POLICY that admitted the row — rather than on the row's own project (`capBucket`), and a request that both mixes `_global` into one bucket and fetches it in another is REFUSED (`validatePassiveBudget`): the overlap is invisible to `sliceBuckets` because the names differ, every global row would be admitted twice, and neither slice's cap would describe the block. The check resolves the flag across the whole budget BEFORE the loop, because a version that sets it as it walks refuses one slice order and serves the other — a refusal a caller talks past by sorting. `Request.Record` is a per-REQUEST `RecordSink` (no package global, so two Runs cannot record into each other's store, and nil — the bench and every test here — records nothing) that receives ONE `memory.RetrievalRecord` per SUCCESSFUL Run, and the projection in `record.go` is a THIRD reader of the trace's verdicts beside `Leaks()` and `TrimmedByBudget()`: kept rows come from `Result.Items` and dropped rows from `Trace.Decisions`, and nothing is re-derived. It is set by THREE surfaces and no others, all three through the same seam: `ghost_memory_search` in its handler, `assembleProjectContext` for the tool, the resource and the prompt, and `loadSessionPassive` for the session-start block (#850 — before it, only the search recorded, so every per-source denominator in the audit described searches). Its placement is the whole correctness of the record — `emit` runs after `fitResponse` and only when it returned no error, because the post-pass drops rows from the bottom of the ranking and records each as a `response_fit` decision (a record written earlier would name a row the caller never received as KEPT, which is the false "used" verdict that flatters Ghost), and because a failed retrieval, a refused request and an unfittable envelope all return an error instead of a Result, and a record for any of them would put a denominator in the audit for calls that returned no memories at all. `Request.SuppressRecordWhenLegsFailed` covers the one case `Run` cannot see from inside: a result with a FAILED leg and nothing admitted is a valid `Result` that the MCP handler then CONVERTS into a retryable error, so an agent never reads "nothing matched" as a fact. A row the retrieval window CUT is in neither `Result.Items` nor `Trace.Decisions` and is deliberately NOT recorded: it was never judged, and a verdict for it would claim a decision nobody made. `queryHash` asks the SINK for the store's keyed digest — an HMAC under the per-install key, not a hash of the question — and is EMPTY for an empty query rather than the digest of nothing, because a constant in a digest column reads as a fingerprint and a passive session start is exactly the call that has no question. Any agent-visible rendering of a stored id goes through `Token`, a name or path through `Label`, and free text through `quoteData` inside «…» under the `dataDelimiterNote` line; a new listing tool or resource must use them. `Token` renders a KEY and quotes a space, so `Label` exists for the values that are normally full of them, and it keeps ordinary text byte-identical because that is the point of it. `PreviewLine` is the ONE first-line cut of stored text: three copies had drifted, and a test per copy is what let them drift. `ShortID` is the ONE eight-character id form for an agent-facing surface (`mcpserver.shortID` is a delegation to it, and `cmd/ghost` and `memref.Short` keep their own because a report line is pasted back and an id there has to stay copyable): the eight are CHARACTERS measured by `memref.Short`, the result goes through `Token`, and a note is rendered bare between `(Note: ` and `)` with no `«…»` around it, so an id Token had to quote is shown whole rather than as eight runes of an escape. Every rune `isTokenRune` writes bare is ASCII, so the rune measurement is currently unobservable through `ShortID` and reverting it survives the suite — it is kept because it is the shared rule and because widening `isTokenRune` to a non-ASCII name is the one edit that makes it matter. `TagsLabel` is the ONE ` tags:[…]` label for the same reason `formatMemories` no longer marshals a tag list itself: two implementations of one rule are two rules, with the untested one shipping the bug. A tag list is a JSON array printed OUTSIDE `«…»`, and `json.Marshal` escapes a newline, a quote and a backslash but not `«»` or a backtick, so `neutralizeDelimiters` rewrites the delimiters as `<<`/`>>` — after the marshal, because substituting first introduces ASCII `<`/`>` that the encoder then HTML-escapes into noise — and a backtick becomes `\u0060`, the JSON escape the surrounding array already uses. Two conventions differ on purpose: `<<` is what a content block prints for a literal `«`, so a reader who has met one knows the other, and there is no such convention for a backtick. A stored tag is neutralised rather than refused because refusing a value on the way OUT of a backup costs the user the whole memory — and the same field reaches `internal/reflection`'s consolidation prompt, which prints it with NEITHER JSON NOR delimiters on a line the model emits operations against, so a newline there ends the record. That surface escapes the field too, with `|` as its separator because a comma cannot be one, and it carries its own `neutralizeDelimiters` because that function is UNEXPORTED and assemble imports memory, so sharing it would mean exporting it. That surface's class is the LARGER one — every control character, not just the delimiters — and it also escapes its own separators, which is a semantic rather than a rendering problem: a tag holding the separator reads as a different tag COUNT, and `TestTheTwoTagSubstitutionsAgreeOnDelimiters` pins the spelling it shares with the row while `TestTheOrdinaryTagListIsUnchanged` pins that the escaping is invisible for every tag a real store holds. The ordinary cases stay byte-identical — `tags:["golden","pinned"]` is what every existing store, golden and test asserts on. `TestLabelKeepsOrdinaryTextAndNeutralisesTheRest`, `TestLabelNeverForgesALineWhateverItHolds`, `TestPreviewLineCutsAtACarriageReturnAsWellAsANewline`, `TestAGuillemetInsideATagCannotOpenADataBlock` and `TestShortIDMeasuresEightCharactersAndRendersThroughTheSafeToken` pin them. `TagsLabel` also BOUNDS each tag it prints at `MaxRenderedTagLen` with a `…[tag truncated]` marker, per tag rather than per list — see "A rendered label is bounded by the RENDERER" under Key patterns for the direction that makes it a bound and not a data change.
- `internal/audit/` — retrieval audit (#646 part 2): heuristic comparison of what Ghost retrieved against what the agent WROTE, and the whole package is one line it refuses to cross. The transcript holds both what the agent said and what Ghost handed it, and the injected block repeats a memory's wording verbatim — so a comparison that read the whole file would report every retrieval as used, whatever the agent did, which is the one result that makes the audit worthless in the exact way it was built to detect. Only agent-authored regions count, and the three ingest paths are kept apart for three different rules: `AddProse` (assistant text) draws BOTH the token and the negation arms, `AddSaveArgs` (the arguments of Ghost's own save tools) is a restatement and gets its own arm, and `AddToolArgs` (every other tool call) gets NEITHER the negation arm nor a restatement — a tool argument is a string literal, so the cue words in it are the tool's, not a denial of anything, and routing them back through `AddProse` is what made `contradicted` fire on ordinary use (`TestAnOrdinaryUseIsNotAContradiction`). Precedence is contradiction > identifier > token > save restatement > ignored. **A CONTRADICTION IS A STRONG SIGNAL OR IT IS NOT ONE**, and both of its arms use the token arm's OWN bar (`clearsTokenBar`: at least 3 shared tokens AND at least a third of the memory's tokens, so `matched >= tokenFloor && matched*tokenFraction >= total`), because contradiction outranks every other verdict and a loose bar there reports ordinary use as a finding an operator would act on — a second, weaker threshold for negation was the original design and it was wrong. The bar is on the CUES being denials OF A CLAIM (`TestTheNegationCuesAreAllDenialsOfAClaim`), and the words must be the MEMORY'S OWN: the id arm needs a denial cue in the same SEGMENT as the id, so a cue in one sentence cannot satisfy an id named in another. **A CUE COUNTS ONLY AS A DENIAL OF THE MEMORY IT IS BOUND TO**, and every step of that is one rule rather than a matcher (#854). A cue is a run of WORDS matched on word boundaries (`negationCueWords` + `cueSpans` over `splitWords`), never a substring — "ignored"/"ignores" and "falsehood"/"falsely" are ordinary English, and an agent that named an id and then talked about its own ignored files had AGREED, which substring matching filed as the agent finding the memory wrong; every entry in the list is asserted in both directions by `TestACueIsMatchedAsWholeWords`, because a list matched as substrings passes "some cue works" for all fifteen. Then it must be BOUND, and the two arms bind by DIFFERENT rules because the thing bound differs: an id is bound by COUNTING the words between it and the cue (`boundToCue`), and that count is bounded twice over: up to `cueGap = 1` word however it is spelled, or up to `cueFillerGap = 3` words **if every one of them is in the CLOSED SET `cueFillers`** (#860) — the determiners an agent uses for a memory (the, this, that, a, an), the nouns Ghost's own vocabulary is written in (memory, note, entry, advice), the one preposition that binds a cue to its object (in), and the two adverbs that interrupt a denial (now, also). One word was narrower than English: "Memory <id> is now obsolete", "disregard the memory <id>" and "ignore the advice in <id>" are all denials and each puts a determiner, a noun or a preposition between the cue and the id, so each came back `used`. A set rather than a distance, because the DISTANCE is not what was wrong: widening `cueGap` for arbitrary words would trade those misses for the false contradiction #858 exists to prevent, since the sentence that rule is FOR — "Per <id>, I'll ignore the formatting" — also reaches an id across two ordinary words. It is MEMBERSHIP and not proximity: every word between them must be in the set, so a gap of the allowance's width holding ONE word outside it is not an allowance at all ("ignore the formatter, <id>"), and a run of four is beyond the width ("ignore the memory note in <id>") — both pinned by `TestTheGapWidensOnlyAcrossAClosedSetOfWords`. What the set must not contain is a SUBJECT or a VERB, since those are what a sentence's own clause is made of ("per", "my", "formatter", "covers"), and the guard above survives only because no filler is one of them — note that "I'll" was never safe by being a subject but by tokenising to two words, so "Per <id>, I now ignore the formatting" is the same sentence with the contraction expanded and is pinned in the same place. The same set is transparent INSIDE a cue's own run of words, bounded at `cueRunFillers = 1` (`cueRun`), so "is now obsolete" is the cue "is obsolete" with an adverb in it and is a denial rather than no cue at all; a separate constant from `cueFillerGap` because it is a different question — what separates two words of ONE cue, not a cue and what it denies — and since a cue run is the whole of what makes a sentence a negation SEGMENT, the wider bound would be widening the set of sentences this package reads as denials. Every one of those is a membership test rather than a distance, so a cue still cannot match inside "xis wrongy" and "is very obsolete" is not a cue at all. **AND NEVER ACROSS A CLAUSE BOUNDARY** (#860), which is a SEPARATE rule and not a wider condition on the count, because the words either side of a boundary can be zero and the binding still has to die: `splitClauses` splits a sentence into its words AND the clause each word falls in (`clauseBoundary` — comma, semicolon, colon, and the ASCII, em and en dashes), and `boundToCue` requires the cue's first word and the id to be in the SAME clause, so the rule holds in BOTH directions — "Memory <id> is wrong" binds and "Memory <id>, that is wrong" does not. It cannot be fixed by one fewer filler, and the reason is the whole of it: the words in `cueFillers` are precisely the words an agent uses to CITE a memory, so an allowance that spans them also spans the punctuation of ordinary agreeing prose. "The build is stale, the memory <id> applies" denies the BUILD and cites a memory, and every word between the cue and the id is in the set — the citation and the filler run are the same words. Nor is it a consequence of the widening: "The CI is deprecated, also <id> covers lockfiles" and "Disregard this, <id> is accurate" each reach the id across a SINGLE arbitrary word, so the comma was already the only thing stopping them on a gap of one. The cost is stated rather than discovered: a denial phrased across a boundary is a contradiction that goes UNFILED, which is the direction `cueGap` already chose, and a dash boundary splits a hyphenated compound so "ignore the memory-entry <id>" no longer binds. Parentheses, quotes and an ellipsis are deliberately NOT boundaries — they bracket a clause rather than ending one, and the marking they do is the cue's own. The rule is the ID arm's alone and the asymmetry is the point rather than an omission: the fingerprints are bound by `boundPositions`' skip, which crosses every boundary, because "that is wrong: <the memory's wording>" is a denial followed by its RESTATEMENT and the words on the far side of the colon ARE the memory, whereas an id is only a NAME for the memory, which the words around it are free to introduce and walk away from (`TestACueIsNotBoundAcrossAClauseBoundary`, which asserts the verdicts, the clause ids themselves, and the two-sidedness at the mechanism; `cueRun` applies the same clause test to the cue's own MATCHED word as well as its fillers, or a run straddles a boundary and the span has no single clause to compare an id against). **AND THE TWO HOLES THIS DOES NOT COVER ARE PINNED, NOT ASSUMED CLOSED** (`TestWhatTheClauseBoundaryDoesNotCover`): the FINGERPRINT arm's skip still crosses every boundary, so `boundPositions` lands on the closed-set noun of a citation and a memory whose own wording contains that noun is still filed contradicted — "The build is stale, the memory <id> says <the memory's wording>" — which is pre-existing (verified at d0f53686 and at main), not introduced here, and closing it needs the skip to tell a COLON from a COMMA, which an int clause id cannot express and which the colon-restatement positives forbid: the boundary would have to become a boundary KIND rather than a boundary COUNT. And `;` is in `clauseBoundary` AND in `sentenceSplit`, and that overlap is a property of the CALLER rather than of the function: a `;` DOES reach `clauseBoundary` on every prose scan, because `splitWords` is `splitClauses` with the clause ids dropped and `addWords` calls it through `memoryIDs` over the same text — so the counter splits at the `;` and the split is then never read. What is unreachable is a `;` on the only path that READS the ids and can therefore change a verdict, because inside `AddProse` `segments` split at the `;` first; the exported `HasNegationCue` is the other reader and has no such guard, so it can be handed a multi-sentence string by a careless caller and is only as disciplined as that caller. It is kept so both boundary lists can be audited against each other. **AND NEVER BEFORE A CUE'S FIRST WORD**, which is the one place that tolerance could become a second way to widen `cueGap`: a filler AHEAD of a cue is part of the distance its object is measured over, so consuming it would move the span's leading edge onto the filler, and both span consumers then read from the wrong position — `boundToCue` would measure a one-word-shorter gap (filing "Per <id>, I now ignore the formatting" contradicted) and `insideCue` would report the filler as cue text, so `boundPositions` steps over it and a memory whose own distinctive word IS that filler ("the entry that records weekly releases", denied by "that entry is obsolete, we discussed weekly releases earlier") stops being contradicted at all. Those are opposite errors from one mechanism, and the second is the price of naming `memory`/`note`/`entry`/`advice` at all — the alternative lets the skip stop ON the word, a false contradiction on any memory sharing a cue-adjacent closed-set word — so the miss is the one taken, being the same honest direction `cueGap` already chose (`TestANAdjacentFillerIsStillApartOfTheDistance`, which asserts the SPANS as well as both verdicts because a verdict can be reached by the other arm), while the fingerprints are bound by SKIPPING to the nearest word on each side that could BE a token (`boundPositions` + `isDistinctive`), a skip that crosses punctuation without looking at it because a clause boundary is not a word, which is what makes "that is wrong: <the memory's wording>" a denial rather than a missed one; the id arm cannot use that skip because an id is 32 characters and would always survive the filter, reaching the cue across a subject clause — and the id arm carries the clause rule below instead, which is strictly tighter than the skip it refuses: the skip crosses EVERY boundary and the id arm crosses none. `boundToCue` counts a SLICE of the words STRICTLY between them, which is what makes both constants readable and is what stopped `cueGap` from meaning adjacency by accident: it was once a pair of position bounds (`c.start-cueGap-1`/`c.end+cueGap+1`) where the ±1 is the cue's own edge, and dropping that ±1 reads as "one word may separate them" while admitting none — silently dropping "ignore memory <id>", the construction the constant exists for, while the comment still claimed it (#858). A slice has no such edge to get wrong (`TestTheGapIsAWordGapAndNotAdjacency` holds the sentence rather than the formula, and its fixtures carry NO memory wording so the fingerprint arm cannot carry them by accident). What a cue binds to is also NEVER THE CUE'S OWN WORDS (`insideCue`): they are ordinary tokens — "ignore", "stale", "wrong", "superseded" all clear `minTokenLen` and none is a stopword — so recording them lets a memory whose wording happens to contain one satisfy the binding while the cue is about the changelog (`TestTheCuesOwnWordsAreNotWhatACueIsBoundTo`), and a sideward skip that stops on a SECOND cue in the same sentence binds the first one to it (`TestASidewardSkipStepsOverASecondCue`). The fingerprint arm therefore needs BOTH `clearsTokenBar` and one of the words clearing it to be bound to the cue (`deniesByWording`), so a sentence that denies a formatter and then quotes the memory verbatim is not a denial of the memory — the 3-token floor is retained on top of the binding, never replaced by it. Because the binding is what the comparison asks about, it is what the sidecar carries: the `neg` line gained a third field kind, a cue-bound fingerprint marked with `~` (`cueMark`), so the header is `v2` and `ReadSidecar` REFUSES a v1 file rather than reading it back with nothing bound — a v1 line parses cleanly and would silently unfind every contradiction in that turn. A token is `HMAC-SHA256(key, lower(word))[:8]` as sixteen lower-case hex, not a hash: an UNKEYED digest of a word is a pure function of the word, so a reader holding a word list reproduces the file exactly, and the word list in question is this project's own database — the comment that claimed a hash "cannot be read back as that word" was false. The key is the per-install key that already guards `query_hash`, read through `memory.ReadRetrievalKey` and NEVER created by either side, so a store that has never recorded a retrieval writes no sidecar and loses one turn's audit rather than putting a reversible file in the shared temp directory (`TestTheTokensAreKeyedRatherThanHashed`, `TestAnUnkeyedSignalsProducesNothingRatherThanUnkeyedTokens`, `TestTheHookAndTheChildMustSignWithTheSameKey`). There is no unkeyed fallback and no way to build one: `Hasher.distinctTokens` returns nothing for a keyless hasher (HMAC will happily hash under an empty key, so the zero value would otherwise produce a file of perfectly reversible tokens while looking keyed), a keyless `Signals` is `Empty` so the hook never even attempts a write, and `WriteSidecar` REFUSES one — the one way to get an unkeyed file on disk is to not be able to build one. The hasher is a PARAMETER to `ReadSidecar` rather than something the file carries, because a key written into the sidecar would be a key beside the hashes it protects, and a child under a different key from the hook would find no common token and file every kept memory as `ignored` — a confident report of total silence from a store that was fine. The REPORT (part 3, `report.go`) reads those rows back and prints figures an operator can act on, and its whole shape exists because a wrong number here looks exactly like a right and unflattering one. PER SOURCE AND NEVER POOLED is structural, not documented: `Report` has no project-wide `Used`/`Kept` field and no method that computes one, `Report.Pooled() *int` returns nil ALWAYS and says why, and `BuildReport` refuses an empty `ProjectID` so the store-wide scope cannot arrive at the type at all — a caller wanting one number over everything has a method to call and that method cannot answer.
