The one permitted combining direction is `MergeProjects`, which pools PROJECTS within a source and hands back a `Report` with an empty `ProjectID` so it cannot be printed as a per-project one; the arithmetic is SUM-of-counts and never an average of percentages, because a project with one verdict would count as much as one with three hundred. `Precision()` is `used / SCORED` and returns `(percent, ok)` so a caller MUST ask: a 0% over a denominator of nothing is a claim, and `Scored` rather than `Kept` is the denominator because dividing by admitted-but-unjudged memories would make precision DROP as an audit gets more complete, which is backwards — a fresh install with searches and no lifecycle run reports `no verdict recorded yet` instead. There are THREE shapes and only the third is a measurement: no rows (`Calls == 0`), calls with no verdict (`Precision` not ok), and figures; a line of zeroes would make all three read as a precision the report does not have. Every source in `KnownSources` (from `assemble.Source`, a copy would drift) is reported even when empty, because before #850's passive injections write their records `session_start` and `project_context` have no rows on ANY store and a report built from the rows present would be silent about them — and unrecognised sources found in rows are reported TOO, after the known ones, because a closed list must not be able to hide rows. The undetectable half of "missed" prints as NOT MEASURED in those words and is never 0: no heuristic can tell a fact the agent re-derived in-session from one it worked out. `--since` filters BOTH tables in Go over `recorded_at` (`RetrievalAudits` has no time filter), an unreadable stamp is KEPT rather than dropped, and the two tables are never JOINED by `record_rowid` (#852's orphan rowid reuse after a history purge would attribute a verdict to the wrong call; both carry `source` themselves). The two columns are TWO INSTANTS, not one — `retrieval_record` is stamped when the call happened and `retrieval_audit` when the detached run judged it, so one window over both is two populations and a call at 23:50 judged at 00:05 splits across any boundary. The verdict half is therefore intersected with the rowids of the calls the report COUNTS (an existence check, not an attribution join), and a verdict naming any other rowid is counted and named as **detached** rather than dropped in silence — the call cap evicts at 5000 rows while the verdict cap holds 50000, so verdicts outlive their calls and `Scored` would otherwise exceed `Kept` permanently. A verdict with `record_rowid = 0` names no call at all and is counted and named as **unattributed**, which is the only other way the numerator can outrun the denominator. Output is ids and counts: contradicted memories by id through `assemble.Token`, grouped under their source, with no content and no query text reachable because the rows hold none.
The REPORT (part 3, `report.go`) reads those rows back and prints figures an operator can act on, and its whole shape exists because a wrong number here looks exactly like a right and unflattering one. PER SOURCE AND NEVER POOLED is structural, not documented: `Report` has no project-wide `Used`/`Kept` field and no method that computes one, `Report.Pooled() *int` returns nil ALWAYS and says why, and `BuildReport` refuses an empty `ProjectID` so the store-wide scope cannot arrive at the type at all — a caller wanting one number over everything has a method to call and that method cannot answer. The ONE combining direction is `BuildStoreReport` (see the STORE-WIDE reading below), which pools PROJECTS within a source and hands back a `Report` with an empty `ProjectID` so it cannot be printed as a per-project one; the arithmetic is SUM-of-counts and never an average of percentages, because a project with one verdict would count as much as one with three hundred. There is deliberately NO exported summing function over `[]Report`: the per-project sum the store-wide reader is tested against is spelled out in the test, because a shipped combiner with no caller is how the next reader concludes it is the sanctioned way to pool. That is now ENFORCED rather than promised — the guard is `TestSourceReportHasNoExportedCombiner`, which parses `report.go` and rejects any exported `SourceReport` method with a pointer receiver (every read on that type has a value receiver, so a pointer receiver exists to mutate a per-source figure) or a `SourceReport` parameter, and any exported function taking two `SourceReport`s or a `[]SourceReport` — because a claim about an API cannot fail a build. `SourceReport.AddInto` shipped for one review round under a docstring calling itself "the ONE permitted direction" while adding the two sources it had not checked were the same, and the guard's failure message prints the docstring's first line because THE CLAIM WAS THE FAILURE. `Precision()` is `used / SCORED` and returns `(percent, ok)` so a caller MUST ask: a 0% over a denominator of nothing is a claim, and `Scored` rather than `Kept` is the denominator because dividing by admitted-but-unjudged memories would make precision DROP as an audit gets more complete, which is backwards — a fresh install with searches and no lifecycle run reports `no verdict recorded yet` instead. There are THREE shapes and only the third is a measurement: no rows (`Calls == 0`), calls with no verdict (`Precision` not ok), and figures; a line of zeroes would make all three read as a precision the report does not have. Every source in `KnownSources` (from `assemble.Source`, a copy would drift) is reported even when empty, because a project that has only ever searched records no `session_start` or `project_context` call at all and a report built from the rows present would be silent about them — and unrecognised sources found in rows are reported TOO, after the known ones, because a closed list must not be able to hide rows. The undetectable half of "missed" prints as NOT MEASURED in those words and is never 0: no heuristic can tell a fact the agent re-derived in-session from one it worked out. The DETECTABLE half is the per-source empty-call figure, and it is NAMED after the source that ran it — a search's renders as "kept nothing" and every other source's as "admitted nothing" (`keptNothingName`, shared by `report.go`'s two renderers and `run.go`'s lifecycle summary) — because both renderers are followed by a sentence saying that a search's is the detectable half of "missed", so one name for both put an injection's silence under a count of failed lookups, and an injection admitting nothing is what a healthy session start looks like. The COUNT is unchanged; the name is what says which question it answers. The count is per source and never pooled for the same reason every other figure here is not pooled. `--since` filters BOTH tables in Go over `recorded_at` (`RetrievalAudits` has no time filter), an unreadable stamp is KEPT rather than dropped, and the two tables are never JOINED by `record_rowid` (#852's orphan rowid reuse after a history purge would attribute a verdict to the wrong call; both carry `source` themselves). The two columns are TWO INSTANTS, not one — `retrieval_record` is stamped when the call happened and `retrieval_audit` when the detached run judged it, so one window over both is two populations and a call at 23:50 judged at 00:05 splits across any boundary. The verdict half is therefore intersected with the rowids of the calls the report COUNTS (an existence check, not an attribution join), and a verdict naming any other rowid is counted and named as **detached** rather than dropped in silence — the call cap evicts at 5000 rows while the verdict cap holds 50000, so verdicts outlive their calls and `Scored` would otherwise exceed `Kept` permanently. A verdict with `record_rowid = 0` names no call at all and is counted and named as **unattributed** — counted and named but in NO figure, because precision is a ratio over (call, memory) pairs and a verdict with no call has no pair to belong to; a numerator and a denominator from two populations under one name is how this report could report a precision its own numerator was not part of. So the printed precision is never a ratio of two populations, and `Scored` never exceeds `Kept`. Output is ids and counts: contradicted memories by id through `assemble.Token`, grouped under their source, with no content and no query text reachable because the rows hold none. **A STORED ID IS CARRIED WHOLE AND NEVER ROUND-TRIPPED THROUGH A DELIMITER**, in this report and in the store-wide reading below: a memory id is arbitrary stored text, not a 32-hex shape — `retrieval_audit.memory_id` carries no shape CHECK on purpose, `memref.FullIDLen` is a boundary between two honest sentences rather than a test, and `CheckImportedID` refuses only length, control characters, whitespace, a backtick and «», so `ghost import` puts a comma inside one routinely. The contradicted ids are therefore SELECTED AS ROWS (`SELECT DISTINCT source, memory_id`) rather than pasted into a `GROUP_CONCAT` column and cut apart again on a separator somebody believed absent from the data: the old shape printed a CORRECT count beside a list of ids naming rows that do not exist, which is the wrong-but-plausible output this package exists to refuse, and a count that is right is what let it survive. The listing pastes its attribution and its window from the SAME helpers the aggregate uses, so a memory is named only under a figure that counts it and only for a period the figures describe, and it runs only when the aggregate found a contradiction to name (`TestRetrievalSourceTotalsKeepsAContradictedIDHoldingAComma`, `TestRetrievalSourceTotalsNamesOnlyTheContradictedIDsItCounted`, `TestRetrievalSourceTotalsWindowsTheIDsItNames`). The STORE-WIDE reading of the same figures (`memory.RetrievalSourceTotals`, assembled by `audit.BuildStoreReport`, rendered by `ghost_health`) is ONE GROUP BY per table rather than `BuildReport` per project summed in Go, and the reason is cost, not taste: `OpenDB` pins the pool at one connection, so the per-project loop made a health check cost a number of full passes over both tables proportional to the number of checkouts on the machine — the tool an agent calls exactly when something feels wrong. Its output is bounded by the CLOSED vocabularies (sources × outcomes × degradation reasons), not by the table sizes, and `TestBuildStoreReportIsTheSumOfThePerProjectReports` asserts it equals the per-project reports field for field and line for line, because two implementations of one figure is how the degraded note's denominator came to disagree with the health block's. The block says its scope is STORE-WIDE on its face and names `ghost context --audit --project` beside it, because every other number in `ghost_health` is store-wide and a per-source line under them otherwise reads as a statement about the project the agent is working in.
**A CLOSED VOCABULARY IS DECLARED ONCE, AT ITS COLUMN, AND EVERY READER OF THAT COLUMN MATCHES THE SPELLING ITS WRITER USES.** `retrieval_audit.outcome`'s four buckets are declared in `internal/memory` as `VerdictOutcomeUsed`/`Ignored`/`Superseded`/`Contradicted` — AT THE COLUMN, because `internal/audit` imports `internal/memory` (its report reads a `memory.Store`) and not the other way round, so that is the only direction two packages can share a declaration in. `internal/audit` derives its typed `Outcome` constants from them and lists them in `AllOutcomes`; `RetrievalSourceTotals` switches on the same constants, and `retrievalContradictedIDs` BOUNDS `contradicted` as a query argument rather than pasting it into the statement (a pasted spelling there disagrees silently: no rows come back, so the report names no contradicted memory while still counting the verdicts). The bug this rule exists for cost a release: the store-wide aggregate's switch matched `case "superseded"` while the comparison stored `superseded_in_session`, so every superseded verdict was counted in `Scored` and in NO bucket — `ghost_health` printed "0 superseded in session" beside a precision whose denominator included them, the four buckets stopped summing to `Scored`, and `BuildStoreReport` stopped equalling the sum of the per-project reports. A spelling is not tidiness here: NO test could have caught it while every fixture filed its superseded verdicts by hand around the reader, because a hand-typed bucket in a fixture is itself a second spelling. The fixtures now reach the bucket through `VerdictOutcomeSuperseded` (`TestRetrievalSourceTotalsCountsEveryShapeTheTablesHold` carries a verdict in EVERY bucket), and two guards hold the list itself. `TestTheStoreWideAggregateMapsEveryOutcomeTheComparerCanStore` drives the store-wide aggregate with each value in `AllOutcomes` and requires it in exactly one bucket, with the four buckets summing to `Scored` and the contradicted case naming its id — Go cannot enumerate constants, so a new bucket added without an arm otherwise fails in a store. `TestTheOutcomeVocabularyIsOneListSpelledOnce` parses `compare.go` and holds `AllOutcomes` and `memory.AllVerdictOutcomes` to it in BOTH directions and in ORDER (a set comparison passes a list that dropped a bucket the const block still declares), and it fails if a constant in `compare.go` carries a string literal instead of NAMING the `internal/memory` constant — so the derivation the comments claim is checkable, and re-spelling a bucket here is a test failure even when the re-spelling happens to be right. The tolerance is ONE-DIRECTIONAL and deliberate: an outcome this build does NOT know — a stranger store's row, or a bucket added by a later Ghost — is counted in `Scored` and in no bucket, because the row IS a verdict and dropping it would report that store's precision as better than it is; a gap over OUR OWN vocabulary is a bug, not a tolerance, and the two are told apart by which list the value came from.
**A FLAG IS APPEND-ONLY EVIDENCE, NOT AN ACTION, AND ITS REASON NEVER LEAVES THE STORE.** `memory_flags` (schema v23) holds one agent's objection to ONE memory: `kind` is a closed two-word vocabulary (`wrong`/`stale`, CHECKed at its column the way `retrieval_audit.outcome` is), `content_hash` stamps the text the objection was about so a rewrite withdraws it while a retag leaves it standing, and the write refuses an unknown id, a memory in another project, a third kind, an empty or over-long reason (500 RUNES — refused, never clamped, since a truncated reason is a claim nobody made) and anything `rejectSecret` refuses. Nothing here resolves, deletes, demotes or re-ranks: the flag reaches `resolve` and `reflect` ONLY as a `Flagged` count in `UsefulnessEvidence`'s `Line()`, the reason column is read by no prompt, no ranking and no evidence line, and `ghost_memory_flag`'s answer carries a count and an id rather than echoing the reason it was given. The count joins `resolve.KeepStamp`, so a flag filed after a KEEP re-asks that cached KEEP exactly once. A memory's flags go with the memory — purge, retention eviction, consolidation and project delete, in the same transaction, the way `retrieval_audit` rows go.

- `internal/mcpserver/` — MCP server: 23 tools + 4 resources + 2 prompts (`recall_project`, `record_decision`). The project-context MEMORY ROWS are `assemble.Run`'s (`projectContextBudget` + `assembleProjectContext`, `project_context.go`): the tool, the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt each get one passive assembly at their own cap, and the resource's `## Global` section is a SECOND REQUEST rather than a second slice, because a project and `_global` that overlap are one cap under one section and the `seen` filter between them is a SECTION BOUNDARY — it runs after the cap, and moving it into the store would admit 15 new rows where the shipped code admitted 15 rows of which some were repeats (`SlicePolicy.ExcludeSeen` stays unread for that reason). The empty block is gated on the VERDICT, and the gate is the interesting part: the census ("nothing has been saved for it") is true only of an EMPTY window (`ReasonNoMemories`), because a `LIMIT limit*2` read never counted the store, while any reason meaning rows were FOUND and withheld renders the assembler's own abstention sentence and points at `ghost_memories_list` — without that a project whose every memory has retired is reported as never having been saved for. AN UNRESOLVED project name is the same class of problem one step earlier: `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with `""`, and the old loader was handed it and read `project_id = '' OR project_id = '_global'`, so the block MISCELLED the global rows under a `## Memories` heading for a project that does not exist and fell through to the census only when the store held no globals. The assembler refuses a project context with no project, so all three surfaces now SKIP the project-keyed reads and render the `## Global (applies to all projects)` section alone — it does not depend on a project, and the base ref delivered those rows (under the wrong heading, which is the mislabelling being removed, but delivered) — then APPEND `projectNotRegistered(asked)`. The APPEND rather than a replacement is load-bearing: returning the sentence alone drops the cross-project preferences on a project's first session, which is exactly when they matter and exactly what the server's own SessionStart instructions tell the agent to look for. The `as_of` branch is the ONE case that REFUSES instead, and the asymmetry is the point rather than an inconsistency: a caller who asked for an instant must not be handed the present, and there is no set to show, so the answer names the instant instead — and the store's `asOfCapableStore` assertion is made BEFORE that case, or an unregistered name would report success on a store that cannot read history at all. `asked` is the RAW name, never the resolved id, which is `""` here. One function produces that sentence for the three project-context surfaces (`projectNotRegistered`) and ONE writes the Global section (`projectContextGlobalSection`, which now writes into the caller's builder rather than returning a string, so the heading and the blank-line separation are `projectContextSection`'s job alone). That section is not only the second request: it is also where a RESOLVED project's window hands over its `_global` half, because `projectContextSplit` partitions the admitted rows by their own `ProjectID` before either surface renders them (#809). The split is a partition and not a selection — nothing is added, dropped or re-sorted by it, and on the TOOL `limit` still caps the whole block, because the tool's Global section is the window's own `_global` half with no second read. The RESOURCE is the one that reads past its window: its section is that half plus a second `_global`-only read capped at 15 with the window's rows filtered out of it, so the section is not capped at 15 and the block's memory rows are bounded at 20 + 15 — and it exists because the SessionStart instructions key their trust guidance on the `## Global (applies to all projects)` heading by name, so a cross-project row printed under `## Memories` lost the one thing that guidance points at. It is keyed on the row's own project rather than on a comparison against the requested one, which is what makes `project_id: "_global"` fall out as an empty own-half and one Global section — and that path SKIPS the second read rather than the section, because the pre-existing `projectID != memory.GlobalProjectID` guard, written to stop a bucket being counted as a project, also discarded the window's own rows and answered a full store with the never-saved census. An EMPTY `_global` window is a fourth case: `projectContextOwnRowsNote` refuses that id, so every surface that reads the bucket answers from the assembler's VERDICT — the abstention when rows were withheld, and a census OF the cross-project rows when the window was empty — and none of them answers the project census, which is a claim about a project. `ghost://memories/global` joined that set when it moved onto the assembler, and no count is involved on its path because `projectContextGlobalBudget` sets no `IncludeGlobal`: the bucket IS the population, so there is no second population for the verdict to be wrong about. Its census keeps its OWN wording rather than the block's — "No global memories saved yet. Use ghost_save_global…" — because it is the surface an agent is told to write to, so the empty answer is where it is told where; both wordings are gated on the same verdict, which is the part that has to agree. Both surfaces, because fixing one and forgetting the other is how the defect survived a round: the tool's own empty branch asks `projectExists`, which is `true` for a bucket, so it shipped the "is registered but has no memories" census over the same store the resource now describes correctly. `projectContextEmptyNote`'s `ghost_memories_list` pointer is CORRECT on this path and is asserted, since the tool resolves `_global`; the clause that would be false is the "Ghost holds N memories for this project" one, which is the one `projectContextOwnRowsNote` builds and this function does not. The `as_of` branch reached the same union through `MemoriesAsOf`'s `ProjectScoped` read, so it splits too — `splitMemoriesByProject` is the same predicate written against `memory.AsOfRow`, because `assemble.Run` does not produce `memory.Memory` and `MemoriesAsOf` does not produce `assemble.Item`. `validateTags` REFUSES a control character, a backtick or a `«»` in a tag on all four tools that write one, naming the position and the tag through `assemble.Token`, and trims the count and the length rather than refusing either — a tag is a label, so a shortened one names a different label and not a different row. That refusal is on the WRITE path on purpose: the same class on `ghost import` put the memory in danger of falling out of every `ghost export`, because the exporter applies the importer's own checks, and nothing on the write path had refused such a tag for the whole life of the feature. A tag is therefore carried byte for byte by import and export, and `assemble.TagsLabel` is what makes a stored one safe to read. `ghost_memories_list` resolves the same way and had the identical `Project ""` wording, and its read was never guarded — `ListMemories` widens to `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'` whenever a category or retention filter is present, so a FILTERED browse of an unknown project returned the global rows. Both are fixed on their own branch ([#781](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/pull/781)) rather than here: a different tool on a different code path, and not a consequence of this migration. A PASSIVE `Result.Abstention` makes no promise about a note, because it is bytes a CALLER renders and the callers do not agree on what follows it: the search surface writes `assemblerNotes(res.Notes)` after the sentence so "the note below breaks the removals down per stage" is true there, while `ghost_project_context`, the project-context resource and `recall_project` return the abstention as the ENTIRE answer and render no notes. The assembler does not own the rendering, so the promise is dropped from the passive half only; the search half keeps it, and the clause that carries the reason without the notes is inside the sentence ("withheld as out of date, their validity windows having closed or not yet opened"). The project-context census is gated on the PROJECT'S OWN ROWS, not on the block: `projectContextBudget` sets `IncludeGlobal`, so the block is populated by `_global` whenever the store holds any — and `cmd/ghost/bootstrap.go` seeds the global memories on every real store — and a block that admits no row of the requested project says so (`projectContextOwnRowsNote`: how many the project holds, that none is above, and where to browse them). The count is `CountMemories`, which covers rows left out for ANY reason — validity, the cap, deduplication, resolution — so the sentence names no cause. The population split has to live at the caller, because with one bucket holding two populations nothing above it can tell them apart: the live global is an admitted item, so the outcome is `answerable` and the reason is empty even when every row of the project was withheld. And that caller's gate is the ADMITTED ROWS, never the rendered text: `## Learned Context` is a direct read of `ghost_state` and not a memory row, so a project reflection has already summarised has a full block and an EMPTY read even when every memory of its own has been withheld (#788) — `## Recent Decisions` is that shape only once its companion memory is gone, since `RecordDecision` inserts a `decision_log` row in the same transaction. An empty item set therefore consults the project-scoped count, because the VERDICT describes the union window rather than the project: an empty item set with an exclusion reason can be describing `_global` rows, and a sentence read as a claim about the project needs a project-scoped fact behind it. THE COUNT IS THE SENTENCE'S INPUT, NOT A PERMISSION, and ALL THREE GATES GO THROUGH THE ONE FUNCTION THAT OWNS THE CHOICE (`projectContextOwnRowsNote`, which reads the count itself) — the two empty-block branches answer on the same union verdict and needed the same gate, and a gate a caller can pass without obeying is the failure this arrangement exists to prevent: a version read the count, was satisfied, and then rendered a different and false sentence for a project whose every row `ghost resolve` had withdrawn (the passive window binds `resolved_at IS NULL` and `CountMemories` does not, so the count is one and the window is empty, no stage withheld anything, and the abstention is false). TWO COUNTS, because the two sentences are claims about different populations: `CountMemories` (no `resolved_at` predicate) is what the project HOLDS and names no cause, while `projectWindowRowCount` (`CountActiveMemories`, a `windowCountCapableStore` assertion binding `resolved_at IS NULL` the way `passiveFetchSQL` does) is what a WINDOW could admit, and only the abstention — which names a cause, and so is a claim about which rows that cause explains — may be rendered when it is above zero. A project whose every row `ghost resolve` has withdrawn reports one to the first count and zero to the second, and the second is what stops it being told its rows "were withheld as out of date" on the strength of aged-out rows that belong to `_global`. A count that errors is no sentence at all, because an unreadable count is evidence that the project holds rows neither way. A browse that resolves a project NAME that matches no `projects` row is answered with the not-registered sentence and reads NOTHING: `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with `("", "", nil)`, so the handler keeps the RAW name for the message (formatting the resolved id produced `Project "" is not registered`, naming nothing the caller can act on) and SKIPS the `ListMemories` call. Skipping it is not tidiness — `ListMemories` widens to `(project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global')` whenever a category or retention filter is present, so a FILTERED browse of a project Ghost has never heard of returned the `_global` rows, and the not-registered sentence was unreachable for that call because the answer was never empty. The bound is exactly the cross-project rows: the widened clause binds `project_id = ''` — `ResolveProject` answers an unknown name with the EMPTY string — so only the `_global` disjunct can match, and a named project's own rows are never in the answer (an UNFILTERED read does not widen, which is why only the filtered case leaked). An unresolved project browses NOTHING rather than showing the cross-project rows, and that is a deliberate difference from `ghost_project_context`, which DOES append them for the same unresolved name: a browse is "this project's memories, optionally filtered" and a project with none has an empty answer, while a context block is what the SessionStart instructions send an agent to precisely when the directory matched nothing. Whether `ListMemories` should widen at all is a question about its own scope and is not settled here. `ghost_memory_search` sets `assemble.Request.Record` through a `retrievalCapableStore` capability assertion (#646) and a session id taken from `sessionIDFor(req)`, so the row is built in ONE place (the assembler) while only the handler knows which session the call arrived on; the assertion is `, ok` rather than a requirement, so a provider that cannot be audited still answers searches — a gap in a report is a smaller problem than a failed call, and the assembler treats a nil sink as "record nothing". THE TWO PASSIVE SURFACES RECORD TOO (#850), and for the first two releases only the search wrote a row, so every per-source denominator in the audit was a statement about searches and the `session_start` and `project_context` columns could only ever be zero. `ghost://memories/global` JOINED the project-context side of that when it moved onto the assembler (#581), which is a behaviour change rather than a wiring detail and is the reason it is written here: that resource used to read `_global` through `Store.GetTopMemories`, which ranked and trimmed in SQL and recorded NOTHING, so an agent reading the cross-project memories appended no row at all. It now owes exactly ONE row per read, under `source=project_context`, `project_id=_global` and an empty `query_hash` — a listing carried no question, and a digest of `""` would be the same constant on every read — pinned by `TestTheGlobalMemoriesResourceRecordsItsRead`. The attribution is `_global` rather than any requesting project because the row is a statement about the bucket it READ, which is the same reason the project-context resource attributes its Global section's own read to `_global` rather than to the project the call was about. The row carries verdicts and ids and never memory text, so recording a read of a global memory does not put that memory into every backup: `TestTheGlobalMemoriesResourceRecordCarriesNoQueryOrMemoryText` scans the raw bytes of every column for the rendered content, as a substring, because equality would miss a leak stored inside a longer value. `assembleProjectContext` sets the same two fields inside ITSELF rather than at its call sites, because the tool, the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt all reach the assembler through it, and a sink wired at the two callers would have recorded the tool and left a resource read unaudited — a resource read is a retrieval an agent acted on exactly as much as a tool call is. It records one row per READ and not one per block, which is why the tool owes ONE (its `## Global` section is the `_global` half of its own union window, not a second read) and the resource owes TWO (its section caps are per-section, so the second read is real). The second row is attributed to `_global` — the bucket it READ — and never to the requesting project, or that project's precision would be computed over a window holding none of its rows. Neither passive surface sets `SuppressRecordWhenLegsFailed`, which reads the OPPOSITE way from the search handler's choice for one reason: that handler CONVERTS a leg failure with nothing admitted into an ERROR, so the call never reached its caller as an answer, while a passive surface always answers. `ghost_memory_search` KEEPS `ReadOnlyHint: true` even though that path now writes, and the reasoning is at the annotation: the annotation is a claim a CLIENT acts on — auto-approve or ask the user — so it is about the effect a user would want to confirm, and a search confirms nothing (no memory created, nothing deleted, no answer changed); the row it appends is bookkeeping about the call, observable by no other tool. The search's session id is read through `sessionIDFor` and NOT through `provenanceFor`, because that function's other half is harness detection — `detectCallingSource` walks /proc on Linux and SPAWNS `ps` on darwin — and putting a process walk on every search to obtain a value that is `""` over the stdio transport Ghost ships is not a cost worth paying for a column nothing reads yet. Every id a tool prints goes through `assemble.Token` and every free-text field through `quoteData`, with `dataDelimiterNote` once per block and never on a row listing; a new listing tool or resource must use them. `shortID` abbreviates a well-formed id and shows a quoted one WHOLE, because eight runes of an escape is nothing a reader can act on. `ghost_health`'s RETRIEVAL block is the one place per-source figures reach an agent, and it is a block inside the existing tool rather than a 24th one: an agent debugging "search returns things I did not use" fetches `ghost_health` and never opens a terminal, so figures that live only in `ghost context --audit` are read exactly when nobody is measuring anything. It asserts on the concrete store (`s.store.(*memory.Store)`) because `audit.BuildReport`'s two readers are not on `provider.MemoryStore`, and it reads only — `writeRetrievalAuditBlock` writes nothing, which is what keeps this off the write-refusal path the store-version line is there to explain. It pools PROJECTS within a source through the ONE permitted `audit.MergeProjects`, and its per-source lines are `SourceReport.Summary()` rather than a second renderer, so the compact shape and the report shape cannot disagree. It goes LAST in the output because every line above it is already something an agent or a script reads by name. The `"ignored" is not a score` sentence is printed only once SOME source has a scored verdict and WITHOUT the `⚠` this tool reserves for trouble: a caveat attached to no figure is noise, and a fresh store carrying a warning glyph is how an agent learns to skip the warnings that matter (`TestHealthOnAStoreWithNoHistorySaysSo` holds that). `TestHealthRetrievalBlockIsAdditive` asserts the tool count is still 23, which is the number four documents state in prose. `TestTheListingToolsRenderIdsThroughTheSafeToken`, `TestTheProjectListingsRenderANameRawIntoItsOwnLine`, `TestShortIDRendersAHostileIDWholeRatherThanAFragment` and `TestProjectContextQuotesItsFreeTextSurfaces` pin it.
- `internal/hostevent/` — Normalized host-event contract (v1) and the transcript scanners; every parse or validation failure is an error the caller turns into "allow the stop", and a payload past `maxPayloadBytes` is one of them (nothing else on the path bounded it — the hook read stdin with an unbounded `io.ReadAll`, and `Parse` retains the payload decoded and again verbatim in `Raw`). The hook now reads through `hostevent.ReadPayload`, which stops one byte past the ceiling, so the bound limits the allocation and not only the parse. Field values are text the host did not write, so only the contract's own fields decide the envelope: an unknown field cannot become a known one, an instruction-shaped `hook_event_name` normalizes to nothing, and the goose native aliases (`event`, `working_dir`) bind only for `--source goose` and only per field where the dialect field is absent. `GhostSaves` counts STRUCTURE, never prose — a save tool named inside a text part, a shell command or an opencode Code Mode `execute` body is not a save, because that count is the save-nudge's evidence. `ScanAudit` is a SECOND registry beside the save-nudge's, and it has to be separate: the two want different things from the same transcript and only one of them can be right for a given host — the nudge must not read a tool's arguments (or prose naming a save tool would block a stop) and the audit must not skip a tool's output (or every use would be invisible), so bundling them into one struct would make every host's shape a compromise of both. `TestScanAuditRegistryCoversEveryScanFormat` is what keeps the two in step, because a format added to one and not the other would silently disable the audit, or the nudge, for that host's users. An unregistered format returns ok=false and NEVER a signals value, so a caller cannot persist a verdict derived from nothing; a PARTIAL read returns the signals it did read marked degraded, because the lines that were read are real evidence and discarding them would turn a partial answer into none. The token hasher is a PARAMETER of every scanner rather than something they build: a scanner handed no hasher would produce a Signals with no tokens, which is `Empty`, and the caller would read that as "this transcript held no evidence" rather than as "no key" (`TestAScanWithNoKeyProducesNoTokens`).
- `internal/mcpinit/` — `ghost mcp init`, `ghost mcp status`, `ghost hook <event> --source <host>` (contract-v1 lifecycle dispatch; the session-start block's MEMORY ROWS are `assemble.Run`'s (`loadSessionPassive`, `session_passive.go`) and its bucket policies are ONE statement, `sessionPassiveBudget`, because the project bucket and `_global` genuinely disagree and two copies of a policy is how they drifted; the project slice is CONDITIONAL on a project matching and the global slice is not, which is the case the old shape got for free — the global loader ran above the project resolution, so an unrecognised directory still rendered a Global section. THE BLOCK ALSO RECORDS (#850): `loadSessionPassive` takes an `assemble.RecordSink` and `loadSessionContext` opens one per resolved session through `sessionRecordSink`, because the block a session is OPENED with is a retrieval like any other and the audit's per-source denominator had a `session_start` column that could only ever be zero. That handle is a SECOND one and has to be: the reads above run through `memory.OpenReadDB`, whose DSN is `mode=ro`, so wiring the record to the store already in hand would compile, satisfy `RecordSink`, and fail on every session — with the failure swallowed by the very fail-open contract the record is written under, which is the worst combination available (every behavioural test stays green and the audit is simply never written; `TestTheSessionStoreCarriesTheRecordToo` is the source assertion that pins the difference). It is `bumpSessionCount`'s construction — `rwDSN`, the `os.Stat` guard so a missing database is never CREATED by the write meant to describe it, `TightenPermissions` before and after — but it builds through `sessionStore` rather than `memory.OpenDB`, because `OpenDB` is the constructor that MIGRATES and a hook must not migrate a store behind a live server's back; #746 is then free, since the write runs `Store.RecordRetrieval` and that checks the store version inside the record transaction. An UNMATCHED directory records NOTHING, and says nothing either: `RecordRetrieval` refuses an empty project id, and neither `_global` nor a placeholder is an honest attribution — a store that cannot record must not print a refusal on every session a user opens in a directory Ghost has never seen. `SessionID` stays empty here and on the search path alike, because over stdio — the transport Ghost ships — it is `""` for both; `source` is the exact discriminator. Everything else the digest carries — the learned summary, the open tasks, the active decisions and the interaction count — is still a direct read, because the assembler's `Item` has no field for any of it; the two TOTAL counts are not a read at all, `sessionTally` counting them from the trace of the retrieval the block was just assembled from plus one `Store.PassiveEligibleCount` per bucket (same `passiveWhere` predicates as the window's fetch, so a withheld row is never counted twice and the rows beyond the over-fetch are attributed to the ranking), so "of M total" is the store's eligible rows and never the over-fetch window's size; the two `COUNT(*)`s this package used to run disagreed with the window, and a header built from one while the rows came from the other is exactly how a block ended up calling rows "ranked out" that a stage had withheld. The all-withheld sentence is `assemble.EmptyNote` (the same function `ghost_project_context` prints) and `BucketTally.WithheldNote`, which asks `abstention` for the cause rather than keeping a sentence table of its own ([#897](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/897)). The per-item PREVIEW budget stays with the renderer rather than riding on `Slice.ClampBytes`: a preview has to SAY it was cut, `ClampBytes` cannot append an ellipsis, and a caller cannot tell a clamped row from one that happened to be exactly its budget, which is the distinction the ellipsis needs; the stored row is untouched either way. Every query this package still issues against `memories` is a `COUNT(*)`, asserted by a source scan — there is none since #897, and the scan stays for what a count names no row, while a row projection is a choice about which memories a session is shown, and a choice made here is a second implementation of the assembler's stages (the scope and tier schema-version probes that lived here for two releases are gone with the fetch they guarded, because `internal/memory` owns those substitutions in `passiveColumnsFor` and the store reads the version through its read SNAPSHOT); installers: claude-code, opencode plugin, codex TOML+hooks.json, goose Agent-Plugins package; goose field aliasing lives in hostevent.Parse). Every write to a file the user owns — settings.json, codex config.toml and hooks.json, the opencode plugin, the goose package, the MEMORY.md redirect — goes through `writeFileAtomic`/`writeFileAtomicPrivate` in settings.go, never a bare os.WriteFile: temp file in the same dir, flushed (data and mode) with `f.Sync()`, then rename, then the parent directory flushed, so an interrupted write cannot truncate a config, a reader holding it open still sees a whole document, and a power cut can leave neither an empty config nor a missing one. A temp-file flush failure stops the write and is reported; a directory-flush failure is not (the rename already landed, and Windows has no directory flush at all). The rename carries the permission bits across and nothing else — not the owner, POSIX ACLs, xattrs or hardlinks, so a hardlinked config in a dotfiles repo silently diverges from its repo copy. `writeFileAtomicPrivate` additionally clamps the mode to 0600 and is used only by settings.json, because that is the ceiling every pre-refactor save enforced; the other files keep whatever mode the user chose, even where a codex `[mcp_servers.ghost.env]` sub-table can hold a credential. Ghost's own marker/scratch files are exempt. Two files get a one-time `.bak` through `writeBackupOnce`: settings.json on its first save, and codex `config.toml` the first time Ghost writes to it — the first init, which appends the table to a file the user already had, so the copy is the pre-ghost file (a repair-only backup could never be, since a repair can only find a table that is already there). Neither is ever rolled forward, so a later run cannot overwrite the only pristine copy with Ghost's own output, and a 0-byte config.toml is skipped rather than copied — an empty backup would stand forever and block the real one. It also owns the read-only stale-server scan (`ReportStaleServers`, #746), which reports running `ghost mcp` processes whose executable is unlinked or is not the binary this command is, so `ghost mcp status` and `ghost upgrade` can name the clients to restart. The session hook's `bumpSessionCount` is the ONE write into a memory store that does not go through `internal/memory`'s guarded seams — the structural test behind those seams walks that package, and this one opens its own `sql.Open` handle, as `sessionRecordSink` does for a different reason — so it reads `PRAGMA user_version` itself and skips the write when the store has moved past this build. It is checked before the write rather than inside a transaction with it because there is no transaction: the counter is best-effort by design, its failure path is `return 0`, and wrapping a row that records something about this process in a guarded transaction would change what the hook is. The skip is silent, because a line printed on every session start lands in front of an agent on every start, and `ghost_health` plus `ghost mcp status` already say it where a human is looking. Four properties are load-bearing. It NEVER signals, kills or restarts anything, because only the operator knows which client is safe to interrupt. It detects by EXECUTABLE IDENTITY (the kernel's own `(deleted)` marker on `/proc/<pid>/exe`, then `os.SameFile`) and not by a version string, because a running process's version is text inside a binary this code must not execute, and the installed path plus the deletion marker are facts the kernel already keeps. It reports a THIRD state, "could not answer", as silence on a non-Linux platform and as `checked=false`, so an unreadable tree is never rendered as an empty list that reads as "nothing is stale" — and UNKNOWN is kept distinct from a FINDING at the per-process level too: `sameFile` is three-valued, because a two-valued one cannot say "could not read", and a `ghost mcp` running from a root-owned `/opt/ghost` at 0700 has an `/proc/<pid>/exe` this user cannot stat. Rendering that as "a different binary" would tell an operator to restart a healthy client, so the process is skipped: unreported is a small loss, wrongly reported costs a session. And its WORDING is derived from the evidence each finding carries, because `installed` is `os.Executable()` — the binary running this command — and not a discovered installation, so a `go run` build, a `make build` output and a `~/bin` copy are all ordinary ways to invoke the command and all have current clients. Only `ExeDeleted` is a fact about the world and is reported as "replaced"; a plain mismatch says "a different ghost binary than this one", and nothing anywhere claims a binary "is no longer installed", which asserts an installation this code never looked for. `ghost upgrade` compares against the path it just replaced, not `os.Executable()`, which is the old image this process is still running. Its consequence is hedged ("writes may be refused") because the scan never opens the store and so cannot assert the refusal happened; `ghost_health` is where that is actually reported. The session-start block's project name goes through `assemble.Label` rather than `Token`, because a name is a label and is normally full of spaces, and it is agent-supplied: `ensureProjectFor` stores the caller's `project_id` argument as the project's name as well as its id. `TestTheSessionStartHeadingCannotForgeALine` pins the heading and asserts the spaced cases verbatim, which is what the choice was for. The retrieval audit rides this hook and is the one thing it writes: the sidecar is a temp file named `ghost-audit-*.signals` whose path is handed to the detached lifecycle child on its command line, which is why it is a `CreateTemp` name rather than one composed from a project id. What crosses the gap is IDS and KEYED TOKENS — never transcript text, query text or memory content — and the transcript side is a READ: no store, no config, nothing opened, because the hook runs synchronously and must not touch the database. It resolves the per-install key through `memory.ReadRetrievalKey` BEFORE opening the transcript, and that one failure is the single NOT-fail-open step: a scan without a key produces no tokens, so "no key" and "no evidence" would be the same answer, and the second of those gets persisted as a claim about the session (`TestNoKeyMeansNoSidecarAndStillALifecycle` — and the lifecycle chain still runs, because the audit is a passenger of phases that are worth running whether or not this turn produced evidence). Two files are cleaned up rather than left to a sweep, because both are this run's own artefacts: a sidecar whose `startLifecycleChild` failed is REMOVED (a file written for a child that never started has no reader and no expiry, `TestASidecarWrittenForAChildThatNeverStartedIsRemoved`), and the child deletes the sidecar it read ONLY after the read succeeded AND the file was named like one — `lifecycle --signals <any path>` used to delete whatever it was handed, and it is an internal subcommand, which makes it exactly as reachable as the other internal phases (`TestRunLifecycleDoesNotDeleteAFileItWasHanded`, `TestRunLifecycleStillDeletesTheSidecarItRead`).
- `internal/claudeimport/` — One-time import of Claude Code auto-memory on first contact. The files are written by a session that read a checkout Ghost never chose, so the parse layer is an untrusted surface: a front-matter line gets its own bound (`maxFrontmatterLine`) because `bufio.Scanner`'s 64 KiB default stops the scan on a long description and the block's loss is silent (the type, and with it the category, went with it), a block that could not be read whole is imported as body text rather than half-parsed, and a file past `maxMemoryFileBytes` is skipped. The import writes nothing to disk at all — a fixture asserts the tree is byte-identical afterwards — and it never promotes: `categoryMap` is the only thing that picks a category, so a planted `source: manual`, `pinned: true` or `type: _global` reaches none of the fields consolidation filters on (`source`, `pinned`, `retention` or `resolved_at`, the four `consolidatable` and `ReplaceNonManual` exclude on) — `Upsert` binds the source to the package's own constant and writes an unpinned row at the default `project` tier with no `resolved_at`, so a forged exemption buys the row nothing and a forged `type: _global` lands on `categoryMap`'s `fact` fallback instead of a scope
- `internal/linking/` — Background worker linking similar memories into a graph
- `internal/embedding/` — Ollama async vectorization worker; every vector is stamped with the identity of the space that produced it (`<model>[:<dimensions>][+prefix]`, `embedding.VectorIdentity`) and nomic-embed-text is embedded with its `search_document: `/`search_query: ` task prefixes. A LIVENESS PROBE MISSING ITS DEADLINE IS NOT AN OUTAGE, which is why the probe is three-valued rather than a bool: `Client.Probe` answers `Reachable`, `Unreachable` (a non-200, or a refused connection — an ANSWER, so nothing is listening), or `Inconclusive` (the probe's own 2s deadline expired, so we learned nothing), and `Worker.checkAlive` returns that verdict while maintaining the `ollama-down-since` marker (`ghost mcp status` reads it for outage duration). The two errors are NOT symmetric, and every call site says which one it is making, which means the two gates DISAGREE on purpose: `EmbedPending` PROCEEDS on `Inconclusive` (its callers — a one-shot `ghost supersede` and a save-driven `processProject` — have no second chance, and skipping is what made #736 report a clean `0 candidate pairs`), while `SweepOnce` skips on anything but `Reachable`, because the daemon retries in one interval and a lenient gate there walks a HUNG endpoint through every project at `2 + 2·P + 32·P_pending` seconds, blocking `Worker.Run`'s select loop and dropping save notifications past `projectCh`'s 16-deep non-blocking buffer. `EmbedPending`'s in-batch re-probe after a failed embed stops on anything but `Reachable` (a part-done batch gains nothing from another 30s request), and it RETURNS the verdict `SweepOnce` may ACT ON — the re-probe's own where a batch attempted work, a CONCLUSIVE refusal otherwise, and an `Inconclusive` over no work collapsed to "nothing to act on" — so the sweep reuses one probe and a busy machine cannot end a tick over a project with no work. Reading a timeout as "down" cost a busy machine the whole index: both the daemon's sweep AND `ghost supersede`'s own pre-scan fill (`embedSupersedeCorpus`, #716) gate on this probe, so one slow round trip left the candidate scan with no vectors to score, `Result.Unscored` holding the entire corpus, and a clean-looking `0 candidate pairs` at exit 0 for a pair the operator could see (#736). The marker answers the OPPOSITE question, because a duration is only worth having for an outage that is real: written on a conclusive `Unreachable` at once and never re-written (so a still-down endpoint does not reset its own clock) — reachable only inside the DAEMON, which is the sole caller that builds a Worker with a data dir (`embedSupersedeCorpus` passes "" on purpose: the marker is the daemon's outage bookkeeping) — AND on `Inconclusive` after `inconclusiveStampsAfter` CONSECUTIVE unanswered SWEEP TICKS — a tick counts whether or not the strict gate let the sweep past it, so a hung endpoint that skips every project still keeps the clock running (three ticks is about six minutes at the daemon's 2-minute interval) — and one slow probe is a busy machine far more often than a dead endpoint, and a marker written on one is reported for as long as the machine stays busy, while the endpoints a refusal never describes (a wedged accept loop, a remote URL behind a dropping firewall) need that duration precisely because they never produce a refusal. The streak is counted PER SWEEP TICK (`SweepOnce` -> `noteSweep`) and NEVER per probe, because `checkAlive` is called more than once per tick — `EmbedPending` re-probes after every failed embed — so a per-probe count reaches its threshold inside ONE tick and stamps the marker on exactly the busy machine the streak exists to protect against. Any answered sweep SPENDS the streak, so a stall spread over minutes cannot accumulate into an outage that was never established; a stall never *clears* a real one, and an inconclusive probe never removes the marker at all. `Client.Alive` remains the two-valued form for the two callers that only warn a human (`mcpinit`'s init preflight, `ghost_health`); a caller that gates WORK on the answer uses `Probe`, because only there does a false "down" cost a skipped corpus rather than a conservative warning
- `internal/resolve/` — `ghost resolve`: LLM-classified de-weighting of resolved-evidence memories (drops from ranked injection, stays searchable); classifies via CLI harness (see Classifier backend below); the KEEP cache is keyed by `resolve.KeepStamp` and the audit evidence is read ABOVE its gate, once per pass (#880)
- `internal/memref/` — the ONE ref-resolution implementation, now for every surface that takes a memory by name, not only the ones that CHANGE a row: `ghost supersede --withdraw`, `ghost resolve --mark` / `ghost_resolve_mark`, and `ghost history` (#720), which only READS. Two entry points and ONE set of rules: `Resolve(ctx, store, projectID, which, ref)` scopes the read to a project (its callers are about to change what they name), and `ResolveIn(ids, which, ref)` applies the same rules to an id set the CALLER collected, which is what a store-wide reader needs. The difference is which ids, never how a ref is judged. `ResolveIn` names no project in its refusals because its set is not confined to one, and `FullIDLen` is only the boundary between two honest sentences about a miss, never a test of whether a ref is a whole id. `ErrNoMatch` distinguishes the refusal that says the set holds nothing from the ones that say it holds too much, and a caller branches on it rather than on the ref's length — which cannot tell them apart. The reasoning for all of it, and for why `resolveIn` applies the prefix match itself, is in [architecture.md](docs/architecture.md#memory-history). A ref is a full id, or a PREFIX of at least 8 CHARACTERS (a RUNE count, never a byte length — the id column is TEXT and SQLite's substr() slices by character, so a byte-counted floor on a multi-byte id refuses a prefix the store really has and accepts one that is a third of an identity; the two lengths agree for the hex ids Ghost mints, which is exactly why the bug survives a normal test). The floor applies to a PREFIX ALONE and is decided by COMPARING the match with the ref, never by inspecting the ref's characters — a full id is accepted whatever its shape, because `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim and nothing about the column says they are hex. A byte-exact match wins at any length (the id is a unique key, so the spelling the caller typed is decisive, which is what lets a short full id that also prefixes a longer one resolve), then a single case-folded one, then the prefix rules. AMBIGUITY IS A REFUSAL, never a choice, and it LISTS the matches up to `MaxMatches` — with ONE exception: a ref below the floor is refused WITHOUT the match list, because printing it would turn a refusal into a dump of the id set (a store-wide set is a LARGER thing to dump, not a smaller one). Two case-folded matches and no byte-exact one (a THIRD casing of ids differing only in case) is unaddressable by any spelling, so that refusal LISTS the two stored spellings instead of naming a remedy — the reader's next keystroke is a copy of one. The rules are shared rather than copied because two copies eventually disagree about which id one spelling addresses; a guess is worse on the history path than on the change paths, where it changes a row nobody named — there it also prints one memory's whole recorded TEXT as though it were another's. NOTE THE SCOPE, because the claim is not wider than it looks: `resolve --reassess --only` does NOT route here. `internal/resolve/scope.go`'s `Scope.Select` has its own rules, and it HAS to differ on one point — a selector matching nothing is a REPORTED MISS that lets the rest of the scope still be judged (#698's scoping judgement), where a change to a named row cannot be a miss at all, so a failed ref there is a refusal of the whole request. It also measures its 8-character floor in BYTES and truncates an ambiguity listing with `id[:8]`, both of which memref fixes, and unifying the two is a separate change worth doing on its own. It is worth knowing they are on the same round trip: `--mark`'s own follow-up hands the operator a `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <ids>` command
- `internal/supersede/` — `ghost supersede`: LLM-classified 'supersedes'/'causes' link creation over live memories, batched 8 candidate pairs per harness call with a content-keyed NEITHER cache (`supersede_checked`, schema v8, cascading FKs) so a converged project's fresh candidates make zero calls — except reversed ones, which are never cached and are re-asked every pass — a live edge whose endpoints HAVE moved is judged every pass so it keeps self-healing, and one whose endpoints have not is held quiet by skip-if-unchanged and judged by no pass at all, WHICHER WAY THE SCAN PROPOSED IT (#787: the orientation the scan AGREES with was the one that bypassed the test, so an unchanged live edge was re-billed and re-rolled on every pass even though that is the shape every real supersedes edge is in). The quiet is measured against the EDGE'S OWN `created_at`, and a verdict that re-confirms the edge moves that stamp (`memory.CreateLinkJudged`, #784) to the freshness of the content THAT VERDICT WAS MADE AGAINST — the later of the two endpoints' `updated_at` as the pass read them — and not to the clock, because the two are a classify call apart and an edit landing between the read and the write (reflect's consolidation rewrite, a save through a live `ghost mcp`) would otherwise be covered by a stamp written after it and the pair would sit quiet against text no verdict was given for — which is what makes the promise below true for an edge that has ever been edited, since a tag or importance edit bumps `updated_at`. `PromoteToGlobal` does NOT, and that is a decision rather than an oversight (#807): a promotion is a MOVE — the row's text, `created_at`, category and importance are all what they were — and `updated_at` is the column the two supersede readers above take to mean “the CONTENT of this row changed”. Measured, not argued: with the bump in place, promoting an endpoint of a live edge re-armed skip-if-unchanged and re-billed a pair nobody edited (a paid route to the re-rolling #779 measured), and on a CYCLE it flipped `orient` — the one shape with no stored direction to fall back on — so `ghost supersede --reassess` stood the backwards edge #641 found in the wild and withdrew the correct one, off a bookkeeping column. `TogglePin` does NOT either, and for the same reason (#820): a pin is a decision about how a memory is SURFACED, not about what it says, and the readers that act on it read `pinned` itself — the `_global` passive bucket's own order (`pinned DESC, importance DESC, updated_at DESC, id`), the composite ranking's decay exemption, and `ghost resolve`'s hold-back. The column `updated_at` means something else and two readers in another package take it to mean “the CONTENT of this row changed”. Measured the same way as the promotion: with the bump in place, pinning an endpoint of a live edge re-armed skip-if-unchanged and re-billed a pair nobody edited — and pinning is something an operator does MID-SESSION, while a stop hook's lifecycle phase is mid-flight, so this is the more ordinary of the two triggers — and on a CYCLE it flipped `orient` a second time, standing the backwards edge #641 found in the wild off a bookkeeping column. `pinMemoryTx`, the save-time `pin` on the `Upsert` path, already left the stamp alone; this brings the operator's explicit `ghost_memory_pin` into step with it. What no longer moves with a pin, named: the supersede pass's chronology and freshness test, above; `ghost prune`'s grace, whose `pruneActivitySQL` is `COALESCE(last_accessed, max(updated_at, created_at, expires_at))` — pinning a row no longer refreshes it, and `Touch` has no caller so the grace is measured from the row's last WRITE, which a pin is not; and the `updated_at` TIE-BREAK in that `_global` order, and nothing else there, because `pinned DESC` is that order's PRIMARY key and what is given up is only the ability of a pin to reorder two rows that tie on (pinned, importance). The pin is still recorded where a pin is read: `memories.pinned`, and no `memory_history` version, because that table records none of the columns this statement changes. The one reader whose view changes is `ghost prune`, which measures a row's grace from `updated_at` and so is no longer refreshed by a promotion; that is the right direction for a content-freshness column, and an operator who wants the row kept says so with a `persistent` tier or a pin. A classify call that FAILS is re-issued ONCE, after `classifyRetryDelay` (2s, injectable through `SetRetryDelay`) and counted by `Retries()` — it is on both reports and the reassess summary log — because a real `opencode run` that exited 1 once cost a whole `ghost supersede` pass in #699's rehearsal, and the rerun a minute later answered normally. ONE retry, not a schedule, and it decides nothing: the second failure is returned unchanged so a genuinely dead harness is exactly as fatal as before, `Run` still aborts and writes nothing, an UNPARSEABLE reply is not a transport failure and is not retried, and the wait is context-bounded so a cancelled run does not spend a second spawn to be told the same thing. Pairs are oriented by `updated_at` and then by `created_at` (`orient`), and a pair tying on BOTH is not proposed at all — the prompt also carries each note's `created_at` and offers a fourth verdict, `reversed`: a supersedes link only points newer→older, so a verdict saying the OLDER note is the current one is refused (never written, never cached) rather than written backwards (#641). THE UNIT THE PASS JUDGES IS THE UNORDERED PAIR, reconciled on `pairKey` across BOTH sources (#778), and that is a consequence of the harm rather than tidiness: a demotion lands on the target alone, so two 'supersedes' edges in opposite directions demote BOTH memories of one pair and neither withdraws the other. The old reconciliation keyed the ordered `{newer,older}` pair, so a live edge pointing the opposite way from the scan's own orientation for the same two rows matched nothing and both orientations were put to one classifier in a single pass — three dry runs over copies of three real projects proposed both directions of `02EA044F`/`74CE9D10`, and a verdict that cannot decline a direction wrote the CYCLE. Three rules within one pass, each a counted, named refusal on the report, none a guess: (1) a pair is classified at most once per pass, and a live edge decides its DIRECTION whichever way the scan ordered it — **and “a live edge” means an edge of EITHER relation, which is #823 and was the whole fix**: `causes` is written older→newer, so a pair holding one was reconciled by the same `pairKey` with NO direction override and its direction came from `orient`, i.e. from `updated_at`. So a `causes` edge running against the timestamps was re-proposed every pass in the flipped direction, paid a classify call for the re-ask for as long as the edge lived, and answered `causes` again — which wrote the SECOND direction of the pair. The edge's direction is the one a `reversed` verdict must be shown, since that is the wrong edge the pass and `--reassess` both have to reach, and a contradicting scan proposal is refused AS A DIRECTION and counted as one (`Result.OppositeLive`), which is deliberately a count of refused orientations and not of classifications, because the pair then faces skip-if-unchanged like any untouched live edge. `liveEdge`/`pairDirection` are where the two relations are normalised to one shape, because a shape carrying `(source, target)` would assert two different directions for one pair depending on which relation the read came from; the three answers `pairDirection` gives are three different things and not one answer with a flag (a `supersedes` CYCLE, case 1; a `supersedes` edge winning over a `causes` edge that contradicts it, case 2; a `causes` edge, or a `causes` cycle, case 3), and case 2 is not a refusal on purpose: only ONE of that pair's edges demotes, and a verdict settles both, so refusing it would hand a contradiction the ordinary pass used to clear for free to an operator. A pair a `causes` edge names is also never NEITHER-cache-skipped for the same reason one holding a `supersedes` edge is not — a cache skip is treated as a `neither` verdict, so skipping it asserts the edge is not there, which was graph-only staleness and harmless while a `causes` edge was only swept, and is not harmless now that its direction decides what the pass may write; (2) a pair the graph already claims in BOTH directions is refused outright (`Result.Bidirectional`), not judged, because no third direction exists and a SUPERSEDES would re-affirm one half of a cycle, with the report naming `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` (APPLIED, and GATED — the flagless one is a dry run and the ungated one acts on a single verdict) as the repair, since withdrawal is not this pass's job, as everywhere else here. **That refusal is `supersedes` and ONLY `supersedes`, and a `causes` cycle is judged instead of refused** — the one place the two relations differ, and the difference is the harm each does. A `supersedes` cycle demotes BOTH of the pair's memories and neither edge withdraws the other; nothing demotes on a `causes` edge. And `--reassess` loads live `supersedes`/`llm` edges and can never see a `causes` one, so refusing a `causes` cycle would leave a contradiction whose advertised repair cannot reach it — a worse state than the one the refusal prevents. Such a pair is therefore judged ONCE in the direction the timestamps give it, the only direction a pair whose two edges disagree has (`LinksByRelationSource` has no ORDER BY, so returning the first edge's direction would hand the pair's chronology to a coin flip on two hex ids), and the ordinary apply block converges it: an affirming verdict writes that direction and drops the other, a denying one drops both. It is held quiet by the edge's stamp exactly like every other linked pair, because a `causes` edge's stamp is a judgement time whatever else it is not. A pair whose ONLY live edges are `causes` — one of them or a cycle of them — and whose two rows ALSO tie on `updated_at` and `created_at` is counted as `Result.Unoriented` with everything else of that count, because there is no direction to ask “which is newer” about: a `supersedes` edge carries one and a `causes` edge does not, so a tied pair linked only by `causes` is the one live shape in the rule set that is not re-judged, which is why the report line says so. **AND A `causes` EDGE SUPPLIES A STAMP, NOT A DIRECTION — WHICH IS A TYPE, NOT A CONVENTION**: `pairDirection` returns FOUR NAMED states rather than a bool, and the two refusals are not the same refusal — a pair whose `supersedes` edges disagree is a `supersedes` cycle and is REFUSED, a pair whose `causes` edges disagree is a `causes` cycle and is JUDGED, but only when nothing else describes the pair. A pair holding a live `supersedes` edge **and** a `causes` cycle is exactly what a pre-#823 store holds (that writer shipped unguarded), and letting the `causes` half decide its direction is the #641 shape in a new costume: the live edge this pass, `--reassess` and resolve's piggyback can all reach is the `supersedes` one, so judging the pair the other way asks about an edge that is not there, and a denying verdict then invalidates `supersedes` in the TIMESTAMPS' direction — which is not where the live edge is — so nothing is withdrawn while the report says it was. The states are named because a bool made the caller re-derive which refusal it was holding, which is how that got wrong the first time.

**A `causes` EDGE'S DIRECTION MAY NOT REACH A `supersedes` VERDICT, AND THAT IS A BLOCKER RATHER THAN A NICETY (A #641 HARM THE PASS PRODUCES)**: the first cut of #823 gave every live edge its own direction, and read through a `causes` override a live `causes newer→older` edge asks “does the January note supersede the September one”, the model answers SUPERSEDES, and the SUPERSEDES branch writes `supersedes older→newer` — an edge that DEMOTES the memory that is actually current and buries it, on a pair the store's own ranking then reads as settled. So `pairDirection` returns a `pairVerdict` whose `newer`/`older` and whose `stamp` are SEPARATE FIELDS, and the two `causes` cases leave the direction EMPTY: a `causes` edge settles the STAMP — which is what skip-if-unchanged reads, so the re-bill is fixed either way — and the `livePair` entry that keeps the pair out of the NEITHER cache, and nothing about the question. The `causes` case builds its candidate from the same `orient` a fresh pair uses, which is the pre-#823 behaviour for every pair without a live `supersedes` edge, so the prompt never labels the chronologically older note NEWER for a supersedes question. A live `SUPERSEDES` edge keeps its own override, deliberately: that edge IS the demotion, so the direction to judge it in is the direction it was written in, and a REVERSED answer is how the model declines a backwards claim. The consequences are named because they are behaviour, not accidents: `Result.OppositeLive` is `supersedes`-ONLY (a `causes` edge is never proposed against, because it is never a direction), so the `proposed the reverse` report line names one relation; a `causes` verdict on a pair whose live `causes` edge disagrees with the timestamps is a CORRECTION — the verdict drops the edge it contradicts and writes its own direction, so the pair converges in the pass that settles it, and `Classified.Reclassified` counts it (`TestACausesVerdictCorrectsALiveCausesEdgeThatDisagreesWithTheTimestamps`); the same verdict on a pair whose edge already agrees moves NOTHING and is not a reclassification, which is why the report's verb reads `re-linked` when the relation is unchanged AND a row of it moved and `re-affirmed` only when nothing moved (`TestACausesVerdictReAffirmsALiveCausesEdgeThatAgreesWithTheTimestamps`); and a pair linked only by `causes` whose rows tie on both timestamps is now `Unoriented` where #823 judged it, which is the same answer a fresh tied pair gets and is why the report line has to say which live shape it is. That refusal is counted ONCE, and counting it per half was a review finding: `order` is the union of the scan's fresh candidates and the live-edge pairs, so a tied `causes`-only pair is reached by both and a bulk-imported corpus — where every pair ties by construction — printed each one twice on a line documented as a per-pair count. `Selection` therefore carries `unorientedPairs` beside `Unoriented`, and the reconciliation suppresses its own increment for a pair the scan already refused; the SCAN's set is the one kept because it is the one built for the purpose, so a tied `causes` pair the scan never reached as a near neighbour is still counted here (`TestATiedCausesOnlyPairTheScanAlsoFoundIsCountedOnce`, and both halves of the guard are mutation-checked). A live `supersedes` edge never had the double count because the `pairDirected` path never calls `orient` — the edge carries a direction, so there is nothing to refuse. **AND EVERY VERDICT OWES THE PAIR'S *OTHER* EDGE, WHICH MAKES A RE-AFFIRMATION NOT AUTOMATICALLY A NON-EVENT**: the affirming verdicts write an edge in the direction they were asked about and the guard in front of the write closes a cycle, while the DENYING verdicts write nothing — so `sweepCausesBothWays` is the only thing that can, and a sweep stopping at one direction left the pair's other `causes` edge live while `ReclassifiedNoWrite` and the row's `already gone` marker both said the pair was unlinked. A SUPERSEDES verdict therefore drops the pair's `causes` edges in BOTH directions (one is the edge a supersession implies, the other the edge that CONTRADICTS it, and `supersedes A→B` beside `causes A→B` is a contradiction rather than a redundancy because a `causes` source→target edge asserts the reverse chronology), and so does every other verdict. Each second statement is gated on `claimsHold` — the pass having READ an edge that way — so the ordinary pair pays no second write. And because a verdict can KEEP the relation the pair was judged around and still remove a row of the other one (a `causes` cycle answered CAUSES keeps the direction it was asked about and drops the edge asserting the other; a SUPERSEDES verdict re-affirming a `supersedes` edge beside a `causes` cycle drops both of the cycle's edges), `Result.Reclassified` reads `verdict != liveRelation || verdictDrops(...)[either half] > 0` rather than the first test alone, and the CLI's reclassified-row predicate carries `c.CausesDropped > 0` (and, for the dry-run shape, `c.CausesDroppable > 0`) for the same reason. Since #845 that test is narrowed once more by `movedSomething`, because the withheld half of `verdictDrops` is no longer a change: a DENYING verdict on a pair whose only live edge was the withheld `supersedes` one moves nothing, and counting it prints `1 reclassified` over an `--apply` pass that wrote no link and deleted none. The relation test still differs for such a pair, so the exclusion cannot be folded into it and is its own term — `movedSomething = dropCauses > 0` — except on a CAUSES verdict, which is exempt because the `causes` write beside the withheld withdrawal really does land, which is the difference between a pair that changed and a pair that only declined. `verdictDrops` is a PREDICTION built from the same `claimsHold` predicates the apply block's sweeps are gated on, and it is computed BEFORE the apply block because a dry run runs no block and is left with only a prediction — a count only an applied run can reach is a count the preview does not preview — and the row's two clauses are INDEPENDENT rather than alternatives in a switch, because one verdict can re-link a pair AND drop an edge and a switch that took one branch printed the re-link over a run that also deleted a row. The UNGATED reverse sweep in the `causes` branch is the one guard with no observable effect in `Result`, and it is not for that reason untested: a sweep that can only move a row the pass has already seen is worth one write and not two, so `TestTheCausesSweepIsSkippedWhereThereIsNothingToSweep` counts the invalidations the pass ISSUES through a store spy rather than the rows that moved, which is the third question where `causesTakenStore` counts what moved and the row counts what was predicted. **AND THAT REPAIR WORKS, WHICH IS A SEPARATE INVARIANT AND A SEPARATE BUG**: `Reassess` groups the live edges by the UNORDERED pair before it asks anything, so a cycle is ONE question read as a DIRECTION — `supersedes` names one of the two live edges as current, so that edge stands and its reverse is withdrawn; `reversed` names the other, so the other stands; `neither`/`causes` deny the replacement in either direction and BOTH are withdrawn. Before #780 it judged every edge as an independent candidate, so a cycle was asked twice and a direction-blind verdict confirmed both (`Confirmed 2, Withdrawn 0`) — the pass that exists to remove wrong edges kept the worst one, and the command the creation pass advertised as its repair was a promise with nothing behind it. TWO outcomes decide NOTHING and both withdraw NEITHER, because withdrawing half of a cycle on a hunch leaves a live edge the pass never judged — the state the cycle itself is a report about — and they are SEPARATE values because the operator's next step differs: `CycleNoVerdict` (a failed call, an unparseable reply — the pass asked and got nothing, so a RERUN answers it) and `CycleUnoriented` (both notes share every timestamp, the #778 tie, so there was no direction to ask about and a rerun cannot change it, so the OPERATOR decides). One sentence claiming both was a review finding: it reported the transient #699 harness failure as a missing chronology and answered it with "delete an edge". `CycleOutcome` values are bare words, never prose, because the REPORT owns the wording and only the report knows whether the run was a dry run — a constant that spelled out "was withdrawn" put a past-tense claim under a list of "would withdraw" rows. The block's PER-EDGE verbs come from `apply` and from `WithdrawnEdge.Written` (`would withdraw` / `withdrew` / `already gone` / `not reached … STILL LIVE`), never from the outcome and never from `apply` alone — a failed InvalidateLink leaves the denied edge live. The line ABOUT the pair states the decision in words true in every mode ("so that edge stands and its reverse is denied"), so it can never contradict the markers under it; `ghost_link_withdraw` is named with all THREE of its parameters (`project_id`, `source_id`, `target_id`) because the handler refuses a call missing any of them, and a fallback naming two of the three would move the dead command from the CLI to the tool surface. `VetoSupersede` is NOT applied to a cycle: it asks one orientation's question and a cycle has two over the same two bodies. Every row it withdraws carries the SAME per-row sweep arm the ordinary path uses, and that is worth its own assertion per branch: a withdrawal that DENIES the pair also drops the 'causes' edge pointing into the note it just decided is current — except on a CAUSES verdict, which affirms that relation and sweeps nothing. `InvalidateLink` writes no history row for a non-supersedes relation, so a cycle row that lost its sweep is a second deletion with nothing in `memory_history` to show it, and `TestReassessSweepsTheCausesEdgeOfEveryCycleWithdrawalItDenies` pins all four arms (SUPERSEDES, REVERSED, both NEITHER rows, and the CAUSES row that must NOT sweep) plus the dry-run PREDICTION, which comes from a different read than the apply sweep's own count.
