The report's `cycle:` block is a LIST of ids and says which edge stands, because a headline `withdrew 0` over a pair with two live edges is the one sentence this report must not leave standing alone, and an undecided cycle prints the two `ghost supersede <project> --withdraw <src> <tgt> --apply` commands (through `internal/followup.WithdrawCommand`, the one place that decides how a project name is spelled as a shell argument — the creation pass's own cycle line goes through `followup.ReassessCommand` the same way, because a command printed with a literal `<project>` is one the operator has to edit, and an edit is where a repair lands in the wrong project); (3) a fresh pair whose two rows tie on `updated_at` AND `created_at` is never proposed (`Result.Unoriented`) — a bulk import stamps a whole batch with one stamp (33 dingo rows share `2026-09-20 09:26:05`) and the only remaining "chronology" would be two hex ids, so the old id tiebreak decided the direction of real supersessions by which hash sorted higher. A live edge naming a tied pair is STILL revalidated, because it already carries a direction: the tie refuses proposing one and says nothing about judging one. `created_at` is the tiebreak and never the primary order — it mislabels a note re-saved long after it was created, which is #641. **AND A FOURTH RULE, WHICH IS ABOUT TWO PASSES RATHER THAN ONE (#806)**: all three above are decided from what THIS pass read, and `ghost supersede` is a command an operator (or the stop hook's lifecycle phase) can run twice over one project at once. Two passes that read the same unclaimed pair, scan it in OPPOSITE directions and both reach a verdict wrote BOTH directions of it — the cycle #778 found in a real store, with a second process supplying the half that needed one. Opposite orientations need an endpoint's `updated_at` to move between the two scans, which is ordinary (a tag edit through a live `ghost mcp`), and the window is a whole classify call wide, because a pass reads the graph, scans, then spends seconds to minutes before its first write. So the fix is NOT a lifecycle lock — a lock is a convention between the processes that take it, and a command run twice is exactly where that convention has no enforcement; it is `memory.Store.CreateLinkUnopposed`, which re-reads the pair's REVERSE edge inside the SAME `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction that inserts this one. `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` takes the database write lock before the first statement, so the two writers are serialised and the loser's read happens after the winner's commit; a check outside the transaction would not close this at all, since both writers could read “no reverse edge” and both would insert. A refusal is a normal outcome (`Result.ReverseLive`, `Classified.OpposedLive`, and a `not written` marker on the row), not an error: the edge that exists is the earlier writer's, and the next pass judges the pair in the live edge's own direction. **AND A VERDICT IS NOT A WRITE, SO THE SUMMARY COUNTS THE MODE'S OWN FACT (#834)**: `Result.Created` has always been a WRITE count — 0 in a dry run — and `Result.CausesCreated` was a VERDICT count wearing that name, which since #823 became readable as a write count because the 'causes' write is guarded and can be refused in the same run that reached the verdict. `Result.CausesWritten` is the missing write count and `Created`'s twin, and it is incremented only where `CreateLinkUnopposed` for `causes` RETURNS TRUE. The summary's `N supersedes, N causes` therefore counts `Created` and `CausesWritten` under `--apply` and `Confirmed` and `CausesCreated` in a dry run, rather than printing both or picking one for both modes: under `--apply` the honest number is what the graph now holds, and the refusal has its own line a few lines below, so a summary that counted it as well would be claiming an edge the same report takes back. A refused write stays reported where it happened — `Result.ReverseLive`, `Classified.OpposedLive` and the row's `not written` marker — and the row is the only place a refusal is counted. `Result.WouldWriteLinks` keys off the VERDICTS and is deliberately left there: it answers 'would an --apply of THIS result have something to write', and in a dry run there are no writes to read, so swapping in `CausesWritten` would silence the `Re-run with --apply to write these links.` hint on exactly the pass whose verdict the operator most needs to hear about. The one process that can reach a `causes` refusal is the second one racing it, so the two-process harness is what proves the split (`TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle`: verdicts 2, written 1, refused 1, and `verdicts-written == refused`) and the report's own mode-dependent selection is held by `supersedeReportCountsEdges` over a `Result` a single process cannot produce. The guard is a SEPARATE method from `CreateLinkJudged` rather than a rule inside it, because the bench seeders and the cycle fixtures must still be able to WRITE a cycle — it is the state a real store holds, and the repair pass and its tests can only describe it by building it. It is ALSO relation-agnostic: `CreateLinkUnopposed` refuses the reverse of whatever relation it is handed, and the `causes` write goes through it too since #823. **The two halves had to land TOGETHER, and the order matters, because #819 shipped the `causes` write unguarded on purpose**: with the override built from live `supersedes` edges only, a pair whose `causes` edge ran against the timestamps was re-proposed flipped on every pass, answered `causes` again, and would have been REFUSED on every one — a refusal that never converges, at a classify call per pass for as long as the edge lives, which is worse than the contradiction it prevents. `TestTheCausesWriteIsNotGuarded` pinned that asymmetry from the pass's side so a later change could not guard both relations for the sake of symmetry, and it is `TestTheCausesWriteIsNowGuarded` that replaces it. The guard stays, and it is needed for a second reason once the `causes` direction stopped being an override: the guard is the only thing that can make a two-pass race impossible, and a pass that asks a pair and then REFUSES the write would re-ask it for ever. So a `causes` verdict still converges — it drops the edge it contradicts and then writes its own direction, unopposed — and the refusal is the one-off race `supersedes` has, reported as `not written` (`TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle`). Inside the `causes` branch the SWEEPS run BEFORE the write, and that order is load-bearing rather than stylistic — written first, the guarded write finds a cycle's other half live and refuses, the sweep then drops that half, and the pair ends up holding the OLD edge with its OLD stamp, so the graph looks settled and the pair is re-billed on the next pass for ever. Each sweep is additionally gated on the pass having READ an edge the other way (`claimsHold`), because a second write in the run that can only ever move a row the pass has already seen is the one worth making; and both affirming verdicts now sweep the OTHER relation in BOTH directions, because a verdict of SUPERSEDES denies a `causes` claim whichever end it is written from, and a pair holding `supersedes A→B` beside `causes A→B` is a contradiction rather than the redundancy the old one-directional sweep assumed. `TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACycle` and `TestTwoApplyPassesCannotWriteACausesCycle` are the two-process reproduction for each relation (a built helper, two real stores on one file, a barrier held inside the classify call, a retag between the two scans, and the same fixture with `-verdict causes`), and `TestApplyPassWritesAnEdgeWhenNothingOpposesIt` is the other half: a guard that refused everything would pass both. **AND THE REPAIR PASS IS PARTIAL PER CALL, NOT PER PASS (#808)**: a project's open pairs are chunked across several harness calls, and a transport failure in one of them used to make every open pair `Unjudged` and throw away the calls already paid for. A call that fails answers nothing about the pairs IT carried and everything about the pairs the calls before it answered — complete, parsed, number-indexed verdicts — so `ClassifyBatch` returns the answered PREFIX with a `*PartialVerdictsError` carrying its length, and `Reassess` applies it and names only the failed chunk's pairs. All-or-nothing is not required for correctness, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: a cycle contributes exactly ONE candidate, so no pair's verdict is ever split across two calls. The error and the non-zero exit are unchanged, a reply whose verdict COUNT does not match is still no answer at all (the reply-to-pair mapping is exactly what is in doubt), and `Run` still treats a classify error as fatal rather than acting on a prefix — the creation pass's contract is that a transport failure cannot look like a successful empty pass, and its per-pair writes are the thing that pass exists to make. The `supersede_checked` NEITHER cache stays keyed by the ORDERED pair, because a cached verdict is about a direction; the unordered key is the pass's own, and the cache-skip test reads the unordered pair so a live edge keeps its self-healing whichever source proposed the pair. Since #823 that test also covers `causes` (above), which is the end of the graph-only-staleness bargain the cache was allowed to make: a cache skip is equivalent to a `neither` verdict, so it may not stand in for one on a pair the graph is linked in EITHER relation, and the sentence in `Run`'s doc comment that excused it is gone with the sentence it excused. The cache being content-keyed is what makes that affordable — a pin, a promotion or a project move does not change the pair's text, so none of them re-opens it (#820). The pass is KEEP-biased because the edge is not an annotation: the ranking guards demote its target and `ghost resolve`'s piggyback stamps `resolved_at` on it, so a wrong edge removes a live memory from every later session. Three rules decide that, from #686's 43%-precision measurement (every wrong edge joined two notes that were BOTH still true) and #779's re-measurement of the same shape over three more real stores (108 distinct proposals, 55% precision; 0.79 for edges proposed in all three of its runs against 0.33 for one proposed in a single run, so the wrong edges are an UNSTABLE classifier as much as a wrong one, and the count of runs that proposed an edge IS the correctness signal). **#779's rubric is a COVERAGE requirement over the same four answers, and it is four separate rules because a model can pass one and still fail another**: a `supersedes` must retire EVERY claim of the older note, not one of them (a newer note retiring some and leaving the rest standing is `neither` and NEVER `causes`, since `causes` requires the older note to remain independently true and a partly-retired note by definition does not) — and the `replaced:` field may then name several claims, semicolon-separated, so that is a widening of the VALUE and not a change of the reply format, which `TestClassifyReplyFormatIsUnchanged` holds verbatim against both parsers in both directions. Three shapes are named outright because each is separately false: a release, status, changelog or incident LOG never supersedes an earlier entry, and its one exception is an entry reporting the open issue CLOSED — scoped to that rule, and NARROWING is not closing; a RECURRING DEFECT is not a fix chain (only a note that says it is fixed, and fixes it, supersedes the note that reported it); and PARALLEL INVESTIGATION is not a chain (neither note retired the other, and the timestamps say only which was written last). No deterministic rule was added for any of them, and that is the load-bearing half: `VetoSupersede`'s error argument is that a false veto costs RECALL on the creation pass, and the same rule on the repair path deletes a correct edge and is deterministic, so the pair stays unlinked until a note changes. A rubric clause the model reads is reversible by a later pass; a second prose judgement in Go is not. The rubric is ONE Ghost-owned constant, `classifyRubric` in `internal/supersede/relation.go`, carried verbatim by BOTH shipped prompts (`classifySystemPrompt` and `classifyBatchSystemPrompt` = rubric + the numbered batch contract) and sent as the system prompt through `internal/ai`'s provider — the harness runs it and owns nothing in it, so a rule change is a change here and in one place, and a rule that reached only one of the two prompts would judge single-pair and batched calls by different standards. `TestClassifyRubricCarriesEverySupersedeRule` holds the shipped text to each clause in both prompts; `TestEveryRubricRuleIsMeasuredByALabeledPair` holds the clauses to `liveSupersedeCases` in BOTH directions, because a clause with no labeled pair is a rule nothing scores and a labeled #779 pair with no clause is a shape the prompt says nothing about. The rubric asks the falsity question — after the newer note, is the older note's claim false or no longer applicable, and two notes both still true are NEITHER even on the same topic — and a `supersedes` answer must name the claim in a `replaced:` field; missing/empty/placeholder reads NEITHER, which is a decision and therefore cacheable, and the NEITHER cache key prefix moves v2→v3 for it and v3→v4 for #779's coverage rule (a cache hit is a permanent skip for the life of that text, and every rule since v2 only ever narrowed the set of answers that are SUPERSEDES — so `TestSupersedeNEITHERCachePrefixMoved` holds EVERY prior prefix apart, not just the oldest, because a bump that cleared v2 while keeping v3 would leave exactly the #686-era rows in place). `replaced:` is the twin of resolve's `closed-by:` and is read by the SAME helpers (`resolve.ReasonedField`/`resolve.ReasonlessValue`), so a value that keeps a note in one pass's injection cannot bury it in the other's ranking; a synonym verdict is no way around the field. `VetoSupersede` is the free half, before any call: an older note stating an imperative that the newer note never names as retired or changed is settled as no-edge, and the imperative vocabulary is resolve's own (`VetoKeepImperative`, which `VetoKeep` also calls) rather than a copy, so one list protects a memory from being buried and from being demoted. Its error is in resolve's direction — a false veto leaves a stale note ranked, and #641's own "status-report-fix" fixture is what that costs, which the labeled regression set pins by name. A pair whose endpoints' scopes conflict is never classified: the scope reaches `SearchVectorScoped` so the neighbour budget counts only linkable rows, and `Run` re-checks the rule over every pair it is about to spend a call on — the reclassify path included, which is the only way a link written before the rule existed is reached at all. Beside it, at the same point and for the same reason, a pair with a KEEP-FOREVER endpoint (`retention`, see "Retention tiers") is dropped: `SelectCandidates` refuses such a pair on the fresh path, but the reclassify path re-proposes an EXISTING edge whenever an endpoint changed since the edge was written and never passes through it — so an edge written before the row was declared keep-forever would be billed a classify call and re-affirmed the first time that row was edited, which is the outcome the exemption exists to prevent. The edge is left alone: withdrawing it is `--reassess` / `ghost_link_withdraw`, and since #845 the creation pass deletes no graph history at all, so this exemption has nothing left to suppress. What the refusal stops is spending a call to keep it alive. The classifier is handed note text only, so it cannot see a `memories.scope` column; the guard is structural, not a rubric line. The reclassify half saves one call per endpoint edit rather than one per pass, because skip-if-unchanged already holds an untouched pair quiet AND a re-confirm moves the stamp that test reads (#784); without the second half the first was only true of an edge no endpoint had ever touched, and every edge past its first edit was re-billed and re-rolled on every pass after it. `memory_links.created_at` is therefore a JUDGEMENT stamp and not a claim about when the edge entered the graph — it has exactly one reader, that test — which is why the fix is the upsert's conflict clause and not a `validated_at` column: a new column would mean a schema v19 step, a version bump and a second place to keep in step, for a fact the existing column already held and nothing else reads. The stamp is a PARAMETER (`CreateLinkJudged`, with `CreateLink` as the empty-stamp wrapper every other caller uses) because only the caller knows what it judged; `datetime('now')` there would stamp the write rather than the judgement. The history row is keyed on the edge BECOMING active, not on this column, so a re-confirm still writes none. The scope refusal is not counted in `Result`: against the shipped store the query drops the conflicting rows before `SelectCandidates` sees them, so a count there would read as zero for work declined, while the veto IS counted (`Result.Vetoed`, printed) because a run that declined work it did not do must not report the same totals as one that found nothing to. An existing scope-conflicting edge is left in the graph and exempted at read time by `SupersedePenalties`, by `ghost resolve`'s supersedes piggyback, and by the two fold-target liveness checks — the piggyback is the one that would have been persistent, stamping `resolved_at` on the older endpoint. A vetoed pair's existing edge is left alone there — **AND SINCE #845 SO IS EVERY PAIR'S `supersedes` EDGE, WHICH IS THE PASS'S RULE AND NOT `--apply`'s: AN ORDINARY PASS REPORTS A WITHDRAWAL AND NEVER MAKES ONE, WHILE ITS WRITES ARE UNCHANGED**. Measured over a real store, 4 of 11 withdrawals the pass made were correct, 1 unsure and 6 wrong, and the wrong ones shared a shape — a newer note retiring ONE claim of an older note whose other claims still held. The verdict was a fair reading of the two bodies under the every-claim rule and the pass then deleted a correct edge, which is the one direction the KEEP bias below does not reach: the error surfaces as a stale memory promoted and a current one hidden, both behind a flag the operator set for WRITES, and a pass whose errors are invisible until a memory stops being reminded of is not a KEEP-biased pass. So `ordinaryPassWithdrawsSupersedes` is a package constant and NOT an `Options` field — it is not a threshold an operator tunes, and there is no setting under which a creation pass stops REPORTING a withdrawal it declines to make — and the removal belongs to the two commands a person asked for: `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` re-judges the edge under the current rules and withdraws what no longer holds, `--withdraw` removes the edge the caller names without asking a model. **THE `causes` SWEEP IS NOT WITHHELD, AND THE REPAIR COULD NOT REACH IT IF IT WERE**: `Reassess` — the repair every report here names, and the only one that re-judges an edge under the current rules — loads live `'supersedes'/'llm'` edges and can never see a `'causes'` one, so a `causes` edge left contradicting a supersession would be a contradiction in the graph under a repair line pointing at a command that cannot see it, which is the exact shape `Result.Bidirectional` refuses to create when it declines to judge a cycle; `--withdraw` can remove one, but only for a pair an operator has already named by hand, which is a targeted undo rather than a re-judge. Nothing in the ranking demotes on a `causes` edge either, so the harm #845 measures cannot come from leaving one live; and the ordinary pass's both-ways sweep is what converges a `causes` cycle (#823) at all. The withholding is ONE decision taken once: `withholdSupersedesDrop` zeroes the predicted `supersedes` count and `withdrawSupersedesEdge` returns 0 for a withheld row, so the prediction the row's clause is built from and the write the apply block issues cannot disagree, and all three denying branches reach the rule through the one function rather than spelling the guard out three times. A WITHHELD withdrawal is REPORTED in both modes (`Classified.WithdrawSuppressed`, `Result.WithdrawSuppressed`) and is NOT CACHED — `newNeither` skips every pair holding a live edge, and the withheld one is still live — so the pair is re-judged and re-reported on every later pass until an operator removes the edge (`TestASuppressedWithdrawalIsNotRecordedAndIsReportedAgainNextPass`). The flag is set in the classify loop, BEFORE the pre-write existence check, and it is CLEARED at the one site that learns the endpoint is gone (`supersede.go`'s `!pairAlive` `continue`), counter and all: `memory_links.source_id`/`target_id` are `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a concurrent reflect's consolidation replacement took the edge with it, and a row still claiming a live edge — plus a `N pair(s) withheld` line saying the edge "is still live and still demoting its target" — would be the opposite of the truth for a memory that no longer exists. Before #845 that same stale row fell through to `already gone`, which is what had happened (`TestAStaleWithheldWithdrawalSaysTheEdgeIsGoneBecauseItIs`). The row is NOT excluded from the NEITHER cache write by this (`writable` does that): it simply never had a verdict to record, since the cache key needs the pair and a stale pair is re-judged next pass anyway. **AND THE RESIDUAL RISK IS DISCLOSED WHERE THE REPAIR IS RECOMMENDED, WHICH SINCE #862 MEANS NAMING THE GATE**: the repair acts on the same verdict #845 measured at 6 wrong withdrawals in 11, so every surface that prints the repair goes through ONE renderer, `followup.ReassessCommand(project, n)` — the withheld summary line, the `STILL LIVE` clause, the two creation-pass refusals that name the repair, the note that recommends the gate, `supersedeUsage` and the docs — because a repair line naming the bare form sends the operator to the weakest version of the command that exists to repair their graph. There is no ungated renderer any surface may call: the ungated spelling is what an OPERATOR types, not what a report prints. The gate flag and the `--apply` are rendered together for a reason an earlier version got wrong — appending `--consensus 3 --apply` to a string already ending in `--apply` printed `--reassess --apply --consensus 3 --apply`, which PARSES (both flags are boolean) and which the old `strings.Contains(out, "--apply --apply")` guard could not see; the guard now counts `--apply` per line and `TestSupersedeRepairCommandsParse` parses the rendered form. The two LOG lines in `internal/supersede` are the exception and name the command as TEXT rather than through this package (it does not import `internal/followup`, and a log line has no project name to render); they carry the gate for the same reason, and a test reads their wording. Disclosure is not what replaced the gate here; the flag did (see the consensus bullet), and the disclosure now carries the operator to it. What is still disclosed rather than gated is the UNGATED default itself: `Reassess` reads one verdict per edge, so the flagless `--reassess --apply` remains the weaker path, which is why `reassessConsensusNote` says so out loud after it withdraws something, and why every recommendation names `--consensus 3` rather than the bare repair. `--withdraw <source> <target> --apply` asks no model at all and is unaffected by either. The trade is named rather than absorbed: a backwards live `supersedes` edge, #641's shape, no longer self-heals in the ordinary pass, and `ghost supersede --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` withdraws it. **A pair the pass DID move is reported per edge, which is a separate rule from the counts above**: a live 'supersedes' edge re-judged into NEITHER, CAUSES or REVERSED used to be a real graph mutation through the same `InvalidateLink` that writes the `unsupersede` row, reaching the operator only as the `N reclassified` total; it is now a row naming an edge that is STILL LIVE, while the `causes` rows the same verdicts really sweep are still mutations, and `Classified.Withdrawn` is false for a withheld row through `withdrewLiveEdge` even where `CausesDropped > 0` — the edge the row is about is in the graph, and the `[+N causes edge]` clause names the ones that are not. So `Classified` carries what a report needs and a caller cannot reconstruct — `Reclassified` (the pair held a live edge, so the verdict WITHDREW rather than declined), `ReclassifiedFrom` (WHICH relation that edge carried, and the reason it is a field and not an inference: while a live edge could only be `supersedes`, “the pair held a live edge” and “the pair held a live `supersedes` edge” were the same fact and any verdict but SUPERSEDES was necessarily a change; a live `causes` edge makes both readings reachable — a `causes` verdict on such a pair RE-AFFIRMS it and a SUPERSEDES verdict REPLACES it — so `Result.Reclassified`, the reclassified-row rendering and the resolve follow-up's own filter are all keyed on the edge's relation rather than on the verdict), `Withdrawn` (this call's invalidation moved it: false in a dry run, false under `--apply` when a concurrent pass took it first, and false for a WITHHELD withdrawal even where the `causes` sweep beside it moved a row), `WithdrawSuppressed` (the verdict WOULD have taken the pair's live 'supersedes' edge away and this call declined to, #845 — the third of the three facts a reclassified row carries, and the only one of the three an operator can settle by re-running the same command, because a concurrent pass that took the edge first leaves nothing owed while a withheld edge is still live and still demoting its target; it is CLEARED again where the apply block finds an endpoint gone, since the cascade took the edge and there is nothing live left to claim), `CausesDropped` (the SECOND row the same verdict moves — a NEITHER or a REVERSED also drops the pair's live `causes` edge — counted from what the write RETURNED) and `CausesDroppable` (the dry run's FORECAST of that count, and it costs no read because it is counted off the edges the pass already loaded: forecasting a deletion with a prediction QUERY would put a second read's failure into a pass whose only fatal error is a write error, and a count only an applied run can reach is a count the preview does not preview). Both come from ONE function, `verdictDrops`, gated on the same `claimsHold` predicates the apply block's sweeps are gated on, so the prediction and the writes cannot disagree about whether there IS a row to move; it returns a THIRD value — what `withholdSupersedesDrop` (#845) decided about the `supersedes` half — rather than leaving each caller to re-derive it, because the ONE prediction, the ONE row field, the ONE counter and the ONE apply-block guard all have to be derived from the same decision and a suppression applied in two places is one a later change can half-undo; the two numbers CAN differ, because a concurrent pass may take a row in between, which is why the observed one is what `--apply` reports and the forecast is only what a dry run has. The reclassified-row predicate carries `c.CausesDroppable > 0` as well as `c.CausesDropped > 0` for the same reason it carries the second one: a `causes` cycle answered CAUSES keeps the relation the row is about and still deletes a row, and in a dry run that row has to be REACHED for the forecast to be printed at all (`TestSupersedePairLinesNamesTheEdgeACausesCycleRemoved` holds a dry-run fixture that a real dry run can actually emit — `Withdrawn` false AND `CausesDropped` 0 — because a fixture leaving the observed count at 1 is reached through a field a dry run never sets and would prove nothing about the dry run's own path) and `TargetProjectID` — the CLI renders one row per withdrawal in the SHAPE and the WORDING `--reassess` uses for its own (both ids, the verdict's reason, and a marker that claims a write only where one landed), and an `--apply` run hands the withdrawn targets to the same `withdrawnTargets` / `writeReassessTargets` / `supersedeReassessFollowup` block the two repair modes use, so a third surface cannot print a third version of the command (#785). That follow-up is built over `supersedes` withdrawals ALONE, and the RELATION is a filter rather than an assumption: the repair is a `ghost resolve --reassess`, resolve's supersedes piggyback acts on `supersedes`/`llm` edges only, and a withdrawn `causes` edge orphaned no `resolved_at` — so naming its target sends the operator to clear a memory nothing is holding down. A WITHHELD `supersedes` withdrawal is excluded from it for the same reason reached from the other side, and by its OWN field rather than by `Withdrawn`: the edge is still live, so the piggyback that stamped the `resolved_at` is still holding that target down and there is no orphaned resolution to clear, while a concurrent pass leaving the identical state behind left the edge NOT there. Listing a withheld pair would print a repair for a memory nothing needs re-hiding, and in a dry run it would also print `Re-run with --apply to withdraw these edges.` — a promise of a flag that does not withdraw it, which is how a report talks an operator into a second run that cannot help (`TestReclassifiedWithdrawalsNamesEveryOrphanedTarget` pins the empty list, because since #845 every `Reclassified`-from-`supersedes` + denying-verdict row this pass can reach is withheld, so that guard is the whole of the function's reach and deleting it would silently start printing follow-ups for edges that are in the graph). A `causes`→`supersedes` change is still REPORTED, by the row, because a report that hid a graph change would be the other half of the same lie. A `NEITHER` on a pair the graph never linked is NOT a withdrawal and is reported as none: no edge justified a resolution for it, so naming it would send the operator to re-judge a row for nothing. And `ghost supersede <project> --reassess [--apply]` (`internal/supersede/reassess.go`) is the repair path: it re-judges every live 'supersedes'/'llm' edge under the current rules and withdraws the ones that come back NEITHER, vetoed, CAUSES or REVERSED through `InvalidateLink` (which writes the `unsupersede` history row), printing each withdrawn edge with the rule that withdrew it, which half decided it (`veto, no harness call` vs `classifier`) and — because a withdrawal sweeps the pair's 'causes' edge as `Run` does on the same self-contradicting verdicts — how many `causes` rows that took (`WithdrawnEdge.CausesSwept` is the PREDICTION in a dry run and the OBSERVED count under `--apply` — a row never claims a deletion that did not happen, and a sweep that ERRORED sets `SweepFailed` and prints "unknown" instead of a count (`ReassessResult.CausesSweepFailed` counts those rows, separately from the swept ones, because "swept 0" and "could not tell" are different sentences); a `causes` VERDICT sweeps nothing, because it affirms that relation). The prediction is read through each pair's OWN endpoints (`GetLinks` on the older id), NOT through `LinksByRelationSource`: the sweep deletes by id with no project predicate, so a pair whose older note has been promoted to `_global` or moved by `ghost project merge` is still swept, and a project-scoped read (which joins on the edge's source, and a 'causes' edge's source is the OLDER note) cannot see it. A dry run reports the count it is about to print, not `Result.Withdrawn` (which is 0 by construction in a dry run — the same rule `reassessSummaryLine` follows on the resolve side), and a failed invalidation returns the edges that DID land with the error, because each is its own transaction and a later pass will not see them again. That 'causes' sweep is predicted by a read that ONLY a dry run makes: under `--apply` every row's count comes from the sweep itself, so the prediction is not read at all and a discarded read can never be the reason a repair failed (`TestReassessUnderApplyNeverReadsThePrediction`). A dry run whose prediction read fails marks the row's second deletion unknown (`WithdrawnEdge.PredictionUnknown`, counted as `CausesPredictionFailed`) instead of printing a count nobody looked for, and `SweepFailed` stays false — no write ran. A classify call that FAILED is not fatal to those rows either, and that is the direction of the fix (#699: one dead `opencode run` cost a real rehearsal every withdrawal, 76 of 87 edges waiting on a rerun, including the ones the veto had settled for free): the settled withdrawals apply, the pairs the classifier never answered move to `ReassessResult.Unjudged` with their edges LEFT ALIVE, and the pass still returns an error so the exit says "rerun me". Every open pair is unjudged, not the subset the harness named last — a call that failed answers nothing about anything it carried, and a verdict-count mismatch is the same state, so both take this path. That is the UNGATED rule, and the gate narrows it rather than widening it: under `--consensus N` the bar for "decide this pair" is all N answers, so a pair one answer short of a quorum is unjudged with its edge live and the report says `no quorum` rather than `no verdict` — which is why `unjudgedReason` and the `Unjudged` field's own doc are both worded on the gate rather than on the harness. Withdrawing is the safe direction and settling the veto needs no harness, which is what makes it the one case where a partial repair is still a repair. The withdrawal sweeps the other relation's edge too, as `Run` does on the same self-contradicting verdicts, but NOT under a CAUSES verdict: there the existing 'causes' edge may be saying something true. THIS IS THE ONE PLACE THE VETO'S ERROR ARGUMENT DOES NOT CARRY OVER: on the creation pass a false veto costs recall, here it deletes a correct edge, and since the veto is deterministic on the same two note bodies the ordinary pass re-fires it forever, so the pair cannot be re-linked until a note changes — which is why the report marks those rows. Withdrawing is only half the repair: the memory is still stamped `resolved_at`, and only `ghost resolve --reassess` can clear it, so the chain is pinned end to end in `TestReassessResolvesAChainIntoResolveReassess` `supersede.Withdraw` (`withdraw.go`) is the OTHER repair, for an edge those rules still accept: a pair that is wrong for a reason no rubric can see (the newer note is not a replacement of the older one at all) is confirmed by the classifier, so `--reassess` keeps the edge and only the caller's judgement can withdraw it. Nothing is judged there, so no harness is involved and nothing is billed — `ghost supersede <project> --withdraw <src> <tgt> [--apply]` and the MCP `ghost_link_withdraw` call the same core, through `linkCapableStore`, so an operator and an agent take one path. Its rules are the ones a delete needs: a ref is a full id or 8+ characters of one (every report abbreviates to eight, so requiring 32 would make it undrivable), matched as ONE literal, case-insensitive PREFIX query inside the project plus `_global` — which is what lets a full id of any shape resolve through the same call, and `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim, so a ref check that insisted on hex would make an imported endpoint unnameable; the 8-character floor applies to a PREFIX alone and is decided by comparing the match with the ref rather than by inspecting the ref's characters; an ambiguous ref is a refusal LISTING the matches, never a choice; a pair with no live edge is an error whose message names the target's live edges — every holder id, and the relations among them as one phrase, so a pair live in EITHER relation is answered rather than filtered; and the WHOLE request is settled before any write, so one bad pair out of five withdraws none of them (`TestWithdrawChangesNothingWhenAnyPairIsWrong`). **A PAIR NAMES A RELATION, AND WITHDRAW REACHES BOTH (#833)**: `WithdrawPair.Relation` is empty for auto-select, and `resolvePair` then walks the two directed relations in a FIXED order — `'supersedes'` then `'causes'` — because they are not equal and 'supersedes' is the one that buries its target and the one every pre-#833 call site meant. The order is a stated rule rather than whatever the read happened to return for a stated reason: a pair holding BOTH is precisely the case where it decides which edge moves. A PINNED relation is a FILTER and not a preference — it skips every other relation rather than sorting behind this one, because falling back is the wrong-edge withdrawal the pin exists to prevent — so the holder set `resolveSource` falls back to is deliberately NOT narrowed by it, which is what turns that case into a PRECISE refusal ('no live supersedes link A→B, still caused by A') rather than a refusal of the SOURCE ref as unresolvable. This is the second way a `causes` edge became a first-class claim: its DIRECTION started deciding which way a pair is judged (#823), so a pair whose only edge is a `causes` one is a pair a person has to be able to name — and the pair that most needs it is one the pass refuses for ever, the #778 tie whose two rows share both timestamps and so carry no chronology to orient by, which until #833 had NO repair at all through either surface. `WithdrawnLink.Relation` carries the resolved relation to the `InvalidateLink` write, because that write is relation-scoped: hardcoding 'supersedes' there would invalidate a row that does not exist and leave the `causes` edge live under a report that says it was withdrawn. It is a `supersedes` withdrawal that writes the `unsupersede` history row and draws the resolve follow-up, and a `causes` one that does neither, because a `causes` claim never held its target down. An edge belongs to the named project if EITHER endpoint does, and `_global` belongs to every project — but the two supersede reads are NOT the same rule, and the difference is the question each answers. `LinksInto`, which a TARGETED withdrawal decides on, takes EITHER endpoint: the caller has already NAMED the memory being un-buried, and its project may withdraw whatever buries it. It also takes the RELATION, and an empty one returns the two relations a withdrawal can act on ('supersedes' first, by `ORDER BY`, then `created_at`) — the widening #833 needs, stated as the same query rather than a second one, because a read that admitted 'supersedes' plus 'causes' and a read the selection then filtered in Go are two statements that can disagree about what is live. Only those two relations are in the empty form: a 'related' or 'contradicts' edge is not a supersession claim, nothing in the ranking reads it, and listing one in a refusal would send an operator after an edge neither surface was asked about. `LinksByRelationSource`, which feeds a PASS, takes BOTH: the pass judges a pair and then writes links on it, so judging an edge acts on both of its endpoints, and a read that admitted an edge whose target belonged to a third project would let `ghost supersede p --reassess` delete a claim in `q`'s graph. The shared scope is in both because a promotion creates it: `ghost_memory_promote` and `ghost reflect --promote-globals` move a memory into `_global` and KEEP its links, so a live edge is left with one endpoint in the project and one in the shared scope — and with the SOURCE alone every project-scoped reader lost that edge at once, so `Reassess` loaded 0, the ordinary pass never re-judged it and `Withdraw` refused, while `SupersedePenalties`, which carries NO project predicate at all, went on demoting its target and resolve's floor released the `resolved_at` the demotion was still justifying (#786). So the four `LinksByRelationSource` readers agree with EACH OTHER and with the ranking on every edge with BOTH endpoints in the caller's project or in `_global`, and `LinksInto` — used only by a targeted withdrawal — covers every edge with EITHER endpoint there; they still differ on an edge with NEITHER, which is another project's pair for that project's passes to judge. A project-scoped `--withdraw` cannot even NAME a source in another project (`ghost project merge` leaves those behind), because the refs are project-scoped and widening them would answer questions about a neighbour's corpus — so the target is resolved FIRST and the source ref then falls back to the edges already known to hold it (`memref.ResolveIn` over the holders, and only on a MISS, never on an ambiguity or a short ref). That fallback is deliberately the narrowest widening available: the extra ids come from a read the caller can already see, so the flag cannot be used to enumerate another project, and it closes the one shape that would otherwise be demoted with no single-command repair. `Withdraw`'s TARGET ref resolves with no project predicate when and only when the named project IS `_global` (`memref.ResolveIn` over `Store.MemoryIDsByIDPrefixAnyProject`, the live-row half of `AnyMemoryIDsByIDPrefix`), which is what makes both endpoints of a shared-scope pair nameable there. And the refusal for a pair with no live edge names the edges that DO bury the target while saying NOTHING about whose they are, because the read no longer establishes it. Under `--apply` it prints the follow-up through the SAME helpers `ghost supersede --reassess --apply` uses (`supersedeReassessFollowup` + `writeReassessTargets`, one formatter and one file writer for the three withdrawal paths, and the file's header names which command wrote it), so all three name the SCOPED `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <the withdrawn edges' target ids> --apply` plus a `--only-file` beside it — SCOPED TO THE PROJECT THAT OWNS EACH TARGET, not to the one the command named, because a resolve repair draws its pool from `ResolvedCandidates(projectID)`, which filters `project_id = ?`: a selector resolved against one project and repaired against another is a SILENT no-op under a block promising a clear. It is one project in every ordinary case, and `RepairableTargets`/`withdrawnTargets` return one group per project (both surfaces through `internal/followup.ResolveCommand`, which quotes a project name that needs it — there is no MCP tool for the repair, because `ghost_resolve` is the FORWARD pass and stamps `resolved_at`, so the MCP result names the CLI command and says so) — scoped because an unscoped repair re-judges every resolved memory in the project (#702 measured 143 rows proposed, ~35% stale). The list is deduplicated and holds only rows the call actually withdrew: a row it never reached has a live edge, so nothing was orphaned by it. The report itself carries no resolve hint and prints the target's own first line, so the reader can confirm the edge was the right one **THE CONSENSUS GATE IS A GATE ON THE WRITE, NOT ON THE QUESTION, AND IT RUNS AFTER EVERY FREE FILTER** (#779). `RunWith`'s `Options.Consensus` (N; 0 and 1 are the ungated pass, `Run` is the ungated entry point and takes the same decision) makes N independent `ClassifyBatch` calls over the SAME candidate set in the same order and acts only where all N verdicts are IDENTICAL — unanimity, not a majority, because #779's measurement is the argument: 0.79 precision for an edge proposed in all three of three runs, 0.56 in two, 0.33 in one, so a 2-of-3 majority writes exactly the middle row. THREE properties are load-bearing and each is separately falsifiable. (1) **The N passes are spent on `pending`, after the orientation refusals, the existence check, the scope and keep-forever exemptions, the veto, the NEITHER cache and skip-if-unchanged** — every one of those is a question about a pair's text and identity whose answer cannot change on a second look, so re-running them per pass would buy nothing and the multiplier would land on pairs that were never going to be asked; `Result.ConsensusPairsAsked` is `N × len(pending)` and NOT `N × Result.Candidates`, and the test that pins it is the one that also pins an unchanged live edge is not re-asked at all. (2) **Each pass is a real re-ask: the cache is READ once, before the first pass, and no row is written until every pass is in hand and the apply block runs.** Persisting pass 1's NEITHERs and letting pass 2 be served from them would be invisible in the report — the run would read as a model that changed its mind twice — and it would make the gate measure the cache rather than the model, which is what `TestConsensusAsksEveryPass` is for (the fake says NEITHER on pass 1 and SUPERSEDES on passes 2 and 3, so a served-from-cache pass 2 changes the answer). (3) **THREE outcomes, not two**, and they are `agreed`/`disputed`/`undecided` rather than a bool: unanimous means every pass's ordinary handling applies UNCHANGED, including that a unanimous `neither` is a decision and IS cached and a unanimous `reversed` is STILL refused and STILL never cached — unanimity does not license the #641 backwards link. `disputed` writes NOTHING: no link, no withdrawal, no NEITHER cache row, no row in the decided list, only a `Disputed` record carrying the ids and the tally. It is deliberately NOT a withdrawal and NOT a reversal, because N different answers is a statement about the model's stability and not about which endpoint is current, and a live edge on such a pair is untouched and can still be withdrawn on a later pass's quorum. `undecided` — no pass produced a readable verdict — is `Result.Unclassified` and NOT `NotAgreed`, because collapsing them reports a prompt or harness fault as model instability and sends an operator to raise the quorum for it. A pair the passes split on is therefore RE-ASKED next pass rather than frozen, which is the opposite of the NEITHER cache's bargain and is why it writes no cache row. The gate is OFF unless asked for on the CLI (`--consensus N`, N >= `supersede.MinConsensus`; 1 is refused rather than clamped, because a gate of one is the ungated pass wearing the flag of a safety control) and ON for the automatic phase through `reflection.supersede_consensus` (default 3), which is read ONLY inside the `AutoSupersede` branch and emitted ONLY when it is at least `MinConsensus` — an ungated phase must not print a flag asserting a gate it is not running, and the parser would refuse its own argv. `auto_supersede` itself STAYS FALSE: a gate raises the precision of an unmeasured classifier and does not make it measured. `--consensus` is REFUSED with `--withdraw`, which judges nothing — N passes would buy no agreement about a decision the operator has already made by naming the edge. Since #862 it APPLIES to `--reassess`, and that is the boundary this gate drew the other way: the repair is the only path left that DELETES a live `supersedes` edge (the creation pass has withheld that since #845), so refusing the gate there left the one deletion path in the product with the FEWEST answers behind it. It spends `N` `ClassifyBatch` calls through `classifyVotes` and settles each pair through `settleOne` on the ordinary pass's own `voteCount.quorum`, so there is ONE implementation of "the N passes disagreed" over #779's ONE measurement — a second would be a second rule that drifts. It changes WHERE the decision is made, not what a decision is, and THE ONE POPULATION IT DOES NOT COVER IS THE VETOED EDGE: `ReassessWith` runs `VetoSupersede` while it is still building `open`, appends every vetoed pair straight to `settled` and never offers it to `classifyVotes` (reassess.go), so under `--consensus 3` a vetoed edge is withdrawn after ZERO classify calls and no pass is asked to vote on it (`TestReassessWithdrawsAVetoedEdgeUnderTheGate` holds the zero calls, the withdrawal and `Consensus == 3` together, because a result reading 1 would mean the FLAG was dropped rather than the veto exempted). The reason is the veto's nature and not an exemption granted to it: it is a deterministic rule over the two note bodies, so N passes of a model cannot agree it more than one does, and asking three times to reach the same answer would buy nothing and bill for it. So the help, `docs/cli.md` and `docs/configuration.md` all name the exemption wherever they describe the gate (`TestSupersedeUsageNamesTheGateAndTheVetoItDoesNotCover` holds all THREE help surfaces separately, since a qualifier in one place and an absolute in another reads as the absolute), and this is the price of the arrangement rather than an oversight: this is the ONE place the veto's error argument does not carry over, so a gated `--reassess --apply` still deletes a correct edge on a false veto, the ordinary pass will not re-create it, and the report marks the row `veto, no harness call` and excludes it from `verdictWithdrawn` — so even the gate recommendation printed under the report counts only the rows a model decided. A unanimous verdict settles exactly as one verdict did, and a split moves NOTHING — no invalidation, no `causes` sweep, no outcome counter — and is REPORTED with its tally (`ReassessResult.NotAgreed`/`Disputed`, and `CycleNotAgreed` for a split cycle, which is a distinct outcome from `CycleNoVerdict` because the passes DID answer and the operator's next step differs). **THE THRESHOLD IS N, WHICH IS WHERE THE GATE MEETS #808's PARTIAL REPAIR, AND IT IS THE ONE PLACE THIS CHANGES THE PASS'S CONTRACT**: `classifyVotes` returns a `voteCount` per pair AND how many passes ANSWERED it, because those are not the same number and the difference IS the decision. A pair all N passes answered is read through `quorum`; a pair fewer than N answered has NOT been agreed by N passes, so acting on the answers it does have would be the ungated behaviour the gate exists to remove — a wrong withdrawal on one of three answers is exactly what #845 measured — so it is `Unjudged` with its edge LIVE and the pass still returns the error. A FAILING PASS STOPS the loop, and that is a COST decision rather than a correctness one: the pairs the failed call carried cannot reach a quorum from the passes that did answer, and re-asking against the harness that just died is what cost #699's rehearsal 76 of 87 edges. The pass returns the error, so the exit says "rerun me", and nothing is cached for an unanswered edge. **THE DEFAULT IS UNGATED, DELIBERATELY, AND THAT IS NOT A HOLE**: `Reassess` is the wrapper and `ReassessWith` the pass, so every existing `--reassess --apply` means one verdict per edge exactly as before, and a default of 3 would triple the classify calls behind a flag the operator did not type. So the gate is RECOMMENDED rather than imposed — `reassessConsensusNote` prints one line after an ungated `--apply` that really withdrew an edge, naming `--consensus 3` as a COMMAND and its cost, since that is the trade — and it is suppressed for a run that already gated, which is the case where printing it would be actively wrong. The automatic `auto_supersede` phase was already gated through `reflection.supersede_consensus` and is unchanged.
- `bench/longmemeval/` — LongMemEval-S retrieval-only harness, the one place the embedding pass can be STOPPED PARTWAY (`-embed-deadline`) without lying about it. A pass that hits its budget stops at a batch boundary with every vector it computed already appended to the `-embed-cache` file, prints cache progress INSTEAD of the metrics table, and exits `3` (`exitPartial`, distinct from the floor-violation `1`) so a caller saves the cache and re-dispatches. Two halves, both load-bearing: a partial run must never print a metric or check a floor (it covered a subset of the questions, so its numbers are not a result and would read as a regression), and the budget must count only time inside remote embed calls, never total process wall clock — a warm pass makes no remote call, so a clock-based budget would cut off a dispatch whose cache is already complete and re-dispatch would loop forever without progress. That is also why the CI job stops the pass itself rather than leaving it to the job cap, and the reason is a moving file rather than a skipped step: a cancelled step's shell is killed but its CHILD IS NOT REAPED, so the harness keeps appending after the step ends and any archive taken in that window is not a snapshot (run 36501751853: `tar` refused the moving file, and `actions/cache/save` downgraded that to a warning, so the step reported `success` over an entry that was never stored). What the self-stop buys is a QUIESCENT file and a bench step that COMPLETES, so `grew` is recorded and the save is a real snapshot. The reported `N/M` comes from `warmProgress` over the same `selectedQuestions` set the scoring loop used, so the denominator is the pass that actually ran; it counts the role-prefixed inputs, so a text embedded in both roles is two vectors, and a document-role embedding of a question never satisfies its query role.
- `internal/bench/` — `ghost bench`: retrieval-quality benchmark harness (graded dataset + sweep + staleness/recency/ranking-state/maintenance-state suites + the no-answer false-positive report, measured per condition BY the runner — `runCondition` does not skip a query with an empty rel, it measures it, and the deeper report reads that measurement rather than searching again). Each scenario/fixture asserts the SHAPE of what it can see, not today's numbers: `TestRecencyTrapAtDefault`/`TestDecayFrontier`/`TestSupersedeDemoteClearsFrontier` are scoped to the trap fixture's never-decay half (the invariance the frontier claims; the decaying half moves by design and is reported), the ranking-state suite asserts that decay and the demote are each non-inert, that they do NOT compose (the demote recovers none of the probes decay demotes, because the rows decay pushes down are the rows the demote promotes), while the headline corpus is still inert — asserted by TestDecayDoesNotPerturbGradedBench, which owns the 551-memory load and checks it in the two forms the property has: measured for decay, STRUCTURAL for the demote (no memory in that corpus declares an age_days or a supersedes edge). Do NOT re-seed or re-measure the headline corpus in the ranking-state test: this package was within ~10s of CI's 600s per-binary ceiling, and #677 added ONE more corpus-wide test — ~9s — which failed `build-and-test` at 600.038s. One corpus load is ~10s and #677's per-row validity and provenance writes are free, so the cost here is TESTS, not rows and not loads: it fits again only because TestBenchRegressionFloors, TestBenchDatasetReport, TestDecayDoesNotPerturbGradedBench, TestSweep and TestFalsePositiveReport now run CONCURRENTLY, so assume a new corpus-wide test costs ~10s of a shared CI budget and measure before you add one — `go test -race ./internal/bench -run '^Test[^L]' -count=1` reads 188s on a laptop, a different machine that flatters the answer, and `sweep_test.go` carries the measurement and the failed CI job. The fusion claim is a PAIRED 95% bootstrap interval whose lower edge has to clear -`fusionTolerance` (0.02 = 4.4 queries of NDCG at n=220) — not a comparison of two means, because the margin over the vector leg is 0.018 with an interval of [+0.003, +0.034]. A PUBLISHED NUMBER FROM THIS PACKAGE IS A FUNCTION OF THE FIXTURE, not of the run, and it takes two store properties to make that true: every seeded row is stored under `corpusID(project, key)` — `bench:<project>:<key>`, so the ranking's id tie-break resolves a collision by the dataset's own key order — and one `corpusStamp` per seeding pass, so every row of a pass shares a `created_at` and the decay factor cannot order a tied pair of different categories by which side of a wall-clock second they landed on. Both were drawn per run before #708 and the `vec=0.50` sweep row could not reproduce its own interval; `corpusID` and `corpusStamp` are in `corpusstore.go` and are the reason the four seeders go through `CreateWithID` rather than `Create`. Two tests hold it: `TestSweepReproducesAcrossTwoSeedsInOneProcess` (two seeds, one process, the affected grid point) and `TestCLIBenchSweepReproducesAcrossProcesses` in e2e (two processes, the whole six-point table) — the split is the package's CI budget, not a difference in what is claimed: a second full sweep in this package is ~75s of `-race`, and the package went 336s → 487s of Go's 600s per-binary default in CI when the in-process test landed, so ~113s of headroom is left here. The next corpus-wide test has to be measured against THAT number, not against the 263s this bullet used to quote.
  `ghost bench --context` (#582) is a third report and the only one that measures the BLOCK rather than the order: context precision, contamination, result rate, budget adherence, per-bucket diversity and token cost, each measured by running the real `Store.Candidates` → `assemble.Run` path at the TOOL's own budget (`bench.ContextItems`/`ContextBytes` mirror `ghost_memory_search`'s `limit` 10 and `searchResponseMaxBytes`, and a metric taken at any other budget is a metric about a surface nobody ships). It is REPORT-ONLY and gates nothing, and the reason is the corpus rather than caution: the graded corpus holds no resolved, out-of-window or cross-bucket row and stage 2 drops the rest before admission, so its contamination figure is 0.000 BY CONSTRUCTION and a gate on it gates a tautology — the contaminated measurement is carried by the 8-row `contextFixture` instead, which is why the report prints "a zero here is a reading of THIS corpus" under an all-zero arm table. It adds NO corpus-wide test and must not grow one: the fixture is 8 rows, the new tests read 0.17s, and the package measures 11.7–13.5s over six runs after this against 14.36s on `98ffe9c5` before it, so the ~113s of race headroom this bullet quotes is untouched. Contamination and the trims are read from `assemble.Result.Leaks()` and `assemble.Trace.TrimmedByBudget()` and NEVER re-derived here (see `internal/assemble`); where those are unreadable the bench REFUSES instead of scoring — a missing trace, or an admitted row with no recorded verdict, is an unmeasured row, and a clean zero for one is the single output that report must never produce. Every ratio in the report is a fraction of a NAMED population, and the trim ratios are where that costs something: the query ratio's denominator is EVERY answered query, not the trimmed ones (counting only those makes the ratio its own numerator over its own denominator — `1.000` on a corpus where the cap binds everywhere AND on one where the budget was deleted, indistinguishable), and the two trims get SEPARATE row populations because a row the fit pass drops has already passed the item cap, so the cap saw every row and the fit pass only the ones the cap left. A trim that fired on nothing therefore reads `0.000` over a real population, never `n/a` — it ran and cut none — and the graded denominator is the graded rows that REACHED the stage, which is neither the admitted count nor `reached - cut` and coincides with each only when the other trim dropped nothing. `bench.SeedAt` takes the clock and `Seed` is its wall-clock wrapper; `bench.ContextInstant()` pins the CONTEXT report so two runs of one binary print the same bytes, while the ablation tables deliberately keep their wall clock, because pinning those would age the corpus by however long ago a constant was written and move published NDCG for a reason that has nothing to do with retrieval (#708's rule, still load-bearing).
- `internal/obsidian/` — One-way Markdown vault mirror (`ghost obsidian export|sync`). The store can hold ids, project names, tags and bodies Ghost never generated (`ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim), so every name it builds is a name somebody else chose: `idToken` keeps the first 8 bytes of an id only when they name a single path component a person could type and hashes the id otherwise (a separator put the note outside the `Memories/` subtree prune manages and had it deleted; a tab or line break is a legal name nobody can open; a NUL failed the whole export with `EINVAL` on every run), `folderNames` replaces a folder over `maxFolderName` the same way it replaces one that would escape because `ENAMETOOLONG` failed the export just as permanently, and `hasGhostID` unquotes exactly what `yamlScalar` emits. What prune keys its RETENTION on is the note's vault-relative PATH (`keepSet`, keyed by `keepKey`), not the id read back out of the note and not the bare basename — the front matter still decides whether a file is Ghost's to touch at all, so the block is prune's ownership test, but the id inside it is not its key: `yamlScalar` flattens tab/CR/LF to a space so every key stays on one line, and that is lossy — three records can render one `ghost_id` line, and keyed on that line they would collide on it and two of the three would be deleted. A basename is not safe either, and for a different reason: the same slug occurs in two project folders, and a memory and a task with the same title produce the same name and can share an id fragment, so one live note anywhere in the vault would keep a stale copy alive everywhere. A key that decides what to keep should be one the system holds, not one parsed back out of a file written for a human. Every front-matter value still has to occupy exactly one line — that is what makes the note valid YAML and the ownership test's answer well-defined (it reads to the first `---` and takes the one `ghost_id:` line there) — but no value read out of front matter, forged or merely flattened, can now make prune drop a note it wrote
- `internal/portable/` — The portable JSONL artifact (`ghost export` / `ghost import`): one typed record per line behind a schema-version header, written projects-then-memories-then-tasks-then-decisions in id order so two exports of an unchanged store are byte-identical. It is a wire format, not a backup — `memory.Store.Backup` is the restorable copy. Import never overwrites an id that already exists, so re-running is always safe; a record it cannot import is rejected individually and the run continues, so one hand-edited line does not abandon the file. A dry run opens the store read-only, so the preview cannot migrate or seed what it previews — which means openReadOnlyTransferStore must also refuse a store whose PRAGMA user_version is behind or ahead (memory.DBUserVersion / memory.SchemaVersion), or the first query fails with SQLite's "no such column" instead of a version mismatch. The artifact deliberately omits Ghost's own _global builtin seeds: they are written per-install under hex(randomblob(16)), so every importer's id-only dedup would duplicate them permanently, and the destination already re-creates them by content. Imported memories are stamped `source = "onboarding"` and unpinned unless `ImportOptions.TrustProvenance` is set: an artifact is a file that arrived from somewhere, and on its own authority it must not be able to plant rows the store exempts from consolidation by name (`manual`, `builtin`) or by pin. Embeddings, memory links and the resolve KEEP cache are not in the artifact: all three are derived and rebuilt by their workers. Import report lines render the id through `assemble.Token` in both `labelOrID` and the CLI's `printRecordLine`, and the detail is quoted on BOTH switch arms rather than only when an id is present, so a store written before the shape check cannot forge a report. `TestTheImportReportNeverPrintsAForgedLine` and `TestTheImportRecordLineNeverForgesALine` pin that, and pin the ordinary report line byte-identical to what an operator has been reading. EXPORT CALLS THE IMPORTER'S OWN REFUSAL PREDICATES, ONE PER RECORD TYPE, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE ROUND-TRIP GUARANTEE: `internal/memory.CheckImportedProject` / `CheckImportedMemory` / `CheckImportedTask` / `CheckImportedDecision` (internal/memory/import_check.go) are the ONLY place a portable record is judged, and `internal/portable.Export` calls the same four functions `ImportProject` / `ImportMemory` / `ImportTask` / `ImportDecision` do, so an export cannot exit 0 with a record its own importer will refuse (#813). #796 got the id-SHAPE checks shared and documented the gap it left — an empty project name or path, credential-shaped content, tags, `source_ref`, `agent` or evidence, and every value-set check still exported at 0 and failed on a restore — which is why the rule is now a function with two callers rather than two functions kept in step by hand. A predicate judges a RECORD and never a STORE, which is what lets the exporter call it: the three refusals that ARE about the destination (a project this store already records the same checkout for, a record whose project is absent, a task's unresolved blocker) and the recorded-history check stay in the importers, and each one's reason for having no export-side counterpart is a ROW in `TestEveryImportRefusalPathHasAnExportCounterpart` — the recorded-history one in particular could not be screened at export even in principle, since every write appends a history row and so every memory a store exports has one. A decision's `tags` is the twelfth refusal path the one shared predicate carries for it (#835), and it is the only one whose field is a TAG LIST rather than a column of its own: a credential-shaped tag makes the DECISION unbacked-up, which the credential rule accepts for content, `source_ref`, `agent`, `session_id` and evidence on a memory and on the four other decision fields, because the value is the leak and the `!` line naming the field is a repair rather than a silent loss. A record left out is NAMED with the IMPORTER'S OWN MESSAGE (never a second phrasing, which is the thing that drifted), rendered through `assemble.Token`, and `SkippedRecord.Secret` marks the credential refusals so the report can name the EDIT rather than only the delete — and THE FIELD LISTS IN THAT ADVICE ARE DERIVED, from `mcpserver.EditableFieldList`, which reflects over the same argument structs the three field-editing tools are registered with. A transcribed list is the same mistake as a second refusal predicate and it drifted the same way: the first version promised `ghost_memory_update` could edit a memory's `agent` and `session_id` and that `ghost_task_update` could edit a task's `title` and `notes`, none of which any of them takes, so four of the credential-guarded fields were sent to a call that rejects them. THE UNEDITABLE HALF IS ONE SENTENCE PER GROUP, NOT ONE LIST, because the groups do not share a reason or a route: a memory's `agent`/`session_id` are written by the update from the EDITING SESSION's provenance and a caller must not be able to name their own author; an EVIDENCE row (`evidence[N].agent`/`session_id`/`source_ref`, which `CheckImportedMemory` guards and `ghost export` refuses just as it refuses `content`) is written by nothing at all, and the only surface reaching one is `ghost history purge`; a task's `title` is written at insert; a decision has no update tool; a project's `path` is written by `ghost project bind` — and for THAT one the obvious-looking alternative, `ghost project delete`, CASCADES every memory in the project away, so advice that loses data is worse than the wrong field list the derivation removed — while a project's `name` is never rewritten at all. `cmd/ghost.secretUnfixableRoutes` states each with its own reason and route, because silence would leave the operator holding a row the report refused with advice covering every field they can see and not theirs. A CREDENTIAL refusal names the FIELD and never the value, on both the export and the import side: the value reaches a terminal, a CI log and a paste, so the report and the struct between them hold no copy of it — the FIELD name is what makes the line actionable without the value. A PROJECT'S RECORDS GO WITH IT when it is left out, because the importer resolves each record's project against the artifact. The file is KEPT and the command EXITS NON-ZERO, so a partial export cannot read as a complete one.
- `internal/reflection/` — Memory consolidation: LlmConsolidator + SQLiteConsolidator. The LLM tier speaks per-id operations, not free text (see "Reflection operation contract"). `persistent`-tier rows are never its input or its output: `ReplaceNonManual` excludes them from the snapshot and the replaceable set, which is the only way "a consolidation never saw this text" is true (see "Retention tiers")
- `internal/followup/` — the one renderer for the command that completes a supersede repair (`ResolveCommand`: `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <the withdrawn edges' target ids> --apply`, full ids, the project quoted through `--project` unless it is one bare shell word, and the ids quoted too — `ghost import` writes an artifact's ids verbatim, so an id is caller-supplied text like a project name. It returns TWO further values: the ids it could not carry because they hold a comma (the `--only-file` beside it reads one id per line and never splits, so that surface reaches them) and the ids it could not carry because they hold a NEWLINE, which NO surface can (`--only` splits on commas, the file is one id per line, and a `#` in an `--only-file` line is a comment only when it FOLLOWS WHITESPACE so an annotated `<id>   # note` keeps working — a selector holding a trailing comment is not a prefix and would make the repair refuse the whole file — at the cost of truncating an id containing ' #') — a memory with such an id stays resolved until the row is rewritten, and every surface says so rather than claiming a repair; the id file is REFUSED outright when every id in the set is one of those, because a file naming nothing is a list `readRefSelectors` rejects (one reader for both `--only-file` and `--mark-file`, differing only in the flag it names in its errors — a second reader would be a second place for the two to disagree about which ids a file can name). The command is EMPTY when no id is carriable, and that is deliberate in the other direction too: printing the command without `--only` is the project-wide re-judge #698 measured, and both surfaces print what comes back here as THE repair. A comma therefore reaches the id file; a newline reaches nothing, and is named. `RenderUncarriedIDs(viaFile, unnameable)` prints BOTH buckets — it takes both and returns both, so a caller cannot spell one through `assemble.Token` and the other through `%q` — and all FOUR surfaces call it, because the ids are a stored value at the START OF A LINE and that is the one place a reader takes what they see for Ghost's own: a comma bucket is by definition ids `ghost import` wrote verbatim, and #791's refusal covers a control character, whitespace, a backtick and a « but deliberately NOT a comma (a comma breaks a selector rather than a line), so such an id can still carry all three and `Token` is what makes them data (a « becomes `«`, so it cannot open a block of its own). The MCP pair used to print that bucket raw and the newline bucket with `%q`, which keeps a printable non-ASCII rune as itself — so the same stored value read differently depending on which surface an agent happened to be holding, on the most injection-exposed surface in the codebase. It is its own package because FOUR surfaces print that command and none may render it differently: the CLI under its per-edge list, the CLI under `resolve --mark`'s report (`resolveMarkReport`, whose follow-up lists only the rows THIS call stamped — a row already resolved before the run is not something the command would change), and the MCP tools `ghost_link_withdraw` and `ghost_resolve_mark`, where it is a CLI command rather than a tool call because no MCP surface exposes the repair at all — `ghost_resolve` is the FORWARD pass and stamps `resolved_at`, so pointing an agent at it would bury more memories than it restores- `internal/secret/` — Credential-shape detection for stored text (`Detect`); shape-based, never keyword-based
- `internal/provider/` — Interface contracts: LLMProvider, MemoryStore
- `internal/config/` — Layered YAML + env config (koanf). `Load` returns an error for a config file that exists but does not parse, for a `GHOST_*` value that is not readable as its key's type, and warns on file keys no `Config` field binds (env names are not key-checked — most `GHOST_*` variables are not config keys); CLI subcommands call it and fail. `LoadForHook` is the host-session path (SessionStart/Stop hooks, obsidian auto-sync, routing, the scratch budget): it reports the same problem on stderr and returns `FallbackConfig` — the compiled defaults plus the `GHOST_*` environment, i.e. every layer that does not read a file — so a typo cannot fail someone's session and cannot undo an environment opt-out. `envOverrides` lists the `GHOST_*` names the generic `GHOST_` prefix + `_`→`.` transformer cannot map, each with the parser for its key's type. `SetWarningWriter` redirects the warning sink, which `runMCP` points at its log channel; the sink is atomic because harness spawns warn from goroutines.
- `internal/procstat/` — process identity for the two places Ghost must not trust a bare PID: `StartTime(pid)` returns an opaque creation-time token — per-platform, from the `linux`, `darwin` and `windows` files plus a `!windows && !linux && !darwin` stub that returns `("", false)` — and `IsAlive(pid, wantToken, haveToken)` reports liveness from two variants only, POSIX signal 0 and the Windows handle/exit-code form. The token is opaque — never interpreted as wall-clock time, never compared across platforms or machines, only tested for exact equality against a token obtained the same way — and the load-bearing rule is that liveness FAILS OPEN: when the token cannot be read (an unsupported OS, an unreadable proc entry, permission denied) `IsAlive` reports alive, so a transient token-read failure can never flap a live process to "dead" and let `Reap` or `claimPidFile` delete what it should not. Linux's raw source is boot-relative ticks, which is not reboot-safe on its own while `.pid` files and `.owner` markers outlive a reboot, so the machine's `boot_id` is prefixed into the token. Windows treats a handle that opens with an exit code other than `STILL_ACTIVE` as not alive, because `OpenProcess` succeeding does not mean the process is still running. Both consumers go through it and neither re-implements it: `internal/mcpinit` (a `.pid` file holds `pid:token`, or a bare pid when no token was available) and `internal/scratch` (the `.owner` marker's `start` line). The extraction was forced by the second: `scratch` needs the same token but `mcpinit` already imports `scratch`, so a shared package was the only way to reuse the implementation instead of duplicating it — `mcpinit` keeps two unexported wrappers so its own call sites and platform tests did not move
- `internal/scratch/` — the temp space Ghost's LLM-harness children are confined to. The root is `<dataDir>/scratch`, or `$GHOST_SCRATCH_DIR` when set and NON-EMPTY (a blank override is treated as unset, so an exported empty variable cannot silently move the root to the process's own working directory), created 0700 explicitly because `MkdirAll` leaves an existing directory's mode alone and the override may name a pre-existing shared dir. It is under the data dir rather than the shared system temp because opencode writes a hidden ~4.7 MiB JIT-cache shared object into its temp dir on EVERY spawn and one consolidation lifecycle spawns hundreds of harness processes: left in the system temp those droppings accumulate until the filesystem fills, at which point every LLM-backed phase fails at once. `Root` resolves the data dir through `config.DataDir`, whose #721 `GHOST_DEV_FORBID_DATA_DIR` refusal sits in `config.DataDirPath`, so that refusal reaches this root too and the scratch dir cannot be planted where the store is forbidden. `Open` mints one `<pid>-<16 hex>` directory and writes a `.owner` marker (`pid=`, `token=`, `start=`); `Release` is best-effort, idempotent and nil-safe, because it also runs on early returns and on paths `Reap` may have already collected. WHICH variables a child gets is the spawn site's business — `internal/ai` pins TMPDIR/TMP/TEMP — and nothing here reads or writes the inherited temp dir, because this package must keep working when the system temp is full or absent, which is the situation it exists to survive. `Reap(maxAge)` (once per lifecycle in `cmd/ghost`, and from `ghost mcp init`'s `ensureScratchSpace`, both with a 24h age) scans the root's DIRECT entries and never the root: only entries provably created by Ghost are eligible — a readable marker, or a markerless name of exactly `Open`'s shape, and a markerless one only once its mtime is past `maxAge`, which is what protects an entry whose marker is being written right now — and anything else is left alone, because the override may point at a directory shared with somebody else. A SYMLINK is never followed, because the root is Ghost-owned but a link can point anywhere. A marked entry is owned-and-live only when its pid is alive AND, where the marker carries one, its `start=` creation-time token still matches the live process's (the same pid-reuse token `internal/mcpinit`'s `.pid` files hold); a dead pid, or a live one whose token differs, is abandoned and removed regardless of age. A `start=none` sentinel says the platform cannot supply tokens at all, so liveness alone shields it and that shield is UNCONDITIONAL — no age ceiling, no mtime consultation — because a phase timeout can be configured to 0 (unbounded) and any ceiling could delete a live invocation's scratch. Only a marker with NO start line at all predates tokens, and only that form falls back to the age backstop. `EnforceBudget()` runs before each spawn (`internal/ai`'s `harnessCommand` is its one call site) in a fixed order: measure, and only if already over `scratch.max_bytes` reap, re-measure, then warn LOUDLY through slog naming the root and the bytes-over, and record the bytes and reap count to `maintenance_runs` when the check fired at all. Every failure path warns and RETURNS: enforcement must never BLOCK a spawn (maintenance is not stopped by its own hygiene) but must never be quiet either. `scratch.max_bytes <= 0` disables the check; an unset key is the compiled 512 MiB default (`config.DefaultScratchMaxBytes`)
- `internal/secret/` — credential-shape detection for text Ghost is about to store, and the only place that answers "does this value look like a secret". It answers from the SHAPE of the value and never from the words around it, because a memory store is prose ABOUT credentials ("rotate the deployment password quarterly", "the tokenizer keeps a 30k vocabulary" are ordinary knowledge) and a keyword heuristic refuses all of them — `internal/reflection`'s `looksLikeSecret` asks the different question (is this text safe to replay into every project) and must never reject a save, only narrow a scope. So every rule needs a value: a fixed provider prefix at a realistic length, a complete PEM block, a labelled Cardano key field, an unbroken credential-length hex or base64 run, a whole word sequence from the embedded BIP-39 list, or a credential-shaped NAME assigned to a value that could not have been written by someone documenting the setting — every length floor chosen against the longest innocent value of the same shape (`longHexFloor` 128, so a 32-byte Cardano public key, a payment credential and a stake credential all stay storable; `mnemonicWords` 24, not 12, and the cost is a documented false negative). A `Finding` names the format and NOTHING ELSE — never the matched text, because a Finding reaches the log file, the saving agent's context and a third-party model's prompt, so quoting the value relocates the secret instead of containing it — and the rules are ordered so the first match wins and a specific format must precede a general one. Precision is not optional: the refusal is loud and blocking, so a false positive trains a user to route around the control within a week.
