The performance shape is measured and pinned rather than assumed (no keyword prefilter, because `LiteralPrefix` returns empty for a pattern starting with a word boundary, so a derived prefilter would be useless and a hand-written one a silent false negative — wrong in the strict direction, and a rule that never fires in this control is indistinguishable from a rule that does not exist; `TestDetectIsLinearInLineLength` and `TestDetectDoesNotRescanTheLinePerAssignment` hold the line there). The writers that reach it are named at each call site rather than being all of them
- `internal/repo/` — repository identity read out of the filesystem, one function: `DetectRemote(dir)` runs `git -C dir config --get remote.origin.url` under a 2s `detectTimeout`. It exists to keep process-spawning out of `internal/memory`, which is deliberately a pure storage layer that never spawns a process, while the only place that can say which repository a path belongs to is a caller willing to ask git — so the capability is INJECTED rather than called: `cmd/ghost/main.go` hands this function in through `memory.SetDetectRemote`, `nil` restores a no-op returning `""`, and a store built without one resolves exactly as it did before repository identity existed. The load-bearing rule is that every failure returns `""` rather than an error — not a repository, no `origin` remote, an unreadable directory, no `git` binary, a hung `git` — because detection runs on save paths and "no repository known" is a legitimate answer, not a reason to fail a memory write; the timeout is that same rule applied to a network-mounted worktree or an NFS-backed checkout, which must never stall a write. `-C` walks up to the enclosing repository, so a path pointing inside a checkout still reports that checkout's remote. `internal/mcpserver` calls the same detector, and only when a `project_id` contains a path separator — a project NAME says nothing about a repository, so an ordinary named save never crosses the boundary, which is what lets its tests replace the package var and prove it. `ghost project bind` passes the same function, so the remote recorded there is the one resolution later compares against rather than a second spelling of one repository
- `internal/selfupdate/` — `ghost upgrade` self-update from GitHub Releases. The PRODUCER side of release authenticity (#694) is asserted from here, not from the workflow: every asset `.goreleaser.yml` publishes and every zip the `plugin` job uploads carries a GitHub artifact attestation minted by `actions/attest-build-provenance`, and that action is pinned by full commit SHA because it is the one release step whose output clients are told to trust (`TestReleaseJobAttestsExactlyWhatGoReleaserPublishes` DERIVES the expected subject paths from `.goreleaser.yml` rather than transcribing them, in both directions, so a new archive format or a renamed checksum file cannot merge as an unattested gap; `TestPluginJobAttestsExactlyWhatItUploads` reads the `gh release upload` step and compares; the two attestation scopes are job-scoped, never workflow-scoped, or the `docker` job inherits an OIDC token). The plugin attestation runs BEFORE `gh release edit --draft=false`, so a failure leaves an unpublished draft a re-run completes rather than a public release whose zips have no provenance — that ordering is asserted too, because it is the difference between a recoverable and an unrecoverable release. A DERIVATION only catches what it derives independently: an extra file's path is taken verbatim from `release.extra_files` because GoReleaser resolves that glob from the project root and never copies it into `dist/`, `TestEveryLiteralAttestationSubjectExists` then asks of every literal subject path whether it names a file that will exist at attest time (a repository file, the checksum file, or one this job's own steps build), and `TestTheReleaseTimeGuardFailsOnASingleZeroMatchPattern` EXECUTES the workflow's guard step so a guard that never fails is not mistaken for a guard. That last pair exists because `actions/attest-build-provenance` only errors when the COMBINED glob result is empty, so a single wrong path is dropped silently and the release ships an asset nobody attested. The CONSUMER side lives in the same package (`attestation.go`) and has three load-bearing rules, all of them about which states may be collapsed into which. (1) Nothing is hand-rolled: the bundle is parsed and the certificate checked by sigstore-go, and the trust root comes from that library's TUF client — a hand-written chain check is exactly the thing that is subtly wrong in ways no test here would catch, and the whole value of an attestation is that it is a signature nobody in this repository made. (2) The three failures are NOT one failure, and the boundary between them runs through the HTTP status code, not through the call site. `ErrNoAttestation` (the service holds none) is distinct from a 500, an unparseable response, a 404 on a presigned `bundle_url` and a trust root that would not fetch, because only the first is a fact about the release and only the first is something a user can permanently do anything about; the verifier reports the first as `AttestationAbsent` and everything else as `AttestationUnreachable`, and a client that collapsed them would turn a Sigstore outage into a permanent belief about a release. `attestationGet` takes `absenceOn404` for exactly this reason: ONLY the index's 404 is the answer to "does this release have an attestation", so a blob 404 must not be — and it is a parameter rather than a convention because a blob's presigned lifetime has nothing to do with whether the release is attested. A member that cannot be read is collected as a reason instead of aborting the lookup, so a release with several attestations and one dead blob link still yields the bundles the others carried; the reverse would let anyone who can publish one extra unreadable attestation make a release uninstallable. `AttestationUnverifiable` — a bundle exists and did not verify — is fatal whatever flags exist, because a loud override that can wave through a failed verification hands the feature to anyone who can mint one bundle of their own. (3) A refusal never quotes the identity that was REJECTED. A certificate SAN is attacker-supplied text and sigstore-go's own mismatch error prints the value it got, so `classifyAttestationFailure` reduces every failure to this package's own words and the required identity is named instead. The same rule reaches the reason strings: a blob 404 must not leak the absence wording into a message a user reads, because a correct state classification with a misleading sentence is the same defect told differently. The cutover constant `FirstAttestedVersion` is applied inside `AttestationVerifier.Check` rather than by its callers, so no call site can forget it, and an unorderable version counts as REQUIRING an attestation — "cannot tell" must never read as "old enough to skip". The trust root is fetched only once a bundle is in hand, from a data-directory resolver rather than a path, so a pre-cutover upgrade pays neither a TUF round trip nor the data-directory guard — and it is fetched under its own `attestationTimeout` rather than the caller's, because the caller in production is the whole 12-minute run budget, which is both a long wait and the wrong explanation for a stalled Sigstore endpoint. `install.ps1` is held to the SAME three states and the same override, and `TestInstallPS1AttestationDecidesTheSameWayGhostUpgradeDoes` EXECUTES the script's decisions against a stubbed `gh` while `TestTheTwoBoundariesAgree` and `TestTheReleaseWorkflowIdentityIsTheSameStringInBoth` compare its boundary constant and its certificate identity against this package's, because the script cannot import a Go constant and two copies of one rule drift. The boundary is a MIRROR of `parseVersion`/`coreVersion` rather than an approximation of it, and the two once disagreed in the only direction that matters: PowerShell's `^\d+(\.\d+)*$` accepted any number of components and leading zeros, so `0.42.9.1` and `0.042.9` — both UNPARSEABLE to the client — were read as OLDER than the cutover and installed on a checksum alone while `ghost upgrade` refused the same release. Two rules carry that: exactly three components, and each is `0 | [1-9][0-9]*`; and the table that holds them together needs rows on an OLDER line, because every adversarial row on or above the cutover answers `true` whether or not the parse is right, so a stricter-or-broken parser passed them all. The suite runs in CI on the `windows-plugin` job, which is the only job that can run a Windows script — the skip in `requirePwsh` is for local runs only, and its comment once claimed there was no Windows job at all, which was false. AND every comparison the boundary makes is CASE-SENSITIVE and WHOLE-STRING, because PowerShell's defaults are the opposite of Go's and the difference is invisible in ordinary text: `-replace` and `-notmatch` are case-insensitive with .NET Unicode case folding, so `V0.42.9` lost its capital and `0.42.9-K` (U+212A KELVIN SIGN) folded onto a plain `k` — both accepted as versions older than the cutover, both skipping the check. A `$` anchor also matches BEFORE a trailing newline, so `0.42.9\n` parsed, and an `[int]` cast threw on a component past Int32 where `strconv.Atoi` accepts one. The script therefore uses `-creplace`/`-cnotmatch` with `\A…\z` and `[long]::TryParse` throughout the version path, and the table carries U+212A, U+0130, U+017F, trailing-newline and past-Int32 rows. ONE trap is not in the table and cannot be, because the table chooses its own inputs: the version the installer actually checks is DERIVED by `Get-LatestReleaseInfo`, which has its own `-replace`, so a tag of `V0.42.9` was reaching the check as `0.42.9` while every boundary row stayed green. The case file drives that function with `Invoke-RestMethod` shadowed by a stub, which is the only way that site is covered at all. It cannot reuse the verifier: there is no Sigstore library in Windows PowerShell, and the attestations API serves each bundle snappy-compressed, which PowerShell cannot read — so it shells out to `gh attestation verify`, which needs `gh auth login` even for a public repository. So `install.ps1` splits the failures in two where this package splits them in three, and it does so ESTABLISHING which happened rather than mapping an exit status to a verdict: `gh` answers 'did not verify', 'nothing to verify' and 'I could not reach GitHub' with the SAME status, so the script proves `gh` is logged in, probes that this `gh` has the `attestation` subcommand at all (a gh from before it existed would otherwise be a permanent unoverridable refusal that blames the release for a property of the machine), and `Test-GhMachineFailure` recognises a transport failure by specific text — returning FALSE for anything it does not recognise, because a matcher that cannot be recognised must never be the one that lets something through. That is the fail-CLOSED direction, chosen on purpose: a DNS failure or a 500 must not be reported as a fact about the archive, and an unrecognised failure must be treated as a refusal. The list is therefore a DOWNGRADE list, so every entry has to be a string an attacker cannot put into gh's error text about a certificate — gh quotes the certificate's subject and the repository back in its messages, which is why 'SSL' and 'EOF' were removed from it after a control proved the first is matched by 'not from a trusted CA'. 'Unchecked' is a property of the MACHINE and is the only state `-SkipAttestation` (or `$env:GHOST_SKIP_ATTESTATION`) reaches, for the same reason `AttestationUnverifiable` is unreachable: an override that reached the other one would be handed to anyone who can publish a bundle of their own. The env var exists at all because the documented way to run the installer is `iwr … | iex`, where a parameter cannot be passed; and the check runs after the download (the lookup is keyed on the digest of the bytes that arrived) and before `Expand-Archive`, so nothing nobody has vouched for reaches a decompressor.

- `eval/storyline/` — LOCAL-ONLY multi-session storyline runner (#339), reusing `eval/cycle`'s substrate (scratch XDG roots, seeded opencode auth, the built `cmd/ghost` binary, the embedding drain, the chronology restamp) rather than extracting it: the duplication is deliberate, a refactor would not have fit the diff, and a shared helper that both runners depend on is a follow-up, not a prerequisite. Five rules are load-bearing. (1) **The grade reads STORE STATE, never CLI stdout** — `memory_links` rows and `memories` stamps; stdout is kept in the report because a warning is evidence, but a phase that prints a verdict it did not write cannot pass a check. (2) **The block is rendered BEFORE that stage's own records are seeded**, so what a session received is exactly what sessions 1..N-1 left behind; seeding first hands every session its own answer and makes `carry-forward` pass for the wrong reason. (3) **A supersedes edge's DIRECTION is read from the store's `created_at` stamps, not from the ids' order** — and a pair whose chronology cannot be read FAILS rather than passes, because the arc's direction is the one thing a direction-blind check would hide. (4) **Every id the grade resolves came off the `Result`, the OPENING records' included** — they are saved before any session and belong to none, and `Validate` lets a storyline reverse one (`stageOf` answers -1 for an opening record, so the "must be a later stage" rule passes). An id held only in a local slice makes `supersede-edge` fail on a store that got the edge perfectly right, which is a runner defect wearing a finding's clothes. (5) **The model is resolved ONCE, in `resolveModel`, rather than at each use** — `internal/ai` reads `GHOST_OPENCODE_MODEL` out of the RUNNER's own environment when the pin is empty, and `scratchEnv` rewrites only the CHILD processes' environment, so a stale export would decide the sessions while the phases used the default and the report named a third model. Beyond those five, two limits bound what a passing run can claim: the `stale-original` match is deliberately narrow (the record's own verbatim text) and the opt-in `-judge` covers paraphrase; the arc's lifecycle runs AFTER the last session by construction, so the runner shows what the block does with a reversal already stored, not what a supersede caught mid-arc would have changed. Nothing here is wired into CI (#338), and a non-zero exit is a finding about Ghost, not a runner bug

## Key patterns
- Memory categories: architecture, decision, pattern, convention, gotcha, dependency, preference, fact
- Untrusted parse surfaces (`claudeimport`, `hostevent`, `obsidian`): a value somebody else wrote is DATA. It is stored exactly as parsed, it names at most one path component inside the directory its surface owns, and it never decides the parse — the parser's own vocabulary does, and a payload that does not fit is rejected or mapped to the documented fallback, never obeyed. `internal/adversarial` states the invariant once and holds the corpus all three suites drive (#585). Two rules that look like filters and are not: a planted payload stays retrievable (hiding the tampering is worse than showing it; quoting into the data block happens on the way out), and every `hostevent` refusal fails open, because "allow the stop" is the only response the hook contract emits. Defects here were silent rather than loud — a front-matter line past `bufio.Scanner`'s default cost a memory its category, a separator in a record id cost it its note, a quoted `ghost_id` cost it a second prune pass — which is why each is a fixture and not a line of prose
- Backup and portable transfer: `ghost backup` is `Store.Backup` → `memory.vacuumInto` (SQLite `VACUUM INTO`, the same one implementation the pre-migration copy uses), so both claim the destination with `reserveBackupPath` (a `0600` `O_EXCL` create, before any byte is written) and both refuse an existing path. `VACUUM INTO` names no mode, so creating the file first is what keeps a full copy of the memory database out of a group-readable window on an `--out` outside the `0700` data directory. The write lock is held for the vacuum and nothing longer. It counts the snapshot by opening the written file read-only, never the live one — the counts a restore is checked against have to describe the file that was written. The portable artifact (`internal/portable`) is the other half: `memory.PortableProjects` / `PortableMemories` are separate reads because `ListProjects` has no `repo_remote` and an artifact needs the columns in an order it owns (the other reason this line used to give — that the ordinary list readers drop the validity triple — stopped being true when `memory.Memory` gained `valid_from`/`valid_until`/`verified_at`: retrieval hydrates all three, and `PortableMemories` selects them for itself), and `Import*` are separate writes because no existing writer can preserve an imported id, `created_at` or pin. Every `Import*` takes an `apply` flag, so a dry run runs the same validation as the run it previews
- Time-decay scoring: convention/preference/fact never decay; architecture/pattern 45-day; decision/gotcha/dependency 30-day
- Retention tiers (schema v19, #587): `memories.retention` is `session|project|persistent` with a CHECK, default `project`, and `memories.expires_at` is DERIVED for a session save (now+`SessionTTL`) and stated by nobody else — so no save can schedule the memory it just wrote for deletion, and a NULL expiry is the unprunable direction. `migrateV19` adds both columns and updates no row: a memory that persisted until somebody resolved it WAS a project memory, which is what every pre-v19 row reads as afterwards. `memory.retention` (the column, in Go) is not `Memory.Retention` (the field): the field normalizes an unset column to `project` in `scanMemories`, because one reader forgetting the column would otherwise hydrate a session row as "no tier stated" and a prune could not see it — which is why the six full-row readers share ONE list (`memoryColumns`, plus `memoryColumnsPrefixed` for the two FTS queries that alias `memories`). `DecayFactor(category, retention, pinned, ageDays)` is the Go mirror of `DecayRankingSQL` and both carry the bounded session decay (tau 7 days, floor 0.5, formatted from the same constants); a durable row's score is exactly what it was before tiers, which is the whole reason the multiplier is 1.0 there, and `explain` names it per row as `retention_factor`. `persistent` is the one tier a default may not take back, and the exemption is asked at every WRITE and every ranking consequence, not only where a pass selects inputs: `ReplaceNonManual` (snapshot, replaceable set, concurrent-save set, output delete), `RestoreSnapshot`'s UPDATE, `ResolveCandidates`/`ResolvedCandidates`/`SetResolved`, `supersede.SelectCandidates` (before the classify call, so the verdict is not paid for), `SupersedePenalties` and `DemotionPenalties`, which take the caller's PROTECTION map rather than a list of pins — because a stale `duplicate` or `supersedes` edge would otherwise sink a row every other pass spares, and because both lookups run on read-only handles (`GetTopMemories`, `explain`, and the assembler's near-duplicate stage over a passive bucket), where a statement naming the column fails in full. The two maps are NOT the same: `DemotionPenalties` (the near-duplicate demotion) protects a plain `pinned` row the way it always did before tiers and extends that to keep-forever rows, while `SupersedePenalties` protects ONLY the `persistent` tier — a `supersedes` edge asserts that one claim replaced another, and a pin keeps a row visible without declaring its claim current, so a pinned supersedes target sinks exactly as it did before #587 (the pin-sparing supersede variant is issue #739, kept out of the tier PR; `TestAPinDoesNotStopASupersedesEdge` and `TestASupersedesEdgeDoesNotSinkAPersistentRow` pin the boundary). EVERY caller builds the map from rows it has already read, and a caller handed ids it did not hydrate is a caller that cannot protect them (`TestTheDemotionLookupsDoNotNameTheTierColumn` drops both v19 columns and calls both). A fold RAISES the surviving row's tier and never lowers it (`mergeRetention`): a protection the caller asked for and did not get is a false report, while lowering would let a `session`-tier restatement schedule another memory's durable row for deletion. `ghost prune` is the ONLY thing that removes a `session` row, it is never run for you (no lifecycle phase, hook or scheduler calls it), the dry run is a read-only PREVIEW built from the same predicate, scope and order every apply batch re-derives (so it cannot describe rows the apply would not select), an APPLIED report is counted and listed from `RemovedIDs` rather than from the candidate list — every batch re-derives the predicate under the write lock, so a row a save pinned, re-tiered or re-saved between the preview and its own batch is correctly spared and is STILL in `Candidates` (`TestPruneSparesARowThatStoppedMatchingMidRun` puts such a write in that window through a tombstone trigger, because the `pruneBeforeDelete` seam cannot: it runs inside the batch transaction, which both holds the write lock and has already chosen the batch's ids), the rows it did not take are NAMED as spared and the line names the change rather than the outcome (a write that removed the row outright leaves nothing behind), the apply deletes in bounded batches (500 rows per transaction, each batch with its own tombstones and its own rollback), and every removal appends a `delete` tombstone before the DELETE. Its activity term is `COALESCE(last_accessed, updated_at, created_at)` — nothing in production writes `last_accessed` (`Touch` has no caller), so the grace is measured from the row's last WRITE, and `retention` is NOT carried by the portable artifact, so an imported memory arrives as `project` (kept longer, never pruned)
- Empty-set guard: never replace all memories with empty reflection output, and a reflection response carrying no operations fails the LLM tier rather than relying on that guard alone
- The gate lives in `TieredConsolidator`, NOT in the LLM tier — so naming a backend bypasses it unless the tier is wrapped. `ghost reflect --tier cli` and `--tier opencode` used to hand the bare `LlmConsolidator` to the apply path and had no floor at all, while the default `auto` tier and the unattended path were bounded; `eval/cycle` measures with `--tier opencode --apply`, so the suite was grading an ungated consolidator against a gated production one. Both branches now go through `gatedLLMTier` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`), which calls `reflection.NewGatedConsolidator` — one tier, no fallback added, so "exactly this backend" still means that and a too-small answer is a failed run rather than a quiet drop to Jaccard. It is a named function because `runReflect` calls `os.Exit`, so the tier switch is unreachable from a test and the behaviour has to be pinned at the seam. The wrapper reports the tier's OWN name, not the `tiered:` prefix (`TieredConsolidator.gated`): `Consolidator: cli` must keep printing `cli`, because a script or saved log-grep keyed on that label breaks silently otherwise, and wrapping a tier changes how it is bounded, not which one runs. The prefix marks WHICH CONSTRUCTOR ran, not how many tiers were in the list — `auto --require-llm` is a one-tier list and still prints it, so do not read the prefix as a claim about fall-through
- Reflection reduction warning counts survivors, not one scope: `reportReductionWarning` (`cmd/ghost/lifecycle.go`) measures `reflectRetained`, which adds the cross-project candidates back in when promotion is off because `applyReflection` folds them into the project. Reading `len(projectMems)` understates retention by the whole candidate set and fired `>50% reduction` on rounds that lost nothing — a warning that cries wolf on the one path where nobody reads it is worse than none. The input side is `len(consolidatable)`, not a re-count of non-`manual` sources: `consolidatable` already dropped `manual`/`builtin`/`pinned`/`persistent`/resolved, so every row left is one `ReplaceNonManual` would delete. It fires on a dry run too (the call is above the `if !apply` return), which is deliberate — the preview reports the compression it would apply — and only from `reductionWarnMinInput` (6) up, which is where the quality gate stops judging and this becomes the only signal
- Validity and provenance are written by the tools, or by nothing: `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` and `ghost_memory_update` share one `validityArgs` struct (`internal/mcpserver/validity.go`) and one resolver, so the six optional arguments mean the same thing in all three schemas. `valid_from`/`valid_until`/`verified_at` take RFC 3339 or a date and are stored in `memory.StoredStampLayout` (`verified: true` is the same field as "now", and `verified` together with `verified_at` is refused rather than resolved); a window that does not end after it starts, a `confidence` outside [0,1], an unreadable stamp and a `source_ref` over `MaxSourceRefLen` are tool errors, because stage 2 would otherwise read them as claims nobody meant. Nothing is invented: an omitted argument is absent, and absent is NULL, so a store can still tell "nobody said" from "said to be nothing" — which also means a stamp can be replaced but not removed. Four surfaces filter on a window: `ghost_memory_search`, the session-start block, which since #766 runs the same stage 2 rather than rendering its own rows, `ghost_project_context`, and the `ghost://memories/global` resource — so a memory whose `valid_until` has passed or whose `valid_from` has not arrived stops being offered at every session start and stops appearing in either listing. That is a change in what a session is told, and it is the correct one — the block used to inject a retired row as a plain present-tense line, which is a lie about it — but it arrived by INHERITANCE rather than by decision, so it is stated here: `ghost_memories_list` and `ghost_search_all` still do not run the pipeline and still render one marked `expired` instead (assemble.ValidityStateOf), and no surface carries a validity marker on a line it is filtering on. That list has to be read, not remembered: a surface that starts assembling is removed from it in the SAME change, because a tool description naming it as a browsing surface is a claim an agent then relies on when it decides whether a dated claim is still worth trusting. `unverified` is still kept, because `verified_at` is a flag rather than a predicate; a row nobody has re-checked is still true as far as the store knows. Saying otherwise in a tool description is a lie the next surface inherits — and a surface that renders its own rows does not get the shared renderer for free. `agent` comes from `provenanceFor` (client name, then process ancestry) and `session_id` from `req.Session.ID()`, which is "" on the stdio transport every Ghost host actually uses — NULL is the honest record there, and a made-up id would be indistinguishable from a real one. `memory.UpdateMemoryWithOptions` writes the columns with `COALESCE` (and `NULLIF` on the provenance strings) rather than a read-merge-write, so an omitted field is the stored value by construction and two concurrent edits cannot lose each other; `provider.MemoryStore` is not widened for it, so `mcpserver` reaches it through the `updateCapableStore` assertion. Stage 4's multiplier stays `1.0`: the renderer shows confidence and the writer records it, and nothing ranks on either
- Durable knowledge, not repository facts (#674): a memory is for knowledge that survives the conversation and is expensive to rediscover, and one rule is stated in five AGENT-FACING places that must be reworded together — the MCP server instructions, the `ghost_memory_save` and `ghost_save_global` descriptions, the reflect ops prompt, and the resolve classify rubric — plus the two docs that describe them (`docs/mcp.md` agent guidance, `docs/cli.md` resolve section), which go stale silently because no test reads them. "foo.go contains HandleFoo()" is the bad example (the repository is authoritative, so the note goes stale silently and is cheap to re-read); "Production schema changes require explicit approval." and "Deployment keeps database migrations separate from application rollout, on purpose." are good ones. The rule GUIDES and never refuses: the only deterministic half is `repoFactHint` (`internal/mcpserver/durable.go`), one advisory sentence appended to a response that ALREADY reports a stored id, on the three paths that write caller-supplied memory text and say so: `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global` (the instructions route the cross-project case there) and `ghost_memory_update`. NOT `ghost_decision_record`, whose companion memory's text is composed inside `memory.RecordDecision` — duplicating that format string in the MCP layer to judge it would let the two drift apart silently, and it needs both a reference token and a containment predicate in the SAME sentence — one without the other fires on exactly the durable notes the rule protects, since a note that cites a path and adds a reason is the case to leave alone. The equivalent reflection guidance is an obsolete-drop CANDIDATE only: the unattended path's answer to an unexplained drop is the verbatim re-add, so the new rule splices the mode-dependent `staleTail` and claims nothing the apply does not do under `--allow-drops`. Resolve gets RUBRIC text and no new veto, and NO new veto pattern: a veto is a second vocabulary for what a memory is for, `internal/supersede` reads the imperative half of the same list for the opposite decision, and `ghost resolve --reassess` runs that veto over already-resolved rows, where a false one un-hides a note permanently (#702). The predicate list is LOCATION verbs only for the same reason — a path-anchored behavioural claim ("`lifecycle.go` calls `os.Exit`, so the tier switch is unreachable from a test") is a reason, not a repository fact. The REFERENCE half is PATH-LIKE for the same reason: "it contains a dot" is not a property of code, and a bare `Capitalised.Name` fires on durable notes (an independent review measured five — `U.S. regulators`, `Bob.Smith`, `Node.js`, `Terraform.State`, `Jane.Doe`, every one carrying a real containment predicate), so a bare `Stem.ext` needs a LOWERCASE stem and a bare dotted identifier needs a Go shape (lowercase package qualifier, or a final segment with an internal capital). Measured residual: 0/612 on the committed bench corpora
- Save-time consolidation opt-out: `ghost_memory_save`'s `pin` maps to `UpsertOptions.Pin` and pins inside `Upsert`'s existing `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction, so a memory can opt out of `ghost reflect` in the call that stores it rather than in a second `ghost_memory_pin` call — nothing an agent writes is excluded by `source`. A fold pins BOTH rows (the copy just stored and the existing row the text merged into, which is the one a later consolidation is most likely to absorb), so the save result says so rather than letting the caller infer the pin from the id it returned; `FoldOnly` stores no row, so the target is the only thing the request can mean. `pinMemoryTx` deliberately leaves `updated_at` alone: the row's age and timestamp are load-bearing for decay and the `--skip-unchanged` fingerprint AND THE OTHER PIN WRITER AGREES (#820): `Store.TogglePin` — what `ghost_memory_pin` and the MCP pin tool call — writes `pinned` and NOTHING ELSE, so the operator's explicit call says the same thing about the stamp as the save-time one does. It used to carry `updated_at = datetime('now')` beside the flag, and that column is `ghost supersede`'s chronology (`orient`) and its change detector (skip-if-unchanged), so pinning an endpoint of a live edge re-armed a reclassify nobody asked for — a classify call for a pair whose two notes had not changed since the edge was judged, which is #779's measured route to withdrawing a correct edge and re-creating it on alternating passes — and on a pair live in BOTH directions it flipped the direction `ghost supersede --reassess` asks about, which is what stands the backwards edge #641 found in the wild. A pin is a decision about how a memory is SURFACED, and the readers that act on it read `pinned` itself: the `_global` passive bucket's order (`pinned DESC, importance DESC, updated_at DESC, id` — `pinned DESC` is its PRIMARY key, so a pin still reorders what it names, and only the tie-break between two rows equal on (pinned, importance) is given up), the composite ranking's full decay exemption, and `ghost resolve`'s hold-back, which refuses to stamp `pinned` rows. What a pin no longer moves, named: that supersede pair of readers; `ghost prune`'s grace, whose `pruneActivitySQL` is `COALESCE(last_accessed, max(updated_at, created_at, expires_at))` and which is therefore measured from the row's last WRITE — `Touch` has no caller, so nothing in production writes `last_accessed`, and a pin is not a write of the note, so an operator who wants a row kept for good says so with the `persistent` tier. `TogglePin` files no `memory_history` version and neither does `pinMemoryTx`: the change log records content, category, importance, `resolved_at` and `source`, and `pinned` is none of them, so a pin is recorded in the column the readers read and in no audit row. `PromoteToGlobal` is the same decision about the same column, and the reasoning is on the `internal/supersede/` bullet.
- Upsert dedup is one write transaction: `UpsertWithOptions` opens its `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction BEFORE the FTS duplicate probe and commits the writes the probe decides on, because a probe outside it leaves a window another ghost process can use — a save that strengthened the same row in that window had its increment overwritten by arithmetic on a value read before the fact, and a row committed in that window was invisible to the probe, so two saves of one fact became two unlinked rows (both are cross-process; the per-`Store` `s.mu` cannot close either). The strengthen is therefore `SET importance = MIN(1.0, importance + ?)`: the value it adds to is the one the row holds when the statement runs. Never query the pool inside an open transaction on the same handle — `OpenDB` pins one connection, so it deadlocks; go through the `sql.Tx`. `FoldOnly`'s re-verify of its target happens in that same transaction, which makes it a statement about the probed row, not a race guard
- A newer schema is refused before it is written to: `memory.OpenDB` reads `PRAGMA user_version` and rejects a store from a newer Ghost BEFORE `initSQL` runs. `initSQL` is `CREATE … IF NOT EXISTS`, so on a store that merely has more than this build knows it is nearly a no-op — which is what hid the ordering — but an object the newer build renamed, replaced or dropped is recreated there, in a store the same call then declares unreadable. A fresh store has no stamp to read and is initialized and stamped; a store behind is backed up (`memory.vacuumInto`) and then migrated
- A RUNNING server re-checks that on EVERY write, because the open-time check answers a question that a later migration invalidates (#746): a `ghost mcp` opened the store hours before a newer binary migrated it underneath, and kept writing for ~40 hours with no history row, no version for an edit, and no tombstone for a delete. There are TWO seams, because a check that only guards a transaction misses every write that never opened one: `memory.beginGuardedWrite` for a write TRANSACTION (what `beginWrite` delegates to) and `memory.execGuardedWrite` for a single AUTOCOMMIT statement. The second is not a nicety — the issue's own reproduction is a save, and a lone `Exec` on the pool is how a lone statement reaches SQLite; a version that was cached "somewhere per write" and a statement that never asked are the same hole. `TestEveryWriteRefusesANewerStore` walks the package with go/parser and requires every write to be one of the two or to carry a recorded reason, so a new write path cannot opt out by accident. Classifying writes by METHOD NAME alone is not enough to earn that claim, and the first version of this test was wrong in exactly that way: `CreateTask` (behind the `ghost_task_create` tool) and `IncrementInteraction` both wrote through `QueryRowContext` — a `RETURNING` clause, which is a write the method name denies and the SQL admits — and both succeeded against a store a newer Ghost owned while the scan passed. So the scan now reads the STATEMENT for a `Query*`: a leading word on a small read-only allow-list (`SELECT`, `PRAGMA`, `EXPLAIN`, `VALUES`) is a read, and everything else — every write keyword, `WITH` (which reads like an opener and can introduce an INSERT), and any statement text the scan cannot resolve at all — is a write needing a seam. Resolvable means a literal, the format string of an `fmt.Sprintf`, a concatenation rooted in either, or a name bound to one, read from its FIRST binding only because `+=` cannot change the leading word; a variable assembled anywhere else is unresolvable and therefore a write. `TestTheScanCatchesTheWriteShapesItWasBlindTo` pins each of those shapes against synthetic source, `TestTheScanStillCallsAReadARead` pins the other direction so the holes cannot be closed by calling everything a write, and `TestOnlyTheMigrationIsExemptByFile` keeps the whole-file exemption a single named decision rather than a shape that spreads. The scan also unwraps a parenthesised receiver and follows a bound method value (`f := s.db.ExecContext`), both of which reached SQLite with no selector at the call site; it tells a transaction from a statement the other way round, following `*sql.Tx` parameters, the values a tx-returning function hands back, and the executors and prepared statements derived from one, and it treats anything it cannot PROVE is a transaction as a write — the bias is deliberate, because the cost of guessing wrong is a failure naming a line and the cost of guessing the other way is a silent hole. Exemption keys are receiver-qualified, because this package declares a free function and a method with the same bare name and a key that named both would exempt both. `pinMemoryTx` therefore takes a `*sql.Tx` rather than the `sqlExecutor` interface that also admits a handle. The check runs AFTER `BeginTx` and THROUGH the transaction, which is what makes it race-free rather than merely likely: the DSN asks for BEGIN IMMEDIATE, so the write lock is already held, and a migration is itself a write that needs the same lock — the window is closed, not small. It is not a property a test can pin, and the variant that looks equivalent is wrong twice over: a pre-BEGIN read leaves the gap (and on this `MaxOpenConns(1)` pool it DEADLOCKS rather than racing, since the open transaction holds the only connection the probe would need). READS keep working, because refusing them would turn "restart your client" into "you cannot search"; a `ReadOnly` transaction is exempt for the same reason and the scan says so explicitly. The cost is one `PRAGMA user_version` per write and THERE IS NO CACHE, which is a decision rather than an omission: the pragma that looks like a change counter, `PRAGMA data_version`, is a per-CONNECTION counter rather than a property of the file, so on a connection the pool replaced underneath a long-lived server it can carry the value the previous one had (measured: two handles on one file both report 2, and a brand-new database also reports 2), and a cache keyed on it answers "unchanged" for exactly the stale write it was meant to prevent. What it saved was a memory read of a page the open transaction already holds. A probe that FAILS refuses the write and says which probe failed: an unreadable version is not a licence to write, and not a licence to claim the store is newer either, since that claim sends an operator hunting for a build that may not exist. `ErrStoreNewer` names both versions and says to restart the CLIENT rather than to upgrade, because the binary on disk may already be current and the running process is the stale half — an operator told to upgrade runs `ghost upgrade`, watches it report "already up to date", and learns nothing
- Project shape is refused where a project is CREATED, by the exporter's own predicate (#824): `memory.CheckImportedProject` is the ONE rule for a project's id, name and path, and it is asked at both store creation routes (`ensureProjectLocked`, behind `EnsureProject`/`EnsureProjectWithRepo`, and `ResolveOrCreateRepoProject` → `createRepoProjectTx`) as well as at `mcpserver.ensureProjectFor`. Both store routes and the MCP route exist because there are three of them — a path-shaped `project_id` with a detectable remote takes the repository route instead of `EnsureProject` — so the second and third are the ones a fix on the first misses. The check is on CREATION and nothing else, which is harder to honour than "ask the predicate on the way in" because NONE of the three routes is reached with the caller's own argument: `ensureProjectFor` resolves that argument by exact id, then basename name, then longest path prefix, and hands the RESOLVED id down, so a legacy project with a hostile id and an ordinary recorded PATH is addressed by its path every day and arrives at the store carrying the hostile id. Judging the record above that resolution refuses a save into a project that exists and holds the user's memories — "invalid project_id" for the project the session is working in. So at the MCP boundary the check follows the FULL resolution — not just the exact-id lookup, because a project can hold a refused character in a field the write boundary does not own (`ghost project bind` is the only writer of `projects.path`, and since #840 it asks `CheckImportedProjectText("path", …)` on the path **about to be recorded** — never on the stored one, so a project that already records such a path stays writable and stays repairable by binding it to a directory that passes. Before that it asked only `storedPathIsUsable`, i.e. whether the path is absolute, and «, » and a backtick are all legal directory names, so a clean id could sit above a hostile recorded path that the session's own directory resolves to) — and at BOTH store routes it sits on the arm about to `INSERT`, in the same `beginWrite` transaction as the `INSERT` it gates (an existence check outside the transaction is a check-then-write another process can interleave into). The refresh arm is then not judged, and that is safe rather than merely convenient: the upsert's `ON CONFLICT(id) DO UPDATE` touches only `path`, `repo_remote` and `updated_at`, it keeps the stored `path` whenever the caller passed the id in its place (which is what `ensureProjectFor` does, so a hostile id is never written back), and `repo_remote` is normalized and is not part of the predicate. `createdProject` is the one place that judges the record as it would be STORED (`ensureProjectLocked` normalizes an empty path to the id, because `projects.path` is UNIQUE and MCP callers pass `path=""`). The MCP refusal names the refused `project_id` through `assemble.Token`, which the store's does not: the store's message is also the export report's, where quoting the caller's text is noise and a hazard, so it says THIS SENTENCE does not repeat the value rather than claiming the value is never shown — one message serving two surfaces has to be true on both. Both guards are gated on that one question, including the credential one: a bound path carrying a token must not make a project unwritable by the address a session uses. The credential guard is asked a SECOND time on the resolution's own error path, because `ResolveProject`'s two ambiguity refusals interpolate the caller's argument (`"%q matches multiple projects"`, `"%q has tied path-prefix matches"`) and that sentence reaches the tool answer — so the rule is not "ask before the resolve" but "ask at every point where the argument can become an answer", which is the only form of it that survives the resolve being hoisted. The repository refusal needs no such treatment because it names a `NormalizeRepoRemote`d remote. Naming is bounded by what a value can survive, so on the CREATION path the credential guard is asked BEFORE the shape predicate (`memory.RejectSecret`, the one `rejectSecret` seam under an exported name) and a `*SecretContentError` is returned untouched: a path-shaped `project_id` carrying a token is an ordinary agent mistake, and appending the value to the one message that says Ghost never stores credentials would relocate the secret. Asking FIRST rather than branching on the returned error is what closes the gap, because the predicate judges SHAPE before credentials and a value that is both returns a shape error with the credential behind it. A SPACE and any LENGTH stay allowed, because a project id is routinely a filesystem path; `TestProjectCreationStillAcceptsAPathShapedID` is what holds that against an over-eager fix. A project row the write boundary will no longer create can still be in a store — a pre-guard save, a `RestoreSnapshot`, a hand edit — and every renderer still has to make it safe, so those fixtures plant it in SQL
- A rendered label is bounded by the RENDERER, whatever writer produced the row (#821): `assemble.AgentLabel`, `SourceRefLabel` and `TagsLabel` each cut what they print and mark the cut, because the renderer is the one layer that cannot assume its input came from a writer that enforces a cap. `MaxRenderedTagLen` is 64, the number `mcpserver.tagMaxLen` cuts at, and the DIRECTION is the invariant: the print bound must never be TIGHTER than the write bound, or a tag the writer accepted whole and stored whole would print shortened — a display bound quietly becoming a data change. A looser print bound costs nothing, so only the direction is pinned, and it is pinned by a test (`mcpserver.TestTheRendererNeverShortensATagThisWriterAccepts`) that can see both numbers, since `tagMaxLen` is unexported in a package `assemble` imports. The cut is `clampBytes`, not a byte slice, because this bound handles exactly the untrusted non-ASCII text a writer would have refused and a cut through a multi-byte rune would put an invalid byte inside the JSON array; it happens BEFORE the marshal, so the marker sits inside the string like every other character of it, and each tag is bounded on its OWN — bounding the list as one value would have to drop or merge entries, and dropping a tag is worse than showing part of one. `boundTags` allocates only when it has to, because it runs on every row of every listing. The bound NEVER touches the stored value: it hands back a list to marshal, and the three writers that deliberately do not reach `validateTags` (`RestoreSnapshot` in SQL, `CreateFromCorpus` through `insertMemory`, `ReplaceNonManual` inheriting its source's union) keep their value whole
- A decision's tags follow the memory rule, and a tag's SHAPE is the one place this codebase says **never refuse** (#822): a tag is refused at WRITE time by `mcpserver.validateTags` on the four tools that take a tag list — `ghost_memory_save`, `ghost_save_global`, `ghost_memory_update`, `ghost_decision_record` — and NEVER on export or import, for shape or for anything else. `CheckImportedMemory` says so in its own comment, and neither it nor `CheckImportedDecision` judges a tag's characters; the silence on SHAPE is the point rather than an omission: `ghost export` applies the importer's own predicates, so a tag shape refused at import drops the decision AND the companion memory `RecordDecision` wrote beside it out of every artifact, on the day the operator needs the backup, over a label the user cannot even see. Nothing refused such a tag for the whole life of the feature, so any store written through it can hold one and the tags are carried byte for byte in both directions. Safety is the renderer's (`TagsLabel`, and `internal/reflection`'s larger class in the consolidation prompt), and a legacy decision carrying `["«urgent»"]` round-trips whole. A CREDENTIAL-shaped tag is the one thing this never covered is not extended to, and that is the second half of the same rule rather than an exception to it: `CheckImportedMemory` and `CheckImportedDecision` each ask `rejectSecretList("tags", …)`, so a token in EITHER tags column is refused on import and, because the exporter calls the same predicate, named and left out of every artifact (#835). The distinction is the question being asked — a character is a fact about a RENDERING and the renderer already neutralises it, while a credential is a live value that the next export copies into a file about to be pasted somewhere — and it is why an ordinary tag list still round-trips byte for byte and a shape still never costs you the row
- Project lookup: canonical path-prefix match (longest wins) OR exact repository remote OR basename name fallback — a basename match needs exactly one surviving candidate, a recorded path containing the session's directory, and no remote conflict; ambiguous matches are errors, a name-shaped input skips location checks, and a project with no recorded location is reached by name only. A path-shaped MCP save detects the checkout's Git remote and may bind it to one uniquely named, unclaimed project, but a unique name is the weakest evidence available: a candidate that records a usable path binds only when that path contains the saving directory, so an unrelated clone named after the project cannot take over its remote. A refused binding is reported to the save caller — the store returns it beside the project the memory was written to, and the save result names what kept the name and where the memory went — because routing the save is right and losing the context of a same-named project silently is not. Only the recorded-checkout refusal is repairable and it takes `ghost project merge` then `ghost project bind`, in that order: the merge drops the repository with the row it came from and the bind is refused while the other project still records that directory, so a merge alone re-splits on the next save, and a bind alone loses the memory just saved. A path-prefix match routes a save from inside a checkout to the project that encloses it but may not identify the repository for it: a save identifies the enclosing project only when the saving directory IS its recorded root or reports the same remote, so a nested checkout — a submodule, a vendored repository — never lends its remote to the project around it, while an ordinary subdirectory of the same repository still does. The session's remote is derived at most once per resolve, and only by the steps that read it
- Global memories: `_global` project, included in every project's context
- Source provenance: `manual` marks direct user material; `builtin` marks Ghost-shipped pinned rules and is never presented as user-authored
- Credential guard: no write path stores a credential value. Stored content is embedded, replayed into every later session's context, mirrored to the Obsidian vault, and quoted into the next `reflect`/`resolve`/`supersede` prompt, so anything saved is already copied to a third-party model. `memory.rejectSecret` is the one seam (`internal/memory/secret_guard.go`), called before any statement by every path that writes caller-supplied text — `UpsertWithOptions` (so `Upsert`, `UpsertWithProvenance`, both save tools, the Claude import), `UpdateMemory`, `RecordDecision` (each `alternatives` entry and each `tags` entry too), the three task writers, `UpdateLearnedContext`, and the four portable importers (`ImportProject`'s name and path included — `repo_remote` is not guarded because `NormalizeRepoRemote` strips the userinfo) — and returns `*SecretContentError`/`ErrSecretContent` naming the field and the format, never the value. The importers place the guard in a narrow window: after the id-presence check, so a record already in the store stays a skip and the portable format's re-run-is-always-safe promise holds; before the `apply=false` early return, so a dry run classifies a record exactly as the apply run it previews would. `internal/secret` decides by value SHAPE, never by the words around it: a memory store's ordinary vocabulary is "rotate the password quarterly" and "the access token lives in the runner env", and a keyword heuristic refuses both, which is why `internal/reflection`'s `looksLikeSecret` (a scope decision — is this safe to replay into every project — never a save decision) stays separate. Its rules whose verdict needs the matched text walk every candidate, not the leftmost one, or a `${DB_PASSWORD}` placeholder early in the save shadows a real credential later in it. An assigned credential needs BOTH a key that names a secret (split on `_ - .` and camelCase, so `existingSecret`, `token_url` and `token_sha` are references, not fields) AND a value that is not a reference: no dotted identifier path (every credential encoding excludes `.`, so `opts.OAuthClientSecretFromVault` names a place), no template, no code expression, no single-case word segment, and a length/entropy bar AND a value that is credential material (not a template, not a URL, no single-case word segment, and a length/entropy bar) — a Helm chart, a Grafana provisioning file and a CI workflow are mostly about credentials and record the NAME of one, never the value. Reflection drops rather than refuses, and the drop sits at the WRITE BOUNDARY (`applyReflection`), not in the tier: `ApplyReflection` replaces a corpus in one transaction, so a refusal would roll back the whole round. It must also be after the drop guard's audit — the audit asks which inputs the output failed to account for, and executeOps emits every unclaimed input verbatim, so a memory the tier carried forward is byte-identical to the output that carried it; filtering earlier makes that input look unaccounted for, and it is then either re-added verbatim (the drop is a no-op, after the audit printed its content to stderr) or silently deleted by `ReplaceNonManual` with no `--allow-drops`. At the boundary it is still accounted for, so `RetainGuardedDrops` does not re-add it and the drop is not a no-op; it IS deleted from the store, because `ReplaceNonManual` removes every replaceable row the emitted set no longer accounts for — and the report describes the mechanism without claiming a count, only when a project replace actually ran, since a drop that empties the set writes nothing — in which case the round reports `Applied: nothing`, names the surviving row, and records NO skip fingerprint, so `--skip-unchanged` does not skip the project forever. `applyReflection` returns `applied` for that distinction, and `dropCredentialProposals` returns fresh slices and `applyReflection` returns those post-drop slices, because the caller lists and counts them. `previewContent` in `internal/reflection` withholds a credential from the three tier log lines, which run before `cmd/ghost` sees anything. `--allow-drops` does not gate it — that flag authorises what the MODEL dropped. Every print site in `ghost reflect` goes through `displayProposal`, so a proposal or a guarded drop that holds a credential is shown as `<withheld: format, category, bytes>` rather than as 120 characters of it. A DECISION'S TAGS FOLLOW THE MEMORY RULE ON EVERY PATH, which took a gap to reach (#835) and the gap is the instructive part: `RecordDecision` refused a credential-shaped tag from #656 and `CheckImportedDecision` did not, because a decision's tags are judged in a SHARED PREDICATE rather than in a writer's body — and a guard inside a shared function has no call site at the writer to grep for, so `secret_guard.go` counted the two portable importers as one and claimed "all five" tag-bearing writers while the decision route was unguarded. The reach is now a SET of six, counted from the predicates by `credentialFields()` rather than asserted: Create, `UpsertWithOptions`, `UpdateMemory`, `RecordDecision`, `ImportMemory` and `ImportDecision`, and `TestEveryTagBearingWriterIsGuarded` is the table. The four clauses, which a memory's tags and a decision's tags now satisfy identically: at WRITE, `rejectSecretList("tags", …)` refuses with the field named and the value withheld; at IMPORT, `CheckImportedMemory`/`CheckImportedDecision` ask it inside the narrow window above, so a DRY RUN classifies the record as the apply run will; at EXPORT, `portable.Export` calls the same predicate and prints a `!` line naming the field with `SkippedRecord.Secret` set, so the decision is named rather than silently absent from every artifact; and an ordinary tag list round-trips BYTE FOR BYTE, because the guard refuses a credential SHAPE and not a tag. What is deliberately still NOT checked on either predicate is a tag's SHAPE (#822): a character is a fact about a RENDERING, and a shape refusal at import would put an otherwise-valid decision outside every backup for a label the user cannot even see. So `tags` is the one column in the export report's advice whose fix depends on the RECORD KIND — `ghost_memory_update` edits a memory's tags and cannot reach a decisions row — and `secretUnfixableRoutes` names the field on the decision sentence for that reason.
- The same rule on the way OUT, and it is a RENDERER rather than a second check (#839): `memory.ProjectArg(field, value)` is how a caller-supplied project argument enters a refusal sentence, and it returns exactly `%q` for a value the guard does not recognise — so every ordinary refusal stays byte-identical to the one it replaces, which is the whole diagnostic (`project "foo" not found` is how an agent finds its own typo) — and a placeholder naming the argument and the credential's FORMAT, and no part of the value, for one it does. It is a RENDERER and not a coverage claim: a new refusal that quotes a project argument has to route through it, and a package that interpolates the operand with `%q` is a hole in this rule, not an exception to it (`internal/mcpinit`'s two lifecycle refusals were exactly that until #844's third round, and the renderer's own docstring had started claiming a surface no call site used). It lives beside `rejectSecret` because the store and the handlers owe the same sentence: `ResolveProject`'s refusals are handed back by nineteen handlers as `resolve project: %w`, so a rendering per call site would be nineteen chances to get one wrong, and a rendering that is a CHECK would be a second opinion to disagree with the first. The label is the argument's own name where the boundary can see it (`project_id` for most tools, `project` for `ghost_resolve`, and the CLI's own `<project>` operand — a refusal naming a field the reader never typed is the failure) and `memory.ProjectIdentifierLabel` where the STORE cannot see it. Two different sentences get the neutral label, for two different reasons, and neither is a special case of anything: the AMBIGUITY refusals are the store's on EVERY surface, because every handler hands `ResolveProject`'s error back verbatim, so `ghost_resolve` is held to the neutral label for its ambiguity refusal even though its own not-found sentence says `project`; and `ghost_project_delete` is held to it for its not-found refusal because the tool writes no sentence of its own — it hands `args.Project` to `Store.DeleteProject`, equally reached by the CLI's `ghost project delete <name-or-id>` — with the tool name in the wrapping `ghost_project_delete: ` error telling the agent which argument to change. Knowing the field is necessary but not sufficient to be allowed to use it, and `mcpserver`'s surface sweep holds both cases explicitly: `storeAmbiguityLabel` for the ambiguity shapes, and a `storeRefusal` flag for the one unknown-project sentence the store builds. Rendering is not withholding: `assemble.Label` neutralises characters for a line, it does not keep secrets out of one, so the bind refusal's directory is named through it and the bind refusal's CREDENTIAL is not named at all. `ghost_memory_save` is the exception that proves the rule — it already answers with the guard's own refusal rather than a rendered one, because there the credential IS the finding and there is no ambiguity to explain.

- Four deliberate exclusions from this guard: `CreateFromCorpus`, `RestoreSnapshot`, `SeedGlobalMemories` and `ReplaceNonManual` are outside both it and `MaxContentLen`. `CreateFromCorpus` exists because a public benchmark corpus contains real leaked credentials (measured: three in longmemeval-s) and a retrieval benchmark cannot refuse to ingest them; it is off `provider.MemoryStore`, off the MCP surface, and reachable only from the three `bench/` dataset seeders. The first two write byte-exact data into a restored store, and dropping a row from a restore is worse than the leak. `ReplaceNonManual` is excluded because filtering there DELETES the stored row — an unattended nightly reflect would silently remove a credential from a pre-guard database with no report — and because `dropCredentialProposals` in `cmd/ghost/promotion.go` already drops a credential-shaped proposal at the write boundary, after the drop guard's audit — where the stored row goes with it, since the row is the value. Detecting what is ALREADY in a database is still a separate report-first job, not a side effect of consolidation. A new caller-supplied text writer MUST call `rejectSecret` first
- Reflection promotion: cross-project candidates stay project-scoped by default; `ghost reflect --promote-globals` opts into `_global`, and the project replacement plus global writes share one transaction
- Reflection drop guard: every one of the eight categories is audited for a surviving merge target, not just the operational ones — an input under 45% token containment in the output it is compared against is re-added verbatim (`internal/reflection/drops.go`, `AuditGuardedDrops` + `RetainGuardedDrops`), and `ghost reflect --allow-drops` accepts the deletions instead. It is not the `manual` source that covers this: seeds are written `builtin`, agent saves `mcp` and reflection writes `reflection`, so nothing an agent writes is ever excluded from consolidation by that filter. With the per-id pass-through the guard no longer rescues omissions, and what it still covers is narrow: a MERGE's sources (`ReflectionResult.Merges`, carrying the ids it folded in AND the text it produced, scored against the text of THAT merge), an explicit `obsolete` drop, and the SQLite tier's absorbed duplicates (it names no ids, so all of its inputs are measured the strict way). A merge source is scored against its own merge and not against the union of the outputs, which #659 chose when the union was a handful of survivors and which the pass-through made into the whole project's vocabulary: every id the response never named is emitted verbatim, so a source whose substance its merge discarded passed containment on the strength of an unrelated memory sharing its words — the same class of loss the pass-through exists to remove, reintroduced through the merge branch (#549). The merge's own text is the whole witness available, because a merge source is consumed by its merge (its own text is never in the result) and the parser rejects an id claimed twice (so a sibling cannot be there either); scoring against the siblings could only LOWER the bar. A merge that is not in the result has no witness and its sources fall back to the strict per-output test. THERE IS NO EXEMPTION. Not for a rewrite and not for a supersession: **an unattended reflect never deletes a memory on the model's say-so alone.** A `rewrite <id> -> <text>` or a `drop <id> reason: superseded by <id>` is audited exactly like an `obsolete` drop — the corpus has to be able to SHOW the row is gone. `ReflectionResult.Replacements` still RECORDS the ids a response disposed of and the text it says took their place, because that is the only place the response's own account of a disposal is visible to a reader of a result, but nothing acts on it. The carve-out this replaced was the hole: a supersession disposed of a row whenever the named successor's text was in the result, and the parser's only rule is that the target survives the response — so naming a neighbour was a valid operation and two ordinary deployment notes in the same project, paired because they sat adjacent in the prompt, was a silent deletion with no warning, no `--allow-drops` and a zero exit status. Requiring the witness to be RELATED closed that, and then turned out to be redundant: the witness text is itself an output, so a row at 45% containment against it also passes the per-output scan. The whole branch was dead code. The rule is KEEP-biased on purpose, and the reason is repairability: a stale row that is KEPT can be demoted, because the lifecycle runs `resolve` and `supersede` immediately after `reflect` and demoting a genuinely stale row is their job, while a deleted row is gone. So the failure direction is a duplicate — the old text back beside its replacement — never a silent deletion. The cost, named: the "three issues are still open" class comes back as a kept stale row whenever the successor is reworded below the containment bar (measured: this repo's own superseded note about the Anthropic API client scores 0.429 against its replacement), and stays visible until resolve or supersede demote it, so a corpus of those grows the input each pass has to read. That is the right way round for a command that rewrites a memory store with no human in the loop. Because a re-added verbatim memory reaches `ReplaceNonManual` through the exact-content reuse path, retention must not be treated as consolidation: `reusePreservesAge` (`internal/memory/store.go`) keeps the stored `created_at` and `source` when the re-emission matches the row's content and category — for every verbatim re-emission, not only retention: a `keep`, a pass-through and the SQLite tier's unmerged (always verbatim) output keep their age too, so decaying categories now age out of injection instead of being refreshed by each applied reflect — and omitting a stale memory cannot restart its decay or relabel its source, while a genuine rewrite (new text, or the same text in another category) still gets `created_at = now` and `source = 'reflection'`. That decision is only as good as the row it is made against, so the pick is deliberate too: `takeReusableRow` prefers the candidate whose category matches the emission (a project can hold one text under several categories, since Upsert keeps the incoming category on a linked copy) and falls back to the first, and the candidate query is `ORDER BY created_at, id` so a duplicate keeps the oldest age deterministically rather than whichever row the planner happened to return
- Reflection operation contract: the LLM tier returns `{"learned_context", "ops"}` where each op is `keep <id>` / `merge <id>,<id> -> <text>` / `rewrite <id> -> <text>` / `drop <id> reason: obsolete|superseded by <id>` on the ids the prompt renders (`internal/reflection/ops.go`), because the retired free-text contract had no vocabulary for "this row is fine": only a byte-identical re-emission kept an identity, so retyping — including retyping to improve — reset the id, and with it the embedding, the link graph (both ON DELETE CASCADE) and the age (measured: 12 of 13 new ids in one project, 15 of 17 in another). A memory leaves the corpus only when its id is named as a merge source, named for a rewrite, or named in a drop with a reason — AND a surviving output accounts for it (the drop guard's 45% token containment, with a merge source measured against its OWN merge) or `--allow-drops` accepts the deletion. Naming an id is NECESSARY BUT NOT SUFFICIENT: a rewrite whose replacement does not carry the old row's substance leaves that row in the corpus verbatim, so a rewrite is not a free change of wording on the unattended path (#549). EVERY OTHER INPUT IS CARRIED THROUGH VERBATIM by `executeOps` — the pass-through that makes the tier zero-loss, and the reason the token guard is not load-bearing for omissions: an unnamed memory was emitted by nobody, so it survived only if the guard FAILED to recognise a survivor, which made a false positive in the "absorbed" direction a silent deletion with no warning and no `--allow-drops` (measured: "SSH to the bastion goes through port 2222, not 22" gone, because an unrelated survivor shared 45% of its tokens). A guard can only re-add what it flags, so an id the harness never mentioned needs no inference at all. `keep` and the pass-through emit the stored row's own content, category, importance and tags so the exact-content reuse updates it in place; a merge or rewrite derives its category from the first id, its importance from the strongest source, its tags from the union, and its scope from `inferGlobalScope` — the model states none of them, and an unattended model choosing `global` moves knowledge into every project's injected context. An operation id may echo the prompt's own `id:` label, which is stripped rather than failing the pass. Everything else is strict, because a partially-understood operation list is a corpus rewritten as if the model had said something else: an unreadable line, an id Ghost did not feed the model (compared case-insensitively, never by prefix), an id claimed twice, a supersession whose target the same response does not carry forward, or a response with no `ops` array at all each fail the whole response so the tiered consolidator falls through to SQLite. A rejection costs ONE extra call, not the pass: the LLM tier re-reads the same prompt once with the reader's own complaint attached, and the second answer is read under the same strict rules — a re-read, not a repair, so there is still no fuzzy id matching, no prefix completion and no application of the answer just rejected (the pass-through above is what makes discarding it lossless). Two rejections fail the run with the SECOND complaint; a transport failure is not a rejection and is never retried here; the first complaint is logged at WARN and the turn is reported as `repair: 1` on the `Result:` line, which IS the append-only lifecycle.log on the unattended path, so a benchmark counts the token once — against the `Result:` lines, with the WARN line the record for a run that repaired and then failed outright (#689). The complaint has TWO renderings because the line it quotes is model prose over stored memory: `Error()` carries the clipped operation line for the person and the repair prompt, `opRefusal.Safe()` withholds it for the log and keeps the reason and the id — the same reason the three other lines the tier writes go through `previewContent`. TWO log sites use it: the tier's own WARN (`readerComplaintForLog`, fail-closed) and the tiered wrapper's `consolidator failed, trying next tier` (`safeTierError`, which redacts a refusal but passes a HARNESS failure through unchanged, because "signal: killed" is worth telling apart from a refused answer) — and on a manual `auto` run that wrapper's line lands in the log with no failure anywhere to explain it. TWO things are deliberately NOT redacted, and do not read the seam as a guarantee: a harness failure is not value-free either (internal/ai builds it from the child's own output, and opencode's stdout stream carries the model's `text` events — pre-existing and #540's deliberate trade, since the child's explanation is what made those failures diagnosable), and `runReflect` still prints the full refusal on its `error: consolidation failed:` line, which on the unattended path is lifecycle.log, the phase tail and the next session's maintenance alert (300 bytes of it). Every model-supplied fragment interpolated into a complaint is clipped at that point (`clipOpText`), or a `drop <id> reason: <a paragraph>` puts a paragraph into a prompt, a log and an error message — and THAT CLIP IS ALSO WHERE THE VALUE GATE RUNS: SIX reasons quote a model fragment (parseOpLine's unreadable drop tail and unknown verb; executeOps' unknown id, unknown superseded-by target, doubly-claimed id, and not-carried-forward supersession), all six routed through `clipOpText` at six call sites and eight invocations, enumerated in the source so the list can be audited against the code. That gate probes ALL THREE SPELLINGS (as written, lower-cased, upper-cased), and the two folds are what cover the case-SENSITIVE rules, which split by literal case — `gh[pousr]_` is lower, `AKIA|ASIA|ABIA|ACCA`+16 and `AGE-SECRET-KEY-1…` are upper. All three are reachable and each hides a token from the others: the parser upper-cases a supersession target (hiding a `ghp_…` from an as-written-only probe) while the free-form drop tail is probed in the model's own spelling (hiding a lower-cased `AKIA…` from a lower-fold-only probe, and folding that down is a no-op). The as-written probe is not redundant either — it is the ONLY one that reaches the mixed-case literals below, which is why `parseOpLine` keeps a raw copy of the verb instead of gating the folded one: lower-casing a token before the gate sees it turns a mixed-case literal into a shape no probe recognises. A stored id is safe here for two separate reasons, both from the detector's own constants: 32 hex characters is under the two bare-hex floors (`cardanoKeyMinRun` 68, `longHexFloor` 132) though above `assignedSecretFloor` (20), and it carries no provider prefix and no `key: value` assignment, which is what every remaining rule needs. `TestClipOpTextStillGatesARealId` pins that. KNOWN RESIDUAL, not closed: the MIXED-case literals (google-api-key `AIza…`, pypi `pypi-AgEIcHlwaS5vcmc…`, JWT `eyJ…`, PuTTY `PuTTY-User-Key-File-`) match ONLY the as-written probe, so a re-spelled one (`aizasyd-…`) is caught by no fold — closing THAT would mean lower-casing the rules in internal/secret, which changes what they match everywhere including the write boundary, so it is a change to that package, not to this probe set. Each of the three probes has a test that fails when it is removed, and `TestReaderComplaintGatesAMixedCaseLiteral` covers the as-written probe and the raw verb together rather than describing one, and a `drop <id> reason: <credential>` is refused BECAUSE the tail is free-form, so the value is in the reason. `clipOpText` therefore applies the same `secret.Detect` gate `previewContent` does, before the clip; a stored id is 32 chars (`hex(randomblob(16))`) so 60 never truncates a real one. A rewrite fixture cannot catch this class — its replacement text is inside the line that IS withheld. `ReflectionResult.RepairTurns` is per RUN, not per tier: `TieredConsolidator.Consolidate` carries a discarded tier's repair turns onto whichever result survives, so a run that repairs and then falls through to SQLite is still visible. The grounding check (`internal/reflection/grounding.go`) is the other half: a merge or rewrite that introduces a path, hash, version, hostname or number found in none of its source ids is REJECTED and its sources are emitted unchanged — the output is never repaired, because Ghost cannot know which side of a corrupted identifier (`2.BeXIAhbj.js` vs `2.BeXIAhbq.js`) is true, and a wrong specific is stored as fact forever. That is also why a `rewrite` fixes a CLAIM and never a specific: the corrected value would be an identifier the source does not contain, so the prompt asks for `keep` (or `drop`) instead and the check is never asked a question it must refuse. Ordinary prose is not an identifier: a merge that needs a new connective word is a legitimate merge, and a bare proper noun the model misspells is outside these classes
- Reflection input accounting: `ghost reflect` prints a section after the result list that accounts for EVERY input id (#684), identical for a dry run and for `--apply` and printed BEFORE the write so a dry run previews it exactly. The invariant is a partition: each input id appears in exactly one line or one count and EVERY count is a count of ids — a merge names any number of sources, so `Merges (3)` over one three-source merge is right and the count of operations is not — so an operator (or an independent judge) can add the numbers up to the input total, and the test asserts the quoted ids plus the two counts ARE the input set with no id twice AND that the section's own headers sum to the input total. The one place a line quotes an id it does not account for is a `superseded by` reason, which names the successor that replaced the row: that successor is accounted for by its own bucket, so the accounting is per input id and not per mention. It exists because the report used to list only what the corpus BECAME — an input a merge consumed vanished from it, so a merge and a loss printed identically, and the applied run recorded the answer in `memory_history` while the preview showed none of it. The truth lives in `reflection.AccountInputs` (`internal/reflection/accounting.go`), a PURE function of the result, because the records about a round belong beside the round; `reportInputAccounting` (`cmd/ghost/reflect_accounting.go`) only formats, and is a named function because `runReflect` calls `os.Exit`. A merge prints `new <- <ids> (<bytes> B from <bytes> B)` and NOT a successor id: the row does not exist until the apply writes it, and `memory_history`'s `related_id` is where the identity becomes visible, so the section cannot name an id that is not knowable yet and a dry run is still an exact preview. The buckets are the result's OWN records, which is why three of them had to be added: `Kept` (an explicit `keep`, otherwise indistinguishable from the pass-through), `Drops` (every explicit drop with its reason — an `obsolete` drop names no successor, so before this the result said NOTHING about the id it disposed of) and `Refusals` (a grounding-check rejection with the ids it kept and the identifiers that caused it, which used to be a log line inside the tier). Like `Replacements`, all three are RECORDS: nothing acts on them, and the drop guard still audits every disposed row on its own evidence. The sixth bucket, **deleted**, is the set of rows an apply REMOVES, one line each with its reason, and it is counted by the replace's own reuse pass rather than by whether the row's text is still in the result. `ReplaceNonManual` reuses stored rows by byte-identical content and claims ONE per emission — the row in the same category, else the oldest, which is why the claim is re-implemented in `claimedRows` over `(created_at, id)` — and deletes every candidate left in the bucket, so two inputs holding the same bytes both have their text in the result and only one is still there afterwards. Keying on the text called BOTH of them carried, which is this report's own defect pointed the other way (#684 review, a blocker). The two reasons are different outcomes: a row nothing carries is a loss, a row whose identical twin was reused is a deduplication whose knowledge is still in the project, and collapsing them is the confusion the section exists to remove. Every id a line prints is the STORED spelling, resolved through the input map rather than echoed from the record: `memIDKey` compares case-insensitively so a response may spell an id any way, and the parser normalises only a drop's successor target (a stored id is `hex(randomblob(16))`, which SQLite renders UPPER-case — measured on a real store, not read off the schema).
