A guard verdict annotates the line that named the id, by COUNT rather than by id (the line already names them, and a second mention would break the one-account contract), and says `re-added` or `--allow-drops accepts the deletion` so a drop line cannot be read as a deletion that did not happen. `--full` removes the 120-byte preview everywhere in the report through one `reflectTextLimit` seam, is display-only, and is deliberately not on the unattended lifecycle path whose stdout is an append-only log
- Hybrid search: 70% vector (cosine, Ollama) + 30% FTS5, RRF fusion — window selection is `Store.fuseAndRank` delegating to `selectWindow` (scope narrowing, keyword reservation, final cut), which every production search reaches including the FTS-only degenerate path; `FuseAndSelectWindow` is the exported entry point over the same seam, called only from the fusion tests. Explain mode does not RE-DERIVE any of it: `SearchParams.trace` (an unexported `*searchTrace`, nil everywhere but `ExplainSearchScoped`) is written by the stages themselves — the leg ranks and the vector cosine in `fuseCandidatePool`, the scope verdict in `scopeEligiblePool` (for the DROPPED candidates too, which is the structural fix for #571: explain reports the decision window selection made, so the two cannot disagree), the fused base and the status factor in `demoteStatus`, the two sides of the one multiplication (the product is not recorded — it is exactly that product, and the payload note tells the reader to perform it), the reservation exchange in `selectWindow` (both the promoted row and the one whose slot it took), the decay factor and the CLOCK in `decayRank`, the floor verdict in `searchHybridLegs` where the floor is applied, and the penalty counts plus their attributed counterpart ids in `supersedeVerdicts`/`nearDuplicateVerdicts` — one edge read each, so the id explain names is the id the demotion chose. Every stage already received `p SearchParams`, which is why the trace rides on it rather than as a new argument: a separately threaded trace is one a future stage can forget to accept, and a stage that forgets is a stage whose decisions explain cannot report. `TestExplainScoreIsTheScoreTheRankingUsed` pins the property by comparing explain against `Store.Candidates`, the other entry point into the same seam. A signal the ranking does NOT apply is reported as not applied and never given a contribution to fill the field: `confidence_contribution`, `provenance_contribution` and `validity_penalty` are 0 and `provenance_weight` is `"off"`, with a note per payload saying so — a multiplier that does not exist reported as 1.0 would read as "provenance was weighed and found neutral". Validity is a reported state read through `memory.ValidityState`, the ONE rule the assembler's validity stage also delegates to (it used to hold a second copy, and two copies is two answers to "may this row be used"). The payload is bounded at 150 CANDIDATE rows (`explainMaxRows`), the tool's window ceiling being 100 and the answer never being part of the budget; the cut spends itself on excluded candidates and never on a returned row, and it is marked in a `truncation` FIELD rather than a note, because a note list is bounded from the end and a short list that looks complete is the failure the marker exists to prevent. Status demotion (`statusDemotionFactor` and the `resolvedDemotionFactor`/`globalDemotionFactor` constants in `internal/memory/demotion.go`) multiplies a resolved row's fused score — and a `_global` row's when a specific project is searched — by 0.5 inside `fuseCandidatePool`, before the cut, so it owns membership as well as order; it scales the score rather than dropping the row, so both stay searchable, and explain reports the same number as `status_factor`. With k=60 the factor effectively ranks a demoted row below every live candidate the legs fetched (a halved rank-1 two-leg score of ≈0.0082 sits under the 0.7/80≈0.0088 floor of the deepest vector row in a default window), but it only reorders what the legs fetched — each leg pulls limit*2 rows from the project plus `_global`, which counts against that budget, so a short project result set still fills with demoted `_global` rows. The keyword reservation skips rows whose status factor is below 1 (raw rank and the demoted score would otherwise disagree), and the two passive surfaces still never reach FUSION — `loadSessionPassive` (`internal/mcpinit/session_passive.go`) assembles the session-start digest and `projectContextBudget` (`internal/mcpserver/project_context.go`) backs `ghost_project_context` with the `ghost://project/{id}/context` resource and the `recall_project` prompt, both filter `resolved_at IS NULL` — so no factor reaches either. The project-context read is the ONE passive bucket that admits `_global` into a project bucket (`SlicePolicy.IncludeGlobal`), because it is the one that has always read `project_id = ? OR project_id = '_global'` under a SINGLE cap; `ghost://memories/global` reads the SAME bucket over `projectContextGlobalBudget`, which is that policy with `IncludeGlobal` unset because the bucket is already the population, and the two browse surfaces still have their own reader. `Store.GetTopMemories` has NO call site left outside its own definition and its declaration on `provider.MemoryStore`, which is where it is staying for now: it ranked and trimmed in SQL, so every surface that could have wanted it moved onto the assembler and left the stages nothing to decide. It is left rather than deleted because the interface method is not this change's to remove, and a reader who greps for it will find a live exported method with no caller — which is the accurate description of it. It will not reach fusion when the digest moves onto the seam either, and that is deliberate: the status factors scale a FUSED score, and a passive retrieval has no fused score to scale. A passive bucket states its own order instead (`decay`, or `pinned_importance_updated` for `_global`), excludes `resolved_at IS NOT NULL` in SQL, and selects over a window rather than over a fusion — so the resolved/global demotion has nothing to demote and adding it there would be a second ranking nobody measured. What the digest does share with the tool surface is the scope rule and its label: the block prints it with `assemble.ScopeLabel` — the same label a search line carries, so the two cannot spell one scope differently — and its fetch is narrowed by `injection.session_scope` inside the store's passive statement, using `memory.ScopeMatchesSQL`, the SQL statement of `memory.ScopeMatches` that the assembler applies in Go, held to the Go form by a test that runs both over the same rows. It has to be decided in SQL for the reason `scopesConflictSQL` gives: a statement that chooses its own candidate set with a `LIMIT` cannot check scope after that cut, and a passive bucket's `OverFetch` IS its candidate set. Unset, the clause is absent and the block is byte-identical to the one that shipped. The formatted `ghost_memory_search` path does not read that window directly: it asks `Store.Candidates` for a widened untrimmed set and hands it to `assemble.Run`, so the caller's category and scope are applied before the window closes (the window itself is still chosen inside fusion, unchanged) — `internal/assemble` owns every decision after retrieval, and `internal/memory` never imports it. It also owns the RELEVANCE VERDICT (#580): every block is `answerable`, `weak` or `empty` with a reason from a closed set, and the search tool's last line is machine-readable (`[ghost:outcome=… reason=… floor_fts_rank=… abstain_cosine=… candidates=… admitted=… legs=… tokens_est=…]`) with a human abstention sentence for the two non-answerable cases. `weak` withholds nothing — it labels the rows so a caller treats them as leads; the ONLY thing that removes a row for a reason other than a stage is the `response_fit` post-pass, and that is bounded by `Budget.MaxBytes` against the COMPLETE response (`Slice.MaxBytes` is item content; one field cannot bound two units). Arm A (keyword rank 0-3) is on and arm B (a vector cosine) ships OFF, because the bench no-answer report shows the two cosine distributions overlap and no constant separates them — `context.abstain_cosine` is a user's decision, and `abstain_cosine=off` on the line is how a disabled threshold is told from a cosine of zero. An arm applies to a result when it holds a VALUE, not when its leg ran: a leg that answered can have retrieved nothing, leaving every row at the -1 sentinel. A leg that FAILED suppresses the floor outright (`retrieval_partial`); a result with no arm value at all is `no_floor_arm`; a leg that never ran changes neither — the keyword arm still judges keyword-only results, and the leg's state is on the line (`legs=`, `abstain_cosine=not_applied`), never in the reason. An outage is not evidence about the corpus. An empty result whose rows were FOUND and withheld says "no sufficiently trustworthy memory found" and names the cause; only `no_candidates` claims nothing matched, and only that reason is evidence about the store. A leg that never RAN and a leg that ran and FAILED are different facts with different next steps, and the empty branch keeps them apart the way the non-empty one does: the first is `vector_backend_unavailable` (the keyword leg was all that ran), the second is a tool error rather than a verdict (an incident — retry); `legNeverRan` covers both "no embedder" and "this query would not embed", because the tool swallows the embed error and the leg status cannot tell them apart
- Vector scan: both legs are brute force over the project's embeddings, and they run in two phases (`internal/memory/vector_scan.go`, #556). `snapshotVectors` copies every candidate row out of SQLite and returns, holding the store's read lock and its single connection for the copy only; `vectorRows.search` scores the copy afterwards, so the O(corpus × dims) cosine pass — which `ghost supersede` runs once per memory — no longer blocks writers for the length of the corpus. The copy keeps the stored bytes rather than decoding them and the score loop reads the little-endian float32s back out in place (`cosineFromBytes`, bit-identical to `cosineSimilarity` on a decoded vector), and the scope column is parsed only for a row that wins a slot; a `scopeProbe` decides per-row eligibility by checking whether the stored text can mention a requested key at all, which can only save a `json.Unmarshal` and never decides membership, since `ScopeMatches` only excludes a row that names a requested key and disagrees. Ranking keeps a bounded window instead of the corpus — a min-heap whose root is the worst row held, ordered once at the end over at most `limit` rows — and the order is total (cosine descending, exact ties broken by scan position) so the answer is a function of the stored data AND the order the scan yielded it in, not of the heap's internals; that is not independence from the scan order (the query has no ORDER BY, so a VACUUM or index rebuild can reorder a tie), and it does not claim to be — what it buys is that two searches over the same rows in the same order agree, which the unstable `sort.Slice` it replaced did not promise. `TestSearchVectorRankingMatchesTheCollectAndSortBaseline` pins the whole result against a transcription of the pre-#556 collect-and-sort search, and `TestVectorSearchCopiesTheCorpusUnderTheStoreReadLock` / `TestVectorSearchScoresWithTheStoreLockReleased` pin that the copy takes the read lock and the scoring pass does not. A writer is still kept out during the copy, and that is the connection rather than the mutex: OpenDB pins the pool at one, so no amount of lock work lets a write start while a query is streaming. The scratch is recycled through a `sync.Pool` on the store, and an oversized embedding buffer is dropped on return rather than retained. What a query still allocates is the driver: `modernc.org/sqlite` copies every BLOB and TEXT column into a fresh allocation per row, which is why the remaining cost is a decoded-vector cache keyed on `PRAGMA data_version` (and then an ANN index), not more work on the scoring loop
- Vector-space identity: a stored vector is only scored when `memory_embeddings.model` equals the identity this process embeds into (`Store.SetEmbeddingIdentity`, fed from `embedding.VectorIdentity`). A model, dimension or task-prefix change therefore retires the old vectors instead of comparing them with queries from the new space. The rule is read in four places, one per caller: `loadVectorRows` (may a vector enter a search), `GetEmbedding` returning nil (may a stored vector act as a QUERY vector — the link worker and `ghost supersede` both do), `UnscannedEmbeddedMemoryIDs` (may the linker compare this memory yet), `EmbeddingStats` (is it covered, which `ghost mcp status` and `ghost_health` report). A retired vector never hides its memory: the keyword leg still finds it. Two side effects of a rewrite ride along: `StoreEmbedding` clears the row's `link_scans` slot when the identity changes (so the linker re-scans it in the new space — the re-scan ADDs current-space `related` edges alongside the ones the retired space produced; nothing invalidates those old rows, and `CreateLink`'s `MAX(strength, excluded.strength)` upsert means an edge whose new-space similarity is lower keeps its old strength), and the foreign-vector warning in `loadVectorRows` is emitted once per retired identity (a second reconfiguration in the same process warns again). `EmbeddingStats` reports stale and unembedded rows separately so `ghost mcp status` can show both. The identity is opaque (compared, never parsed) and empty means "no model configured", which keeps the bench harness and tests unfiltered
- Memory history: `memory_history` (schema v17, #578) is the append-only CHANGE LOG of how a memory reached its current state — one row per write. The name `memory_provenance` went to a different and later concept (evidence records, several per memory, answering "who or what supports this memory" — see "Evidence provenance" below), so never rename this table to it, and do not treat the two as the same thing; and every writer appends one row in the SAME transaction as the write — `Create`, `Upsert` (insert and the fold's `merge`), `UpdateMemory`, `ReplaceNonManual` (rewrite, reuse, bulk delete), `SetResolved`/`ClearResolved`/`MarkResolved` (the last recording the OPERATOR as performer, and clearing the row's KEEP cache in the same statement as the stamp — the only `resolve` row that can say a person decided rather than a classifier judged), `CreateLink` for a `supersedes` edge and `InvalidateLink` for its withdrawal (`unsupersede` — a memory can be superseded, withdrawn and superseded again, and only the sequence says which claim is live), `RestoreSnapshot`, `ImportMemory`, `Delete`, `PruneSessionMemories` (a `session` row removed by `ghost prune` — the one writer whose row is gone by design, and the reason the audit has to be a tombstone), the decision companion memory and the shipped seeds. Each row is a VERSION, not a diff: the state the memory held once that write landed, so "what did Ghost know at T" is the newest row at or before T (#647) and the prior content is the previous row. `agent`/`session_id` are the PERFORMER (the save/merge/import paths carry a Provenance; the lifecycle passes do not, and an empty field is an admission, not a claim) — which is what makes the issue's "a row changed by two different agents" answerable. `memory_id` deliberately has NO foreign key so a hard delete leaves a tombstone carrying the text the row held; `project_id` does cascade. Growth is bounded in the appending transaction: newest 50 versions per memory, newest 20 000 rows per store (rowid window, since an implicit rowid is max(rowid)+1 and never reused). A write that changes none of a memory's own recorded columns appends nothing (`Touch`, `TogglePin`, the KEEP cache), and a write that moves a memory BETWEEN projects must also move its history rows' project_id or the project-delete cascade takes the past of a memory that survived: `MergeProject` and `PromoteToGlobal` both do (the merge list exists twice on main). A verbatim consolidation re-emission is a NO-OP and appends nothing (#727: it measured at 80% of `memory_history` on a real store, and the per-memory cap was days from evicting real events) — when content, category, importance, tags and scope are all unchanged it writes no row and leaves `updated_at` alone, because `ghost supersede` orients each candidate pair by `updated_at` and `--skip-unchanged`'s fingerprint carries it as a change proxy; a change to any of those five IS a real update and is recorded (`reuseChangesNothing` compares each field by the value the side that produced it carries, an unreadable stored value counts as changed, and importance is compared at the EMISSION's float32 precision (`float32(stored) == emitted.Importance`) because Upsert's strengthen adds in the column's own float64 (with the increment itself bound as a float32) and leaves a value float32 cannot name, so a full-width comparison reported a change on every verbatim keep of a strengthened row and reinstated the flood #727 removed (#750)). TWO writers deliberately repeat the state instead, and neither is a licence to repeat: `ghost prune` appends one per removal and changes no other shape (the backup manifest counts the CORPUS, never the change log, so a tombstone cannot make a post-prune backup fail to verify), and a `supersedes` edge appends a `supersede` row when it BECOMES active, recording a claim about the target's standing rather than a state change. Both spend a slot under the cap, which is what bounds them. Two columns carry the event's other end: `related_id` (the id that replaced a deleted row, or the memory whose edge claims it) and `merged_content` (the text a FoldOnly fold dropped, which no row keeps). `ghost history purge <id>` and `ghost_memory_delete`'s `purge_history` delete a row AND its history in one transaction — the redaction path, because a plain delete keeps the text a memory used to hold — and `ghost_history_content` is the SQL seam where #656's `secret.Detect` will redact content on the way in. A purge reaches a memory that is ALREADY deleted through `Store.PurgeMemoryHistory` (history only, never the row), because the tombstone is the feature and a delete-time purge cannot cover it; the same operation is what frees an id for `ghost import`, which REFUSES an id that still has history rather than splicing two memories' records under it (ids are not reused by Ghost, but a portable artifact carries them verbatim). `migrateV17` backfills nothing, so a pre-v17 memory's first edit files a `baseline` row before it overwrites the text. Read it with `Store.MemoryHistory` (oldest first, rowid order) or `ghost history <memory-id>` `as_of` (RFC 3339) on `ghost_memory_search` / `ghost_project_context` and `--as-of` on `ghost context` is the OTHER reader, and it is the reason the rows are versions rather than diffs: `Store.MemoriesAsOf` takes each memory's newest row at or before T, drops the ones whose newest row is a `delete` tombstone or whose first row is after T, and returns the version's own `content`/`category`/`importance`/`resolved_at`/`source`/`project_id` — one statement, driven by `memory_history` rather than by `memories` (a deleted memory's tombstone is the only surviving record of it, so a read that started from the live tables would report "Ghost never knew this" for a memory it held for months), ranked `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY memory_id ORDER BY recorded_at DESC, rowid DESC)` with `rowid` as the second key because `recorded_at` is second-precision. A pre-v17 memory with no row at or before T is UNKNOWN, never guessed: it is left out of the set and counted in `AsOfSet.Unknown`, because a shorter set that says nothing about the gap reads as the whole truth — and that "no row at or before T" is TWO time-bounded tests, not one, because an unbounded `NOT EXISTS` asks "does this memory have history at all" and a pre-v17 memory the lifecycle touched after the upgrade does (only `UpdateMemory` files a baseline; every other first write appends a row read out of the memories row in the same transaction, so its text is recorded anyway, and that is why no writer needs a baseline there). The gap half also reaches the TOMBSTONES, because a pre-v17 memory deleted after T has no live row either and its gap is otherwise invisible — and that half must do TWO things, or a memory is counted twice: exclude ids that are LIVE again (because `RestoreSnapshot` reinstates a row under the id it recorded, so "tombstoned" does not imply "not live"), AND rank per memory keeping its newest tombstone (a delete → restore → delete memory holds two delete rows, and `UNION ALL` does not dedup). `CreatedAt` — the field the decay measures an age from — is the row's own when that column can answer and the version's `recorded_at` when it cannot: the row is gone (every column NULL, and an empty `created_at` reads as ~56,000 days, so a memory deleted yesterday sat at the decay floor in every listing covering the past year), or the column is LATER than T (no writer produces that, and it is refused because `ageDays` clamps a negative age at 0, making a future-dated column the most favourable value a row could carry into a ranking of the past). **`as_of` state survives `ghost history compact`, and two metadata fields do not.** For a memory that is still LIVE the answer is read from its `memories` row, so removing a redundant version changes nothing a historical read is *for* — the state columns, the supersede edges and `CreatedAt` all answer as before, and `TestCompactingRedundantVersionsLeavesTheAsOfStateAlone` compares them one at a time. Two fields do move, and only because they NAME which version answered: `VersionRecordedAt` and `VersionPhase` can describe an EARLIER version carrying the same state, so a memory that was resolved and unresolved — or folded — may read as merely saved for an instant whose reflect flood has since been compacted away. That is a documented consequence rather than an accident, and it is the price of removing the flood at all: those rows are exactly what made a past read name a reflect run as the last thing that happened to a memory. A DELETED memory is excluded from the repair instead (rule (6) above), so for one of those neither field moves and the age does not either — which is why that is a scope rule rather than a fourth retention rule. Everything else in `AsOfRow` is the current row, including the validity triple: `valid_from`/`valid_until` are the present's — [#575](https://github.com/wcatz/ghost/issues/575)'s writers ship, but the change log records no validity, so an `as_of` read takes the bounds from the current row and judges them AT T, through the one helper `memory.ValidityAt` (the assembler's stage 2 withholds on `memory.ValidityWithheld` too, and `TestValidityAtAgreesWithStageTwoAtTheRequestedInstant` holds the two to the same verdict): a row whose window had closed or not yet opened at T is withheld by `ghost_project_context` and `ghost context --as-of` exactly as `ghost_memory_search` withholds it, before the limit or cap, and `memory.AsOfValidityNote` says validity was judged at T — as are tags, scope, pin, access count and provenance, which were never versioned — `Retention` is the ONE exception and is always empty, because it postdates the change log and a version has no tier to carry (see "Retention tiers"); the statement does not even name the column, since `ghost context --as-of` opens the store read-only and cannot migrate a store that predates it. A promotion REWRITES the history rows' `project_id` (it has to, or the project-delete cascade takes a memory's past with it), so a pre-promotion instant reports a promoted memory under `_global`; that imprecision is pinned by a test rather than worked around. Nothing in the as_of path reads `memory_provenance` (v18, the EVIDENCE table): an evidence record is one OBSERVATION of a memory, not a version of it, so nothing there can be placed at an instant — a count taken now would be a present-day claim about a past memory. The counts therefore stay zero, and since a zero renders as "no recorded evidence", the as_of DISCLOSURE says they were not read rather than leaving that phrase to stand as a claim. Every statement in the read names `memory_history` or `memories` and nothing else, so a pre-v18 store answers an as_of read in full (`TestCandidatesAsOfDoesNotDependOnMemoryProvenance` drops the table and reads on). In the assembler `as_of` is a BINDING, not a filter: `Run` moves `Now` to it, so age, validity and the trace are all decided against T, and the store treats `AsOf` as authoritative over `Now` for the same reason. The retrieval is keyword-ONLY and says so in the answer, the trace and the leg status: `memories_fts` and every embedding describe CURRENT content, so an as_of search matches the query's terms against the versions' own text (through the same `ftsQueryTerms` extraction the FTS leg uses, phrase- and prefix-aware), ranks by matched terms then the same decay composite at T — not bm25, so not comparable with a current order — reports the vector leg as `not applicable` and REFUSES `vector_only` rather than downgrading it, and does not read `memory_links` at all (it records when an edge was invalidated, never the graph at T) so the supersede demotion comes from the recorded `supersede`/`unsupersede` sequence and the edge status is a fifth value, `not_applicable`, because "no edge joins two of these candidates" is a claim a retrieval that read no graph must not render. Those disclosures are `Result.Qualifiers`, not `Result.Notes`: a note list is bounded from the end and shown for the empty answer, where a qualifier is the first thing to go, and a block that is silently a past reading is read as a present one. `ghost context --as-of` runs NONE of that path's startup side effects (the Obsidian mirror, the session-count bump) — they exist because the command backs a session start, and counting a replay would move the present's session number to answer a question about the past. Restoring the store to a past state is not this; that is export/import (#586)
- A version row is REMOVABLE only when it changed nothing, and "nothing" is every column a version records. `ghost history compact` (#730) is the repair for the rows a pre-#727 build left behind — #727 stopped every applied reflection from appending a byte-identical `reflect` version per kept memory and from moving that memory's `updated_at` to the run's own time, which measured at 80% of this table on one real store — and it removes a row ONLY when it records the same state as the row before it of the same memory, in rowid order (never `recorded_at`, which is second-precision and would make a pass's own writes a coin toss), compared over **every** column a version stores: `content`, `category`, `importance`, `resolved_at`, `source`. The three lists in `internal/memory/history_compact.go` partition the table's columns into that state, the event's own columns, and the row's identity and time, and `TestCompactHistoryColumnPartitionNamesEveryColumn` reads the partition off the live table — because a column added to `memory_history` and to none of the three lists is compared by none of them, which is exactly how a state change stops being one. The comparison is `IS` and not `=`, because every state column is nullable and `=` answers NULL for NULL, so two rows both holding no `resolved_at` would read as a change: the opposite of the damage being repaired. SIX things always stay, and each for its own reason. (1) A memory's FIRST version: the only statement of what it said, with nothing to duplicate. (2) A memory's NEWEST version: the statement of what it says NOW, and `pruneHistoryTx`'s per-memory trim already declines to take it for the same reason. (3) The phases that record a CLAIM the state does not — `delete` (the tombstone, the only record a deleted memory left), `supersede`/`unsupersede` (a claim about the memory's standing, and only the sequence says which claim is live), `resolve`/`unresolve` (who decided), `merge` (the fold), `import` and `restore` (a row arrived from outside) — and the test for that is an ALLOWLIST of the ONE phase whose only content is the state it records — `reflect` — rather than an exemption list, so a phase added to the schema later is not deletable until somebody classifies it. `save`, `update` and `baseline` record a state and nothing else and are deliberately NOT in it: `UpdateMemory` appends an `update` version on EVERY edit with no no-op guard, and a tags-only edit is a real change to what the memory says about itself that this table cannot see, because it has no column for tags. `TestCompactHistoryLeavesATagsOnlyEditAndItsStamp` drives two tags-only edits through the real writer and pins that both versions and the stamp they moved survive. (4) Any row carrying `related_id` or `merged_content`: such a row is a thread a reader follows from one memory's history into its successor's (#648), and it records a claim about ANOTHER memory. (5) Any row recorded at or after the BOUND on the repair, `reflectNoOpCutoff` — the instant #727 reached main (`d60aa301`, `2026-09-28 17:14:07` UTC), overridable by `--before <2006-01-02 | RFC 3339>` and resolved by the exported `ResolveCompactCutoff`. (6) EVERY version of a memory whose NEWEST version is a `delete` tombstone — a SCOPE rule, not a fourth retention rule, and it is a separate conjunct (`tombstonedMemorySQL`) beside the newest-version guard in `historyRemovableRowSQL` rather than a phase, because a tombstone restates the state the row held immediately before the DELETE and so is byte-identical to its predecessor exactly as a no-op `reflect` is. A deleted memory has no `memories` row, so `CreatedAt` falls back to the ANSWERING version's `recorded_at` and removing a version from it CHANGES THE AGE a past read computes — for a memory nobody can edit and nobody can restore. It is stable under the deletion, which is what the fixed-point argument needs: the conjunct reads the memory's newest version and no removable row is ever the newest, so removing rows cannot promote a different one into being newest and cannot un-retire a memory. The cost is nil by construction: a memory's history is frozen the moment it is deleted, so the flood is never written to again. The live memory beside it is still repaired, which is what `TestCompactHistoryLeavesADeletedMemorysHistoryAlone` runs — along with a count through `historyRemovableLikeSQL` showing the two rows are otherwise in reach, because a tombstone rule that is not load-bearing proves nothing. The comparison is a STRING comparison on `recorded_at`, which is exact for the layout every writer produces and for the whole-day form `date()` leaves behind (a whole-day value sorts before any stamp with a time on that day, which is right for a row that old), and and the bound's comparison is NOT a licence to skip an unreadable `recorded_at`: the comparison is a string comparison, and an unreadable value can land on either side of it — the epoch form a Unix writer left behind sorts BELOW the bound and would be read as damage, and any other malformed value sorts wherever its characters put it. So nothing in this file leans on the comparison for that. What is left alone is what the delete's own predicate never named, and `restorableStamp` reports a stamp it cannot read rather than guessing at one. The bound is LOAD-BEARING rather than belt-and-braces, because a current build still writes a byte-identical `reflect` version ON PURPOSE: a consolidation merge whose survivor is one of its own sources carries the union of that source's tags, `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch writes them, and the version it appends restates `content`, `category`, `importance`, `resolved_at` and `source` byte for byte. `TestAReflectReuseChangesTagsWithoutChangingAnyRecordedColumn` runs that path through a real sqlite consolidation tier and shows the version it files, so the claim is driven rather than argued. No state column can recognise that row, so the discriminator is a TIME: a row this build wrote is a current writer's business, and a row written before the fix shipped is this repair's. The bound reaches `--fix-updated-at`'s evidence gate for the same reason — a post-fix restatement is a deliberate change that moved `updated_at` on purpose, so it may not be read as proof that a reflection did. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a store whose clock is behind (a restored backup, a copied database) holds rows that look like damage and are not, and `--before` is how an operator says so after re-reading the dry run. The removable set is a **fixed point of the deletion** — a removable row has the same state as its predecessor, so removing it makes the row after it compare against that predecessor and reach the same answer, and it is never the newest, so removing it cannot promote another row into being newest — which is what lets the apply run in bounded `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` batches (each through `Store.beginWrite`, so #671's bounded BEGIN retry and its wait/hold measurement ride on them) and lets a dry run answer with one count and be exact rather than a sample. Guard (2) earns its place, and the case it is the ONLY answer for is a store whose clock is behind: it can hold a byte-identical `reflect` version older than the bound, and the bound alone would let the compaction take a memory's own latest statement about itself. One such row per memory, never the last. It is NOT what keeps a current build's restatement — the bound is, and a store running the lifecycle does not re-grow what was pruned because the rows it adds are all newer than any bound, so they are outside the repair entirely. `TestCompactHistoryLeavesTheRestatementACurrentWriterStillMakes` pins that with the harder half: TWO re-tag passes, so the first version is not the newest and the guard cannot be what spared it, and a full apply still removes nothing and rewinds no stamp. `--fix-updated-at` is the second repair and a separate flag, and it is gated on the SAME evidence for the same reason: a live memory's `updated_at` is restored to the `recorded_at` of the **ANCHOR**, but only where a REMOVABLE version sits **above** that anchor — that version is the only evidence a reflection run moved the stamp, and moving a stamp nothing accounts for would be the same invention #727 removed, one layer down.

**The anchor is the newest version this repair will NOT remove AND whose WRITER moved `updated_at` in the same statement that filed it** — that is, the newest KEPT row in `stampMovingPhases`. It is NOT the newest version that changed its recorded state, and the two are different questions: a `delete` or a state change records what the memory SAYS, and `stampMovingPhases` records what a writer did to the STAMP. The state-change half was this rule's first version and it was wrong in the permissive direction, which is the one that invents. `SetResolved` writes `resolved_at` and says in as many words that it leaves `updated_at` alone; `Upsert`'s unsaturated fold writes `importance` and moves nothing; `RestoreSnapshot` puts the snapshot's own text back. Any of them became the anchor, and the repair then set a memory's stamp to an instant the store had never held on that column — 49 of 288 restored stamps on one real store. Worse, a memory's FIRST version passes the state comparison trivially (a first version has no predecessor, the `max` is NULL, nothing matches, and it reads as a change), so a pre-v17 memory whose only version was a `ClearResolved` had its stamp "restored" to the moment that row was written, with seven no-op reflects above it. `TestCompactHistoryFixUpdatedAtAnchorsOnAStampMoveNotOnAStateChange` drives `SetResolved` and a real `FoldOnly` fold and pins the answer is the SAVE's instant; `TestCompactHistoryLeavesAStampWithNoRecordedStampWrite` pins the first-version case. There is nothing else to check the claim against, and that is the reason for the phase list rather than a cleverer predicate: `memory_history` records no `updated_at` at all, so a version row cannot be compared with the stamp it is supposed to account for. The only evidence a stamp write happened is the IDENTITY of the writer, and the writer is what the phase names. The deliberate-writer half is kept rather than dropped: `UpdateMemory` files a state-identical `update` version on a tags-only edit and `ReplaceNonManual`'s `reusePreservesAge` branch files a state-identical `reflect` version when a consolidation merge's survivor carries the union of its sources' tags — and both bump `updated_at` ON PURPOSE, so under a reading that could not see the writer they would be skipped and a pre-#727 no-op reflect row beneath them would become "evidence" of a rewind over a change the store had just made and recorded. `TestCompactHistoryDoesNotRewindADeliberateBumpUnderARemovableRow` runs the interleaving and pins it. The one clause of the removable predicate deliberately NOT part of the anchor is the NEWEST-VERSION guard, and the reason is arithmetic rather than judgement: a removable row is by definition not the newest, so treating the newest row as an anchor would put the anchor above every removable row, `removableLast > anchor` would never hold, and the whole flag would report 0 on a store holding nothing but the damage. A memory's newest version is the last row its last reflect run wrote, so it has to be allowed to be that run's evidence.

The anchor is ALSO not every row the repair would not remove. A `supersede`/`unsupersede` version is non-removable, and `CreateLink` writes no `memories` row at all — so as an anchor it would close the gate for that memory permanently, on the very store where the supersede came after the damage. `stampMovingPhases` is what separates the two groups, and it is **three** phases long because three writers bump the column: `save` (`Store.Create`'s INSERT default), `update` (`UpdateMemoryWithOptions`) and `reflect` (`ReplaceNonManual`'s reuse branches). Everything else files a version without touching a live memory's stamp: `MarkResolved` changes `resolved_at` and says in as many words that it deliberately leaves `updated_at` alone, `CreateLink` writes no memory row, and `Upsert`'s importance fold files `phaseMerge` with the folded text and moves nothing. `TestCompactHistoryStillRepairsAMemoryThatWasSupersededAfterwards` runs every phase outside that set and pins that none of them closes the gate. The membership is audited PER WRITER, not per phase, because the rule is that EVERY writer of a listed phase moves the column: a phase with one writer that moves it and a second that does not would make the version's own phase an unreliable witness, and the anchor would land on a row whose writer left the stamp alone — the same invention the state-change reading produced. The audit: `save` has five INSERT writers (`insertMemory`, `seedMemoryTx`, both `UpsertWithOptions` insert branches, `RecordDecision`'s companion) and every one takes the column default `datetime('now')`; `update` has one (`UpdateMemoryWithOptions`, ending `tags = ?, updated_at = datetime('now')`); `reflect` has three in `ReplaceNonManual` (the `reusePreservesAge` UPDATE, the default reuse UPDATE, and the fresh INSERT on the column default), and its `reuseChangesNothing` branch files no version at all. Nothing was removed, and nothing needed to be. `allHistoryPhases()` exists so both classifiers are checkable against the schema's own phase set rather than a hand-written list, and `TestStampMovingPhasesNamesOnlyPhasesWithAWriter` asserts a three-way partition — every phase is either in `stampMovingPhases`, or staged by `inertPhaseRows` for the loop below, or `phaseDelete` — so a phase added to the schema has to be classified in one place or two. Getting this list wrong in the permissive direction is a **silent permanent** loss of the repair: the report then reads `0 updated_at restored` about a stamp that is still a reflect run's time, which is the same wrong orientation `ghost supersede` and `--skip-unchanged` read. The `related_id` and `merged_content` clauses are not what protects those rows — their **phase** is — and no shipped writer files a `reflect` version carrying either, so a fixture that staged one would be pinning the predicate against a shape nothing emits. `phaseDelete` is the one phase `TestCompactHistoryStillRepairsAMemoryThatWasSupersededAfterwards` does NOT run, and the reason is structural rather than an omission: `Delete` REMOVES the `memories` row, so a `delete` version that is a memory's newest version belongs to a memory the stamp repair cannot reach (it joins `memories`), and the one live shape in which a newest version is a tombstone is now outside the repair by name — rule (6) above is its test.

In SQL the anchor is `NOT removable-like AND phase IN stampMovingPhases`, and the parentheses around the first are load-bearing: `historyRemovableLikeSQL` opens with an `EXISTS`, so `NOT EXISTS (…) AND a.phase IN (…)` and `NOT EXISTS (…) a.phase IN (…)` are not the same query, and only one of them is the one anyone is reading. `TestCompactHistoryFixUpdatedAtRestoresTheLastStampWrite` catches it because every memory in it has both kinds of row. The two predicates are one function apart — `historyRemovableLikeSQL` and `historyRemovableRowSQL` — and the two retention guards (newest version, tombstoned memory) live in the second while the anchor takes the first, which is what lets a newest `reflect` version be the damage and a `resolve` version be an evidence-free row above the anchor. The pass therefore runs BEFORE the delete that removes its evidence, and both modes run in that same order, which is what keeps a dry run and the apply it previews in step. Then, and only then, the direction: **backward** only, since the damage moved a stamp forward, and a stamp already at or before the target is left alone. Both stamps are read through `ParseStamp` and the restored one is written in `StoredStampLayout`, because the two columns are written by different statements. `restorableStamp` returns THREE outcomes and they are three facts: restorable, unreadable (a stamp either layout cannot read — left alone, counted as `StampsUnreadable`), and **UNRECORDED** (no kept version of the memory was written by a stamp-moving writer at all, so `target` is the `COALESCE`ed zero the candidate statement substitutes). The last is checked BEFORE any parse, because an absent anchor and an unreadable value are different faults and merging them would send an operator looking for a timestamp shape that is not the problem. Its stamp is left exactly as it is and counted as `StampsUnrecorded`; the versions above it are still removed, since the delete never depended on there being a stamp to move. The `LEFT JOIN` on the anchor and the `COALESCE` around `recorded_at` exist so such a memory REACHES the caller rather than being dropped by an inner join: a row silently missing from a repair is a memory whose stamp reads as a reflect run's time with nothing to say so. The consequence the resolve writers force is now stated rather than left implicit: because `SetResolved` and `ClearResolved` do **not** bump `updated_at`, a memory whose last recorded change was a resolve has the resolve SKIPPED as the anchor — the stamp goes back to the last write that really moved it, and on a memory with nothing else that is the save. That is the difference between "the last change to the memory" and "the last change to the stamp", and only the second is what `updated_at` ever held.
- History growth is ONE read with two renderings, and it reuses the compaction's own comparison rather than a second spelling of it. `Store.HistoryGrowth` (`internal/memory/history_growth.go`, #729) is the only implementation; `ghost mcp status` and the `ghost_health` MCP tool both print ITS result fields and ITS `Detail` strings, so a terminal and an agent reading one store cannot be told different things about it — a second threshold or a second sentence in either surface is a second answer to the same question. The numerator is `historyEqualPredecessorSQL`, the function `CompactHistory`'s delete, dry run and `updated_at` pass all use, because a second predicate would let the report disagree with the command that fixes what it reports. The report's share deliberately OVER-counts against that repair: it counts every restatement while `CompactHistory` removes a SUBSET of them, and the subset is LARGE — FIVE guards keep a restatement the share counts: it is the memory's newest version, it names another memory (a thread, not a state), its phase is anything but `reflect`, it was recorded at or after the repair's `--before` bound, or its memory has since been DELETED. So the share is how much noise the table carries, not how much is disposable, and `TestHistoryGrowthNoOpShareAgreesWithWhatCompactWouldRemove` reconciles the difference row by row — STATING the bound through `HistoryCompactOptions.Before` rather than inheriting the default, because on any clock past #727 the default excludes every row a 24-hour-window fixture writes, so the run would remove nothing and the test would pass while saying nothing about how the two commands relate. THE ADVICE MATCHES THAT SUBSET and is pinned in BOTH directions: the finding says the retention caps are what evict a restatement, because the bound means every row inside a 24-hour window is one the repair keeps by design — the measurement stays true against a moved bound while only the advice goes stale, which is why the sentence asserts both what it names and what it does NOT promise. THE TOMBSTONE GUARD MAKES THE PRESSURE WORSE rather than the advice weaker, and #709 is what made it routine: retention tiers give `expires_at` a day to arrive, so a store now deletes memories nobody asked it to, and each leaves a frozen history no repair will ever reach — those rows still count against the store cap, and its oldest-first trim is the only thing that reclaims them. The store-cap finding is therefore the actionable one on such a store, and the agreement test STAGES the retired memory so the fifth guard is OBSERVED rather than listed: its restatement is otherwise in reach, counted through `historyRemovableLikeSQL`, because a guard that cannot fail proves nothing. The scope is the WHOLE store, because `pruneHistoryTx` ranks over the whole table: both caps are store-level, and a per-project number would be a different question asked of a policy that does not answer per project. The counts are kept in TWO clocks and never blended — the share, the rate and both projections are about the 24-hour window, `MaxVersions` and `TotalRows` are about the table as it stands — and the window is spelled ONCE (`historyWindowModifier`) across both statements, because a share whose denominator is not the set its numerator was counted over is a report that looks right and is not. The per-memory projection measures each memory against its OWN 24-hour rate and takes the soonest, and a memory that wrote nothing in the window is left out entirely rather than counted as infinitely far from the cap: a report about what is moving, not about what is parked. THE DEEPEST MEMORY AND THE MEMORY THE COUNTDOWN IS ABOUT ARE NOT THE SAME MEMORY, and the result keeps them apart rather than blending them: `MaxVersions` is an AGGREGATE for the summary line, while `CapMemoryID`/`CapMemoryVersions`/`CapMemoryRecent` are ONE memory's identity and its OWN two figures, carried precisely so the finding can name it and quote numbers its countdown closes against — a sentence that spliced the store's widest history onto the fastest writer's rate would name no memory at all. The summary line therefore labels the aggregate "deepest memory" rather than "busiest memory", so the line cannot be read as the subject of the finding printed under it, and the id is printed WHOLE because `ghost history <id>` takes it. A memory AT its cap is a SEPARATE SENTENCE from a countdown, not a countdown of zero days, because `pruneHistoryTx` holds a memory at exactly the cap while it is still being written — that is the ordinary steady state of an active store, and `historyDaysText` is only ever handed a POSITIVE number of days so it cannot splice a state into a duration frame. A store that opened cleanly and then cannot be read REPORTS THE ERROR in place of the block, rather than printing nothing, on both surfaces — a check that cannot run must not look like a check that had nothing to say. A FINDING IS A `!` LINE AND NEVER A FAILED CHECK: the `mcp status` verdict is about WIRING, so a history finding changes neither the exit code nor the `ghost mcp init` footer — the repair it names is `ghost history compact`, and a footer pointing at init would name a repair for the wrong thing. The thresholds live beside the result rather than beside a surface, and each is `>` at a documented value: a restatement share above `HistoryNoOpShareWarn` (20%, and exactly 20% is silent, because a threshold that fires on the ordinary shape of a working store teaches an operator to ignore the line) and a cap inside `HistoryCapHorizonDays` (14, long enough to compact at leisure and short enough that the warning is not the first notice of a table that has been full for a month). There is NO `recorded_at` INDEX, deliberately and on a measurement: the window filter cannot use one because `recorded_at` is the second column of `idx_history_memory`, and the pass is bounded because `pruneHistoryTx` holds the table at the store cap on the write path of every save; a standalone index would cost a fifth of the cost of writing the history row at all, permanently, to save a couple of milliseconds on a status line (1.7 ms typical, 183 ms worst case at 20 000 rows), and `migrate_test.go` asserts that absence as an ABSENCE. The cost is bounded by pinning the other side instead: `TestHistoryGrowthQueryPlanIsOnePass` holds the plan to one scan and two index seeks with no temporary B-tree, and a linear-in-table-size canary holds the wall clock. The read takes the store's READ lock across its two statements (a consistent snapshot), and the lock is taken in `HistoryGrowth` rather than inside the per-memory helper so that the FIRST statement is inside it — a lifecycle pass or a compaction from this process appending or deleting between the two statements would leave `TotalRows` and `RowsInWindow` describing different instants, and `DaysToStoreCap` is one of those numbers divided by the other. A read leaves no trace, so a statement that has not run is indistinguishable from one that has: `TestHistoryGrowthIsExcludedByAWriter` reaches between the two statements through the `historyGrowthAfterWindow` seam, which is nil in production. It opens NO transaction, for the reason `compactHistoryPreview` opens none: nothing is written, so there is no write lock to take and a writer must not queue behind a status line for the length of two scans
- Evidence provenance: `memory_provenance` (schema v18, #673) is the EVIDENCE table — SEVERAL rows per memory, one per observation, answering "who or what supports this memory", which the change log above cannot: a fact claude-code reported in one session, codex in another, and a human verified is three rows here and one row of mutable columns on `memories`, which can only remember the last. Three concepts stay distinct and are not interchangeable: SOURCE is how Ghost received a memory (`memories.source`: mcp, reflection, import, manual), AGENT is which agent produced or observed it (`agent`), PROVENANCE is the evidence records themselves. `memory_id` CASCADES (unlike the change log's deliberately FK-free `memory_id`) because "3 observations support this" is a claim about a memory, and once the memory is gone the claim is about nothing; a purge deletes these rows explicitly as well, because `PurgeMemoryHistory` leaves the memory in place and no cascade fires for a row that stays — and it deletes the `memory_snapshot_evidence` rows too, since a snapshot is the rollback point for a whole PROJECT, so its evidence would otherwise outlive the purge by however many reflects prune that snapshot Every value except `kind` is NULL when the host did not report it — a guessed agent or session is a provenance claim nobody made, and `observed_at` is NULL for the migration's seed because the seed can say what the columns hold now and not when the fact was first seen. Writers, each in the SAME transaction as the write, each ONE statement: `Create` and `Upsert`'s insert append `observed`; a near-duplicate FOLD appends the second report to the SURVIVING memory (the point of the issue — the folding agent used to be discarded with the incoming wording, and `FoldOnly` stores no row of its own so without this the report would leave no trace); `RecordDecision` appends `observed` for its companion memory (an ordinary memory search returns and a later save folds against), empty of provenance because the decision tool reports none; `ReplaceNonManual`'s FRESH INSERT carries its sources' records onto the new id with `carried_from` naming each source — a rewrite or a merge mints an id whose evidence the cascade would otherwise have just deleted, which would make every consolidated memory read "no recorded evidence" forever, so the emissions loop runs BEFORE the delete and a test fails if the two swap; the REUSE branch appends nothing because the row never changed identity; `ImportMemory` appends `imported` plus whatever records the artifact carried (and NOT `carried_from`, which names a memory this store has never heard of); `RestoreSnapshot` restores a re-created row's records from `memory_snapshot_evidence` (a table, because a memory has several records, pruned with the snapshots) and touches NOTHING for a row it updates in place — the snapshot holds an older copy of that row's evidence, and restoring it would drop every corroboration recorded since. `AppendVerifiedEvidenceTx` is the exported seam the validity writers (#677) own the reserved `verified` kind through, and they now CALL it. State the contract as an OUTCOME, because the mechanisms differ on purpose and a reader filtering by `kind` sees three shapes for one rule: every writer that stores a `verified_at` leaves a record carrying a verification stamp in the same transaction, and `EvidenceCounts.Verified` counts any record carrying one whatever its kind. The live callers are `insertMemory` (behind `Create`) and all three of `UpsertWithOptions`' branches plus `UpdateMemoryWithOptions`; each goes through `appendVerificationIfStatedTx`, which gates on the caller stating a verification IN THIS CALL and never on the column, because a partial update COALESCEs a `verified_at` an earlier save wrote and appending for that would record a check nobody made. Their stamp is the STORE's clock, not the caller's (a verifier must not be able to date its own check), so the column carries what was claimed and the record carries when Ghost heard; the two are different facts and both are kept. `UpdateMemoryWithOptions` appends the `verified` leg but NOT an `observed` one — an edit is a change to a fact already reported, not a new report of it, and widening #673's design from inside #575 would inflate the report count on every content edit. THREE more writers store a `verified_at` and are deliberately not callers, each for its own reason. `ImportMemory` is IN the set and not among the callers: the artifact carries an OBSERVATION, so a check happened and is attested, and it rides the import's own `imported` record instead of adding a `verified` one — same store-clock stamp, same count, no second statement. That arrival stamp is CONDITIONAL, and the condition is the round trip: it is skipped whenever ANY carried record already holds a stamp, kind-agnostically, because a record with a stamp says the check happened and stamping again would make a corpus that crosses a machine boundary N times report N+1 verifications of one check. Kind-agnostic rather than `verified`-only because the second hop's artifact carries the FIRST hop's `imported` arrival, and that is the only stamped record it may hold — a kind-specific guard re-inflates on every hop after the first. The condition is a NON-EMPTY value, since a hand-edited artifact can carry `"verified_at": ""` and every reader treats that as no claim. `CreateFromCorpus` leaves NO record carrying a stamp — but it still records, because `insertMemory` appends the ingestion's `observed` row on that route unconditionally, and "no verified record" is the whole of it; a third-party dataset's `verified_at` is a value in a column with no observation behind it, so there is no event and the only stamp a record could carry would manufacture one. It opts out through `insertOptions.recordVerification` because it shares `insertMemory` with `Create`, which is why it needed a PARAMETER and not a comment — and the column keeps the dataset's value either way, since losing the dataset's own data would be the opposite defect. `RestoreSnapshot` writes it in pure SQL from the snapshot table, and a restore is a faithful copy of a corpus whose evidence rows travel with it, so appending per restored row would double-count every check the snapshot holds. That enumeration has been wrong three times — `CreateFromCorpus`, then `ImportMemory`, then by over-claiming what its own enforcement could see — every time because prose is not checkable, so it is ALSO two tests that answer different questions. `TestEveryVerifiedAtWriterIsEnumerated` asserts the OUTCOME each writer leaves: whether a record carries a stamp, and of what kind. `TestEveryVerifiedAtMentionIsClassified` walks `internal/memory` with `go/parser` and requires every function naming `verified_at` IN ITS OWN BODY to be in one of FOUR classifications (writes a memory row, touches a snapshot or evidence side, is a migration or reader, or genuinely SPANS two of those surfaces — `ReplaceNonManual` does, since its fresh-insert path now writes the memory row's validity and provenance as well as the snapshot tables), so a new one cannot be added without being placed; it found four mentions a hand-written list had missed on its first run, all scanners, and caught two of this PR's own new functions when they landed. The limit of that one is part of the claim: it does not resolve the column out of a package-level literal, so `migrateV10` — the migration that ADDS the column, naming it only through `phase1aProvenanceColumns` — is invisible to it, and `TestColumnListWritersAreClassified` closes that one indirection by requiring every function iterating that list to be classified. `AppendVerifiedEvidenceTx` is placed explicitly too, since the writers reach the column THROUGH it and its own body names no column. A function reaching the column by some other indirection is still invisible, and that is the honest edge of the guarantee. There is no growth prune, deliberately: it would be a second statement inside the write transaction, which is what took `TestMultiProcessSharedDatabase` to SQLITE_BUSY for the change log's prune, and the cascade keeps deleted memories from contributing. `migrateV18` REFUSES a database whose `memory_provenance` is not the EVIDENCE table (a pre-rename #664 dev build used the reserved name for the change log) rather than converting or dropping it, naming `sqlite3 <db> 'DROP TABLE memory_provenance'`, and `OpenDB` runs the same check BEFORE the pre-migration backup so a refusal costs no copy and its same-second retry reports the real error — the check tests for `id`/`memory_id`/`kind` ONLY, so it is an identity test and not a version test: a future column here must not make it refuse a legitimate store with a remedy that DROPs its evidence — initSQL's CREATE INDEX cannot catch that one, because the change log has a `memory_id` too, and a warning nobody sees leaves every writer failing on the same missing column. Otherwise it seeds one `legacy` row per memory that recorded at least one of agent/session_id/source_ref/confidence, and NONE for a memory that recorded nothing — an evidence row with nothing in it still says "this was observed", and a row per memory would make every store report one observation, the same fabrication as inventing a session id. Read it with `Store.MemoryProvenance` (oldest first, rowid) and `Store.MemoryEvidenceCounts`, whose `Label()` renders "supported by 2 observations, 1 verified"; the assembler records the counts in stage 4's trace (`Signals.Evidence`, carried on `memory.Candidate` because that is the only read the assembler has) and NOTHING RANKS ON THEM — the stage's multiplier stays 1.0 until a measured change justifies one. The portable artifact (v2) carries the records, nested under the memory they support because a bare evidence line cannot be attributed; the Obsidian mirror does not — it is a human-readable mirror, not a transfer format
- Memory links: `memory_links` edge table auto-populated by cosine similarity (internal/linking worker); links cascade-delete with memories and self-heal after reflection. A graph-expansion ranking bonus was evaluated and removed — dominated by a deeper vector-k (links and the vector leg are both cosine); the link graph is retained for Obsidian export and supersedes ranking (see `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-20-graph-expansion-stays-off-design.md`). No edge may join a scope-conflicting pair: every writer refuses one (`Upsert`'s dedup probes, the linker's `related` edge, `ghost supersede`'s candidates) and every reader ignores one already stored — the two ranking guards (`DemotionPenalties`, `SupersedePenalties`), `Store.SupersedesWithin`, `ghost resolve`'s supersedes piggyback and the repair pass's matching floor (`scopeCompatibleSupersedes`, which needed `GetByIDs` on `resolveStore`/`reassessStore`/`resolveCapableStore` for the link endpoints' scopes), and the two fold-target liveness checks (`foldTargetStillLive` + the cross-category probe). Scope exempts a pair from ranking and folding; it never deletes a `memory_links` row. Where the exemption is decided: inside the candidate query, always, because the same `LIMIT` picks the candidates — a scope check applied after the cut spends the budget on rows the caller may not use and misses a compatible one ranked just below (#665). So the two cosine writers narrow before their window (`SearchVectorScoped`, in Go over the scan, so its top-k is the limit and there is no statement to fold the rule into) so their neighbour budgets count only linkable rows, and `Upsert`'s two dedup probes carry the rule inside their own `LIMIT 15` for exactly that reason. `foldTargetStillLive` is the one statement that carries it without a window to protect (it names one row by id) and for a different reason: a scope-conflicting `supersedes` edge is not a verdict that row may be acted on. Both are one rule in two forms, `scopesConflictSQL` in `internal/memory/scope.go`, held to `ScopesConflict` by `TestScopesConflictSQLAgreesWithScopesConflict`
- Resolution classifier: `ghost resolve` runs a keyword prefilter, then a deterministic KEEP veto, then batches up to 8 notes per KEEP-biased CLI-harness classify call (`claude`/`opencode`/`codex`/`goose` subprocess, numbered `N: RESOLVED | closed-by: <fact>|KEEP` reply judged by a strict first-field line parser) to mark `resolved_at`, dropping resolved-evidence memories (changelogs, cost estimates, closed experiment notes) from ranked injection while keeping them searchable. KEEP-biased in code, not prose (#640: an independent judge found ~1 resolve in 3 on a real DB had buried durable knowledge): `resolve.VetoKeep` settles a candidate KEEP with no harness call when its text carries an imperative (`never|do not|don't|must|always|required`) or an open marker (`not yet|outstanding|still pending|still open|still stale|unresolved|todo`), all case-insensitive and word-bounded; the rubric asks the fresh-session question (would an agent starting fresh make a mistake, repeat work, or break a rule without this?) and says dates, PR numbers, commit hashes and "fixed in" do not resolve a note; a RESOLVED must carry a non-empty `closed-by:` or the parser reads it as KEEP. The veto guards the classifier only — the supersedes-edge piggyback and correction pairing still demote. Only explicit KEEP verdicts are cached in `memories.resolve_kept_hash`, under `resolve.KeepStamp` (schema v7; the v3 hash prefix invalidates the v1 implicit-KEEP and v2 explicit-KEEP caches, so #640's re-judged rules are re-asked) — the bare content hash when the verdict saw no negative audit evidence, that hash combined with the evidence when it did (#880), so un-doubted content keeps the byte-for-byte pre-#880 value and a contradiction recorded after a KEEP re-asks it exactly once — once meaning one re-ask per verdict that COMES BACK, because a stamp is written only for an explicit KEEP; the evidence read sits above the gate and is bounded to a pass with something at the gate, so the one new payer is a converged pass, which pays one bounded read and still no call. UNKNOWN parse failures remain uncached and are therefore retried — asked and billed again on EVERY later pass until the parser reads them — so a converged project makes no calls only after explicit KEEPs. Dry-run writes nothing, `--apply` writes resolved_at and explicit KEEP cache entries; transport or verdict-count failures fail fatally rather than applying incomplete results, while UNKNOWN lines remain eligible for a later pass. `ghost resolve <project> --reassess` is the repair path for the resolutions the pass already got wrong: it re-runs the vetoes and the classifier (no keyword prefilter) over memories that are ALREADY resolved, prints the ones that now come back KEEP, and with `--apply` clears their `resolved_at` via `memory.ClearResolved` and caches the classifier's KEEP verdicts under the same `KeepStamp` over the same audit evidence the ordinary pass reads — it reads that evidence once for its pool, appends it to the ask and stamps what came back with it, so both passes write and read one key and a repaired note is not re-asked one lifecycle later (#880); SCOPE THAT REPAIR with `--only <id>[,<id>...]` (full ids or 8+ character hex prefixes) or `--only-file <path>` (one per line, `#` comments allowed; both may be given, the list is the union) — both are refused without `--reassess`, because the ordinary pass has no resolved pool to narrow, and a scoped report says how much of the project it read ("3 of 143 already resolved judged") instead of describing a pool it never looked at. Scoping narrows WHICH rows are judged and nothing else: every floor below still holds a judged row back. A selector that names nothing (mistyped, another project, or already cleared by an earlier repair) is reported and skipped, never an error for the run; an ambiguous prefix IS an error, because nothing can be said about which rows were meant. #698 measured why: an unscoped repair over a real store proposed un-hiding 143 memories and an independent judge sampling 40 found ~35% stale (completed changelogs, PR/host snapshots, notes a newer memory had superseded, a retired code path). `ghost supersede --reassess --apply` therefore prints its own follow-up — the exact `ghost resolve <project> --reassess --only <ids> --apply` over the withdrawn edges' targets — and writes the same id list under the data dir's scratch (`scratch.Root()`, `supersede-reassess-<project>-<ts>.ids`) for `--only-file`; a dry run prints neither, because it withdrew nothing and resolve would refuse the command while the edges are live. The repair path is also the only place `VetoKeep` does not settle KEEP by itself: when the same project holds a NEWER unresolved memory sharing >=2 of the note's key identifiers — a backticked span, an `#NNN` reference, a file name, a host name (`resolve.keyIdentifiers`, `subjectIndex.shadowing`; NOT a bare number or a version, which are what changes when a note goes stale) — the note goes to the classifier instead, because a veto protects a phrase and not a claim. The pool is `ResolveCandidates` for the correction floor's reason: a memory that is itself resolved is a row the next ordinary pass will not see. `Run`'s veto is unchanged, and the asymmetry is why: there a false veto keeps a note OUT of the resolution (one noisy memory, a later pass can still bury it), here it un-hides one permanently; a failed clear is fatal, a failed cache write only warns; `memory.ClearResolved` runs every batch in one transaction so "a failed clear repaired nothing" is literally true. It honours Run's two free demotions as a floor — a row a live `supersedes` edge still asserts, or an older row a NEWER *unresolved* correction pairs with (the same pool Run searches, via `correctionPairTargetsFrom`), is counted as `Demoted` and left resolved, because Run fires both before the veto and the KEEP cache, so clearing it would report a repair the next pass undoes. The pairing is re-checked after the re-KEEP set is known (`holdBack`), because a correction that is itself repaired leaves the resolved pool and would re-assert its pairing; both the floor and the hold-back pair only Run's keyword-prefiltered subset, since a keyword-free row is never a pairing target and holding it would be a permanent, mislabelled hold no later pass could undo. THE RE-CHECK READS THE POOL THE NEXT ORDINARY PASS WILL READ — the unresolved pool PLUS the rows this run clears — and uses it for BOTH the frequency count and the correction search, the two jobs `Run` gives its own `ResolveCandidates` pool (#712). Counted over the live pool alone, a row this run clears is missing from the count, so its subject tokens look rarer than the next pass will find them and a pairing the next pass will NOT make holds a row back for a second, identical pass to release. So the re-check ITERATES, bounded at `maxHoldBackRounds` (8): holding a row takes it back out of that pool, which lowers the frequency of the tokens it carries, which can turn a token rare that was not and reveal a pairing the previous round was blind to. WHAT TERMINATES THE LOOP IS NOT THE PAIRINGS, and the difference matters: a held-back row leaves the pool the re-check reads, and that pool is BOTH the frequency pool and the correction pool, so dropping a row does two OPPOSITE things — it lowers the document frequency of the tokens the row carries, which can reveal a pairing the round before was blind to, AND, when the row is correction-marked, it removes every pairing that row was asserting. A later round can therefore report FEWER pairings than the round before it (`TestHoldBackPairingsAreNotMonotoneAcrossRounds` is the case that does), and the ONLY termination invariant is that every round which finds anything removes at least one row from the repair set, which bounds the loop by `len(repair)` however the pairings move. So `maxHoldBackRounds` caps the WORK ONE RUN DOES, not safety: with it and without it the re-check ends, and what changes is whether one run reaches its fixed point or leaves work for the next. Dropping is ONE-WAY — a row held is never re-added, and a held-back correction therefore cannot free its row again — precisely because the pairings can retreat, and without that rule the answer would depend on how they moved between rounds and the LAST round would win rather than the union. A bound reached WITH a row still changing is a finding and not a truncation: `ReassessResult.Rounds`/`BoundHit` carry it and `reassessRoundsLine` prints its own sentence (“a further pass may clear more”), because a run that iterated is reporting an answer no single re-check would have given. Printed ONLY when there is something to say — more than one round, or the bound reached — since a line an operator learns to skip is a line that stops being read when it matters. No round re-asks the harness: the iteration is over the hold-back decision alone, on verdicts the run already holds, because a re-ask is billed work for an answer in hand. THE FLOOR DELIBERATELY STAYS THE CONSERVATIVE ONE, drawing its corrections only from the live pool, so it may over-hold a row the re-check releases — the right way round, because an over-held row is left resolved and REPORTED with a reason an operator can check, while an under-held one is cleared and immediately re-stamped by the pass that follows. EVERY HELD ROW IS NAMED, not counted: `ReassessResult.Held` (one entry per row, in pool order) is printed by `reassessHeldLines` as `held by supersedes <source-id>` or `held by correction <id>`, because the two reasons need different remedies — a live edge is undone by `ghost supersede --withdraw`, a correction pairing is undone by nothing and can only be READ — and `Demoted` is `len(Held)`, so the summary count and the list under it cannot disagree. EVERY HOLDER IS NAMED, never just one: a note two newer notes both supersede is held by both and withdrawing one of the two releases nothing, so naming one would send the operator to undo an edge whose undo changes nothing; `HeldMemory.Reason` sorts on a copy (supersedes first, then corrections, each by id) so two runs are comparable. A pairing therefore carries its correction now — `correctionPairingsFrom` returns one entry per (correction, target) pair and `correctionPairTargetsFrom` deduplicates the targets on top of it — so there is still ONE implementation of the rare-token rule and `Run`'s demoted-row order is byte-for-byte what it was. Those ids print WHOLE, unlike every other id on a Ghost report, because here they are OPERANDS rather than references: the source is what `--withdraw` takes, the held row's own id is what a scoped repair names, the correction's id is a note somebody has to go and read, and an eight-character abbreviation of any of them turns a repair already in hand into a second lookup. `Held` and `Rounds` are properties of the DECISION and not of what was written, so a dry run reports the same fixed point `--apply` reaches and writes nothing: a preview reporting a smaller repair than the run would reach is a preview an operator cannot plan from. A reasonless `closed-by` is not a reason: a bare mention of the key without its colon ("no closed-by available") and a placeholder value (`none`, `n/a`, `tbd`, …) are both KEEP — and so is a value whose FIRST WORD declines to name one ("not applicable", "no decision", "cannot name it", "unclear"), because a refusal is a phrase far more often than it is one of the nine placeholder keywords. That grammar is `resolve.ReasonedField`/`resolve.ReasonlessValue` and it is NOT resolve's alone: internal/supersede's `replaced:` field is the same contract for a supersedes verdict (#686), so one list decides both and a value that keeps a note here cannot bury it there. `VetoKeep` is likewise split — `VetoKeepImperative` is the imperative half, exported because internal/supersede's `VetoSupersede` reads the same words for the opposite decision (a rule is not a fact that goes out of date), while the open markers stay resolve's. The repair pass treats a live 'supersedes' edge as a floor, which is why `ghost supersede <project> --reassess --consensus 3 --apply` has to run FIRST: withdrawing the edge is what makes the `resolved_at` it justified clearable, `TestReassessResolvesAChainIntoResolveReassess` runs that chain against a real store, and the e2e suite runs it against the built binary end to end (`TestCLISupersedeReassessFeedsResolveReassess` — write the edge, resolve stamps the target, withdraw it, then the printed `--only-file` clears that one memory and leaves another resolved memory in the same project resolved). That floor is counted per EDGE, not per note: a note two newer notes both supersede is held by either one, so withdrawing one of the two does NOT release it (`Demoted`, nothing cleared, no harness call) and only the last withdrawal does. A withdrawal wider than the edge it names would otherwise hand the note back to injection, where the very next ordinary pass re-stamps it from the surviving edge and no edge ever changes again (`TestReassessHoldsTheNoteWhileAnySupersedesEdgeSurvives`). `resolve.Mark` (`mark.go`) is the OTHER repair, and the only one that goes the other way from a caller's judgement: `ghost resolve <project> --mark <ids>|--mark-file <path> [--apply]` stamps `resolved_at` on the memories a caller NAMES, dry-run by default. It exists because NO pass reaches the case it serves — the ordinary pass only proposes a memory carrying a resolution keyword, and even where it does, a KEEP-biased classifier asked about THAT NOTE is not asked whether another memory in the project supersedes it, so a note a newer note says was fixed is routinely invisible to every pass (`--reassess` cannot help either: it judges what is ALREADY resolved, and this row is not). Like `supersede.Withdraw` it judges NOTHING, so no harness is involved and nothing is billed, and a machine that cannot spawn one can still repair its corpus. The refs are internal/memref's, not a second copy, so a ref means here exactly what it means to `--withdraw`. A request is settled before anything is written and a refusal writes NOTHING, not even the refs that were fine (`TestMarkRefusesAnAmbiguousPrefix` marks neither of two refs when one is ambiguous), and the per-row outcomes are three SEPARATE states rather than one "not marked" — `AlreadyResolved` (the issue's no-op: a memory the previous pass already buried, on which nothing is written including NO history row, because a history row records a write), `Pinned` and `ExemptCategory` (the store's guard, whose refusals have different remedies) — each counted on `MarkResult` off the same rows the report prints, so a count and a list cannot disagree. Only rows IN THE PROJECT may be marked, and that is checked on the READ that can see `project_id`, before any write, because `MemoryIDsByIDPrefix` reaches `_global` for the supersede endpoints' sake and a row every project shares is not one a project asked to bury; the store binds the project AGAIN in the same statement as the stamp, so a caller reaching the store directly cannot cross it either, AND refuses the `_global` SENTINEL beside the empty-project refusal — the binding does not catch it (a promoted row's own project_id IS `_global`, so naming `_global` satisfies every predicate MarkResolved has), and it is refused at the STORE layer rather than re-derived per caller, like every other `_global` refusal here. The report's per-row marker defaults to the NEGATIVE claim under `--apply`, never the success, because the eligibility guard is re-checked WHERE THE WRITE HAPPENS and a row pinned or recategorized between the read and the write is declined SILENTLY (another process's decision, not a failure) — a state the result says nothing about, so a default of `marked` would report a change that did not happen. `MarkedMemory.Declined` names it, `MarkResult.Declined` counts it, and both the CLI report and the MCP result fall through to `not marked` for any state they do not recognise, so a state added tomorrow reads as unwritten rather than written.  `Store.MarkResolved` is a separate method rather than a `SetResolved` wrapper for two reasons: it BINDS the project (the pass's ids are already project-scoped by the selection it made), and it RECORDS the performer, so the `resolve` history row it writes is the only resolve row that can say a PERSON decided rather than a classifier judged — which is the whole reason this is a command and not SQL, since the SQL route bypasses `memory_history` entirely and leaves the record showing a memory resolved with nothing saying who said so.
